## 1. Reading the thinkers and learning the mathematics

What does each thinker claim about the self, the world and liberation, and what mathematical structure would preserve the argument? This article offers groundwork for answering that question: selected primary readings, worked formalizations and the category theory needed to understand them.

The order matters. First establish a thinker’s position and reasons. Then construct a model, derive its consequences and check whether we can recover the argument—including its qualifications—from that model. Comparisons within a tradition must preserve what later thinkers explain, revise or reject. Interschool integration becomes a further question once the relevant accounts are understood.

The reader therefore moves between two kinds of work. In the textual chapters, original words appear in romanization with literal English, a named author or speaker, a locus and a source link. Commentary is distinguished from the root text it interprets. Where a textual variant changes the interpretation, the difference remains visible. In the mathematical lessons, examples begin with a question, develop a construction and test it against a case that could make it fail.

The chapters follow a sequence of questions: what a claim denies, what a subject is, how words refer, what persists through change, and how power, practice and manifestation belong to an account of reality. Mathematical lessons enter when those arguments give them a task. The later lessons examine compatibility, fullness and contextual truth, distinguishing worked results from questions for further research.

The mathematics is taught from the beginning. Eugenia Cheng’s *The Joy of Abstraction*, especially Chapters 8 and 14–23, informs the progression from examples to definitions and from constructions to their universal properties. Adjunctions and toposes require further sources, identified when used. Read the worked examples slowly enough to check their arrows and try the exercises before their answers. [Cheng](cite://eugenia-cheng/joy-of-abstraction/category-cheng).

The source words establish the textual premises; a reconstruction explains which formal statements represent them; a proof establishes consequences of those statements. A faithful interpretation of a selected argument is a significant result. It does not by itself establish an equivalence between complete traditions. The closing assessment identifies what the selected constructions explain and what further reconstruction would be needed before testing an integration.

**Reading order**

1. Reading the thinkers and learning the mathematics
2. Qualified absence: learning to construct and test a model
3. Selfhood, instruction and appearance in Advaita
4. Reference without erasing difference
5. Change, discrimination and the continuing subject
6. Real transformation, manifestation and service
7. Knowledge, ignorance, devotion and fulfilled life in Advaita
8. Dependence, divine own-forms and first-person identity
9. Rival accounts of cognition, self and release
10. Divine fullness, pervasion and reciprocal love
11. Reflexivity, contraction and complete apprehension in Trika
12. Śākta and modern accounts of power and devotion
13. Compatibility: what one common model establishes
14. Fullness, closure and controlled reconstruction
15. From relational profiles to contextual truth
16. What the groundwork establishes and what comes next
17. Sources, further constructions and mathematical reading

### What a teaching is trying to change

A tradition teaches someone, under particular conditions, toward a transformation. Its words can describe reality, correct a habitual misidentification, prescribe a practice, or help a pupil recognize what the practice makes accessible. A comparison should identify those acts before deciding that two formulations contradict each other. A faithful reconstruction preserves the audience, practical purpose and error being addressed.

Aurobindo gives this approach a striking precedent. In an early dialogue on the Īśā Upaniṣad, the pupil asks how Śaṅkara could be described as undeveloped. The teacher answers: “He was not an undeveloped mind, but he was dealing with undeveloped minds and had to speak their language.” The surrounding argument interprets Śaṅkara’s emphasis on knowledge as a response to reliance on ritual works for liberation. Aurobindo distinguishes that corrective purpose from an absolute prohibition on action after knowledge. [*The Ishavasyopanishad*, CWSA 17, pp. 113–116](https://www.motherandsriaurobindo.in/Sri-Aurobindo/books/isha-upanishad/).

In the related *Karmayogin* commentary, he explicitly accords Śaṅkara, Rāmānuja and Madhva’s conceptions their own truth and usefulness. He relates the Upaniṣad’s different formulations to Brahman in itself, in the universe and in relation to the individual. This supplies a substantive proposal for relating perspectives: the relation under consideration changes what an adequate account must express. Both passages belong to unfinished early commentaries, dated approximately 1905 and 1905–1906 by the editors; they are distinct from the interpretation he published in 1914–1915. [CWSA 17, pp. 201–203 and editorial note pp. 594–595](https://www.motherandsriaurobindo.in/Sri-Aurobindo/books/isha-upanishad/).

The reader can now ask a more exact question about a polemic. Is the author rejecting another account’s asserted conclusion, or a use of that conclusion which frustrates its purpose? “Action does not itself produce liberation” and “a liberated person can act” need not conflict: they concern different dependencies. An injunction to withdraw attention from changing objects can serve recognition of the subject. Its intended force must be established before translating it as a universal claim that manifestation has no reality or value.

Śaṅkara’s own Gītā commentary makes the distinction concrete. At 3.20, Janaka’s action receives two conditional explanations: preparation for knowledge, or a knower’s continued action for the world. Before 3.25, Śaṅkara writes, *tasyāpi ātmanaḥ kartavyābhāve ’pi parānugraha eva kartavyaḥ*: “Even for him, although there is nothing to be done for himself, benefit to others alone is to be done.” At 3.26, the knower should not unsettle the action-attached learner. At 3.30, the specified ignorant seeker is instructed to act with *ahaṃ kartā īśvarāya bhṛtyavat karomi*: “I am an agent; I act for the Lord like a servant.” A reconstruction must explain what this practical orientation corrects, what it accommodates, and what later understanding preserves. The first excerpt resolves the final sandhi of *kartavya ity āha*. [Śaṅkara, Gītābhāṣya 3.20–30](https://advaitasharada.sringeri.net/read/bhagavadgita-bhashya/3/).

This contextual reading also applies to Aurobindo. His criticisms of withdrawal and his call for transformed life can address different obstacles from a teaching directed toward disengagement from attachment. Their comparison must retain the conditions, reasons and intended transformation of each. It should also retain each author’s claims about what is ultimately real. A sentence explicitly asserting something of every liberated self cannot be restricted to one audience merely because the restriction makes integration easier. The source argument must support the restriction.

A useful formal record therefore distinguishes the situation of teaching, its assertoric content, the response it requests, and the transformation it is intended to enable. A teacher can correct the pupil’s belief while continuing to refer to the same reality. What changes may be the pupil’s identification or access, the role of a practice, or the meaning of an expression as clarified by the instruction. Each possibility requires its own representation.

A reconstruction must preserve who is being addressed, which error is corrected, what the practice requires, and what the teacher says remains true after realization. Recovering those features is a test of the model. Aurobindo’s contextual reading supplies a position to examine alongside the other thinkers’ own explanations.

### What harmony can mean

What does agreement between teachings concern? Agreement might concern a referent, shared deliberation, a destination, textual purport, or the fruit of practice. These are useful questions to keep separate. Two primary passages show why the distinction matters.

Ṛgveda 1.164.46 names a plurality of divine designations:

> indram mitraṃ varuṇam agnim āhur atho divyaḥ sa suparṇo garutmān |
> ekaṃ sad viprā bahudhā vadanty agniṃ yamam mātariśvānam āhuḥ ||

Literal translation: They call [it] Indra, Mitra, Varuṇa, Agni; and it is the celestial well-winged Garutmān. The existent is one; inspired poets speak [of it] in many ways; they call [it] Agni, Yama, Mātariśvan. [Ṛgveda 1.164.46, Aufrecht-based GRETIL text](https://gretil.sub.uni-goettingen.de/gretil/corpustei/transformations/html/sa_Rgveda-edAufrecht.htm).

Here *sat* names what is, the existent; *bahudhā* qualifies speaking in many ways. “Truth” is a familiar interpretive rendering, but “the existent” preserves this literal distinction. The verse names deities, not later philosophical systems. It supplies a substantial many-names/one-reference statement. Extending it to complete philosophical theories requires an additional argument: expressions referring to one reality need not make every assertion about that reality equivalent. The displayed text follows the held unaccented witness, with its dotted *ṁ* normalized to *ṃ*; it does not reconstruct Vedic accents.

Brahmasūtra 1.1.4 makes a different, compressed claim:

> tat tu samanvayāt |

Literal translation: But that, because of [their] coherent convergence. [Brahmasūtra 1.1.4 with Śaṅkara’s commentary](https://advaitasharada.sringeri.net/read/brahmasutra-bhashya/1/).

The ellipsis needs its commentator’s explanation. Śaṅkara writes in his own reply:

> sarveṣu hi vedānteṣu vākyāni tātparyeṇaitasyārthasya pratipādakatvena samanugatāni |

Literal translation: For in all the Vedāntas, the sentences converge, through their purport, in conveying this meaning.

“This meaning” refers to Brahman in the preceding explanation. Śaṅkara is arguing about what these scriptural sentences intend to teach. The immediate discussion contests an interpretation that subordinates them to action. His *samanvaya* therefore establishes a particular reading; it does not suspend interpretive disagreement or declare every school’s entire account jointly true. Its domain and operation differ from the Ṛgvedic enumeration of divine names.

A constructive comparison should consequently state what is claimed to converge. Common reference asks whether expressions concern the same reality. Common destination asks where practices lead. Shared fruit asks what is attained. Coherent purport asks how passages belong to an interpretation. Shared deliberation asks how people inquire together. None alone supplies every answer to the others; each can nevertheless provide a positive starting point for further argument.

## 2. Qualified absence: learning to construct and test a model

A proposed integration must preserve what a sentence affirms or denies. The following pot example makes the issue concrete. Annambhaṭṭa supplies a logical vocabulary of absence; Madhusūdana uses that vocabulary in an Advaita debate about falsity. We will follow the argument before constructing its model.

### What exactly is being denied?

Consider a pot on the ground. It is there through contact. It does not inhere in the ground as a quality inheres in a substance. If we abbreviate both claims carelessly, we obtain “the pot is there” and “the pot is not there.” The apparent contradiction comes from removing information that distinguishes the two claims.

The vocabulary of the Sanskrit argument is worth learning before we write symbols. A *locus* is where something is said to occur or be absent. A *counterpositive* is what an absence is the absence of: a pot is the counterpositive of absence of that pot. A *delimiter* specifies which condition or respect determines a claim. Pothood can delimit an absence of pots generally; the relevant relation can distinguish contact from inherence. These explanatory glosses introduce the technical terms; their exact role must still be established in each argument.

Annambhaṭṭa distinguishes four forms of absence.

**Tarkasaṃgraha, §80, printed 62/PDF 139** [Source](cite://annambhatta/tarka-samgraha/inspectable-nav-ts-absences)

> anādiḥ sāntaḥ prāgabhāvaḥ. utpatteḥ pūrvaṃ kāryasya. sādir anantaḥ pradhvaṃsaḥ. utpattyanantaraṃ kāryasya. traikālikasaṃsargāvacchinnapratiyogitāko ’tyantābhāvaḥ. yathā bhūtale ghaṭo nāstīti. tādātmyasambandhāvacchinnapratiyogitāko ’nyonyābhāvaḥ. yathā ghaṭaḥ paṭo na bhavatīti.

Prior absence is beginningless and has an end: [the absence] of an effect before its production. Destruction has a beginning and no end: [the absence] of an effect after its production. Absolute absence has counterpositiveness delimited by a relation and holds through all three times; for example, “There is no pot on the ground.” Mutual absence has counterpositiveness delimited by the relation of identity; for example, “A pot is not a cloth.”

**Tarkasaṃgrahadīpikā, ad§80, printed 62/PDF 139** [Source](cite://annambhatta/tarka-samgraha-dipika/inspectable-nav-td-exclusions)

> atyantābhāvaṃ lakṣayati—traikāliketi. anyonyābhāve ’tivyāptivāraṇāya saṃsargāvacchinneti. dhvaṃsaprāgabhāvayor ativyāptivāraṇāya traikāliketi.

He defines absolute absence with “of all three times.” “Delimited by a relation” is to prevent overextension to mutual absence. “Of all three times” is to prevent overextension to destruction and prior absence.

A definition is tested against cases it should exclude. If the temporal qualification is removed, prior absence and destruction become unwanted candidates. If the relational distinction is removed, mutual nonidentity becomes another candidate. These are already tests of the definition in the commentary. Our formalization should make those tests executable in thought and notation.

The commentary then distinguishes changes in relation from changes in the specification of what is absent.

**Tarkasaṃgrahadīpikā, ad§80, printed 63/PDF 140, selected consecutive examples** [Source](cite://annambhatta/tarka-samgraha-dipika/inspectable-nav-td-qualified-absence)

> saṃyogasambandhena ghaṭavati samavāyasambandhena ghaṭābhāvaḥ. tattadghaṭābhāvād ghaṭatvāvacchinnapratiyogikasāmānyābhāvaś cātiriktaḥ.

In that which has a pot through the relation of contact, there is absence of the pot through the relation of inherence. And the generic absence whose counterpositive is delimited by pothood is additional to the absences of this and that pot.

There are two independent ways to lose information. We could forget whether the relation is contact or inherence. Or we could replace the absence of one particular pot with the absence of pots altogether. Neither inference follows merely because the word “pot” occurs in both sentences.

For the two quoted examples the 1930 print has been compared with the electronic text. The print reads *pratiyogika* where the electronic witness has *pratiyogitāka*. The displayed wording follows the print. The editor records textual difficulties in earlier adjacent examples.

### The definition Madhusūdana defends

Madhusūdana uses this logical vocabulary to investigate falsity, *mithyātva*. His second definition begins:

**Advaitasiddhi, Second definition of mithyātva, opening** [Source](cite://madhusudana/advaitasiddhi/inspectable-a-as-definition)

> pratipannopādhau traikālikaniṣedhapratiyogitvaṃ vā mithyātvam |

Or falsity is being the counterpositive of negation in all three times in the locus in which [it] is apprehended.

The sentence has several conditions. Something is apprehended in a locus; it is the counterpositive of a negation there; that negation concerns all three times. Finding an ordinary instance of absence somewhere is plainly too weak. The objector develops this problem through increasingly qualified attempts at the definition.

**Advaitasiddhi, Second definition of mithyātva; printed 69/PDF 89** [Source](cite://madhusudana/advaitasiddhi/inspectable-nav-as-objections)

> nanv evaṃ sati yāvatsadadhikaraṇātyantābhāvapratiyogitvaṃ paryavasitam. tathā ca kevalānvayyatyantābhāvapratiyogiṣu avṛttiṣu gaganādiṣu tārkikāṇāṃ siddhasādhanam; yadadhikaraṇaṃ yat sat tanniṣṭhātyantābhāvapratiyogitvaṃ tasya mithyātvam iti vivakṣāyām, adhikaraṇapadenāvṛttinirākaraṇe ’pi saṃyogasambandhena samavāyasambandhena vā yat ghaṭādhikaraṇaṃ samavāyasambandhena saṃyogasambandhena vā ghaṭasya tanniṣṭhātyantābhāvapratiyogitayā sarveṣu vṛttimatsu duruddharaṃ siddhasādhanam. yena sambandhena yad yasyādhikaraṇaṃ tena sambandhena tanniṣṭhātyantābhāvapratiyogitvam iti vivakṣāyām avyāpyavṛttiṣu saṃyogādiṣu siddhasādhanam iti cen na.

But, if this is so, [the definition] comes to being the counterpositive of absolute absence whose loci are all existent things. Then, for the logicians, in the case of things such as space which do not occur [in a locus] and are counterpositives of an absolute absence occurring universally, it proves what is already established. If what is intended is “its falsity is being the counterpositive of absolute absence located in that existent thing which is its locus,” then, even though the word “locus” excludes things that do not occur [in a locus], proving what is already established is hard to avoid for all things that do occur [in a locus]: where something is the locus of a pot through contact or inherence, the pot is the counterpositive of absolute absence there through inherence or contact [respectively]. If what is intended is being the counterpositive of absolute absence located there through the very relation through which something is its locus, there is [still] proving what is already established in cases such as contact, which does not occur throughout its locus. If this is the objection: no.

The successive objections reveal why a simplified formulation fails. First, it could merely establish something the logicians already accept about things that occur in no locus. Second, restricting attention to things that do occur in a locus leaves a switch between contact and inherence. Third, keeping the relation fixed still allows a switch between parts of a locus: a monkey is in contact with a tree at its top but not at its root. *Siddhasādhana*, proving what is already established, is the dialectical objection here. These accepted cases would not establish the disputed falsity.

Madhusūdana’s reply specifies what must remain fixed.

**Advaitasiddhi, Second definition, reply to relation/delimiter objections** [Source](cite://madhusudana/advaitasiddhi/inspectable-a-as-frame)

> yena rūpeṇa yadadhikaraṇatayā yat pratipannaṃ tena rūpeṇa tanniṣṭhātyantābhāvapratiyogitvasya pratipannapadena sūcitattvāt |
> tacca rūpaṃ sambandhaviśeṣo'vacchedakaviśeṣaśca |
> na hi sambandhaviśeṣamantareṇa bhūtale ghaṭādhikaraṇatā pratīyate |
> avacchedakaviśeṣamantareṇa vā vṛkṣe kapisaṃyogādhikaraṇatā |
> tathā ca yena sambandhaviśeṣeṇa yena cāvacchedakaviśeṣeṇa yadadhikaraṇatāpratītiryatra bhavitumarhati, tenaiva sambandhaviśeṣeṇa tenaiva cāvacchedakaviśeṣeṇa tadadhikaraṇakātyantābhāvapratiyogitvaṃ tasya mithyātvamiti paryavasite kva siddhasādhanam |

Because the word “apprehended” indicates being the counterpositive of absolute absence in that [locus] in precisely the form in which it is apprehended as the locus of that [thing]. That form is a particular relation and a particular delimiter. For the ground’s being the locus of a pot is not apprehended without a particular relation, nor the tree’s being the locus of contact with a monkey without a particular delimiter. Thus, when the definition comes to this—its falsity is being the counterpositive of absolute absence located there through precisely that particular relation and precisely that particular delimiter through which the cognition of that locushood can occur there—where is the proving of what is already established?

The repeated “whichever … that very same” is doing inferential work. The locus, relation and delimiter cannot drift between the apprehension and the denial. The source also distinguishes the order of reality under which the claims are made.

**Advaitasiddhi, Second definition, reality-order reply** [Source](cite://madhusudana/advaitasiddhi/inspectable-a-as-orders)

> na caivaṃ sati – bhāvābhāvayoravirodhāttajjñānayorbādhyabādhakabhāvo na syāditi – vācyam ; bhinnasattākayoravirodhe'pi samasattākayorvirodhāt |
> yatra bhūtale yasya ghaṭasyātyantābhāvo vyāvahārikaḥ tatra sa ghaṭo na vyāvahārika iti niyamāt |

Nor should it be said that, since being and absence do not oppose, their cognitions would not stand as cancelled and cancelling: although [being and absence] of different orders of reality do not oppose, those of the same order oppose. For the rule is: on the ground where the absolute absence of a particular pot is transactional, that pot is not transactional there.

This passage permits a distinction of orders while retaining incompatibility within one order. It does not make every contradiction harmless. Nor does the word “absence” identify a presented-and-corrected appearance with what cannot be presented as existent at all.

**Advaitasiddhi, Second definition, reply to sheer-nonexistence objection** [Source](cite://madhusudana/advaitasiddhi/inspectable-a-as-nonexistence)

> maivam ; sarvatra traikālikaniṣedhapratiyogitvaṃ yadyapi tucchānirvācyayoḥ sādhāraṇam ; tathāpi kvacidapyupādhau sattvena pratītyanarhatvam atyantāsattvam , śūktirūpye prapañce ca bādhāt pūrvaṃ nāstyeveti na tucchatvāpattiḥ |

Not so: although being the counterpositive of all-times negation everywhere is common to the utterly nonexistent and the indeterminable, nevertheless utter nonexistence is incapacity to be presented as existent in any locus whatsoever. This certainly does not belong to shell-silver or the manifold before cancellation; therefore utter nonexistence does not follow.

A mathematical representation therefore needs room for at least two different questions: whether something can be presented as existent, and whether the relevant claim survives the specified correction. Identifying those predicates in advance would erase the distinction the passage is defending.

### Start with the rule we can actually reconstruct

We will first represent one constraint from the argument, not the entire definition of falsity. Let \(X\) be a set of intentional targets, \(F\) a set of fully specified frames, and \(T\) a set of times. A target is something a claim concerns; including a target in \(X\) does not by itself assert an externally existing object. Each frame records a locus, relation, delimiter and appraisal order. We must justify the chosen frame when applying the model to a source claim.

In the notation below, \(X\times F\times T\) is the set of triples consisting of a target, a frame and a time. The symbols \(\forall\) and \(\exists\) mean “for every” and “there exists”; \(\land\) means “and,” and \(\Longrightarrow\) means “implies.” Introduce two relations:

\[
\begin{aligned}\operatorname{Pos}&\subseteq X\times F\times T,\\\operatorname{Neg}&\subseteq X\times F\times T.\end{aligned}
\]

Writing \(\operatorname{Pos}(x,f,t)\) says that the positive claim holds for target \(x\) in frame \(f\) at time \(t\). \(\operatorname{Neg}(x,f,t)\) records the corresponding negative claim. The selected incompatibility axiom is:

\[
\forall x\,\forall f\,\forall t\;\bigl(\operatorname{Pos}(x,f,t)\land\operatorname{Neg}(x,f,t)\Longrightarrow\text{false}\bigr).
\]

\(\operatorname{Neg}\) is a separate relation symbol, not the logical complement of \(\operatorname{Pos}\). The axiom prohibits their overlap; it does not say that every triple belongs to one of them. Both may fail in a model. To require an exhaustive decision, we would need another axiom, \(\operatorname{Pos}(x,f,t) \lor  \operatorname{Neg}(x,f,t)\), which we have not adopted.

The word “selected” matters: we are extracting one consequence of the reply and same-order restriction. This axiom alone says nothing about which targets are presented, which absences hold for all times, or what cognition removes ignorance.

Now build a model. Take one target \(p\), one time \(\text{now}\), and two frames \(c\) and \(i\). Both concern the same ground and the same specified region, but \(c\) uses contact while \(i\) uses inherence. Assign exactly these positive and negative facts:

\[
\begin{aligned}\operatorname{Pos}&=\{(p,c,\text{now})\},\\\operatorname{Neg}&=\{(p,i,\text{now})\}.\end{aligned}
\]

Check the axiom. In frame \(c\) there is a positive fact but no negative fact. In frame \(i\) there is a negative fact but no positive fact. No triple belongs to both relations. The model satisfies the constraint while retaining the pot example’s distinction.

Suppose we now propose a simpler language with no frame variable:

\[
\begin{aligned}\operatorname{BarePos}(x,t)&\Longleftrightarrow\exists f\;\operatorname{Pos}(x,f,t),\\\operatorname{BareNeg}(x,t)&\Longleftrightarrow\exists f\;\operatorname{Neg}(x,f,t).\end{aligned}
\]

Both \(\operatorname{BarePos}(p,\text{now})\) and \(\operatorname{BareNeg}(p,\text{now})\) are true. Thus the frame-free rule

\[
\operatorname{BarePos}(x,t)\land\operatorname{BareNeg}(x,t)\Longrightarrow\text{false}
\]

fails. This is a countermodel to the proposed simplification. The original theory remains consistent; the proposed translation fails to preserve its intended incompatibility. Forgetting the frame distinction causes the failure.

The scope of the existential quantifier makes the failure exact. The original constraint excludes

\[
\exists f\;\bigl(\operatorname{Pos}(x,f,t)\land\operatorname{Neg}(x,f,t)\bigr).
\]

The abbreviated predicates together assert

\[
\bigl(\exists f\;\operatorname{Pos}(x,f,t)\bigr)\land\bigl(\exists g\;\operatorname{Neg}(x,g,t)\bigr).
\]

The witnesses \(f\) and \(g\) can differ. Conflating these formulas is the mathematical version of switching respects between the two claims.

Try the same exercise while retaining the relation and deleting only the spatial delimiter. Use one frame for the tree’s top and another for its root, with contact as the relation in both. The same two-witness pattern reappears. This explains why the objector’s third step is not answered merely by preserving the name of a relation.

### Learning to ask a categorical question

The countermodel used sets and logical conditions. To compare whole models systematically, we now need categories: specified objects, arrows and rules for composing them. Begin with a small example in which every arrow can be checked.

The examples follow Cheng’s progression from concrete relationships to a definition and its consequences. [Source](cite://eugenia-cheng/joy-of-abstraction/category-cheng)

There will be three distinct mathematical settings: a tiny research workflow, sets of records and functions between them, and an ordering of selected commitments. Keeping these settings distinct is part of the lesson. An arrow can mean a procedure in one category and an implication in another. It cannot quietly change its meaning halfway through an argument.

### Objects and arrows: what is being related?

Imagine that we track three kinds of research dossier:

- \(R\): a dossier containing a passage and its source reference.
- \(G\): a dossier that also contains a grammatical analysis.
- \(I\): a dossier that also contains an interpretive argument.

For this deliberately simplified workflow, allow one named step \(g\) from \(R\) to \(G\) and one named step \(i\) from \(G\) to \(I\). These are formal steps in our example, not claims that grammatical or philosophical interpretation has a unique correct procedure.

\[
R\xrightarrow{g}G\xrightarrow{i}I
\]

Reading an arrow starts with its endpoints. The notation \(g: R \to  G\) says that \(R\) is its source and \(G\) its target. We can follow \(g\) by \(i\) because the first target matches the second source. We call the combined arrow \(i \circ  g: R \to  I\). Read the expression from right to left: first \(g\), then \(i\).

We cannot follow \(i\) by \(g\) in this diagram. The output type of \(i\) is \(I\), whereas \(g\) expects \(R\). Merely writing \(g \circ  i\) does not make it meaningful. This is the simplest form of type-checking, and it is already philosophically useful. A claim about a cognition, a claim about its subject, and a claim about the referent of a sentence need not have interchangeable types.

Every object must also have an identity arrow. The identity \(1_{R}: R \to  R\) leaves the formal dossier exactly as it is. This mathematical identity differs from a researcher’s act of self-recognition.

We have now made a category with three objects and six arrows:

\[
1_{R}, 1_{G}, 1_{I}, g, i, i \circ  g
\]

The identities and the composite are present even when a diagram does not display them. Composition with an identity changes nothing. If we extend the workflow by \(j: I \to  J\), grouping three successive steps in either way must give the same arrow:

\[
j \circ  (i \circ  g) = (j \circ  i) \circ  g.
\]

This is associativity. It does not say we may reorder the steps. Parentheses can move; the sequence cannot.

A **category** consists of objects, arrows with specified sources and targets, identity arrows, and a composition operation for every composable pair. Composition must be associative and satisfy the identity laws \(f \circ  1_{A} = f = 1_{B} \circ  f\) for \(f: A \to  B\). The collection of arrows \(A \to  B\) is written \(C(A,B)\), or \(\operatorname{Hom}_{C}(A,B)\). It can contain no arrows, one arrow, or many.

A category is **small** when its objects and arrows form sets. Our finite examples are small. A category is **locally small** when each individual \(C(A,B)\) is a set, even if its entire collection of objects is too large to be a set. The category of all sets is locally small but not small in the usual foundations. We keep this distinction because later constructions use a small category of contexts; calling everything a set would create foundational problems rather than solve them.

Our workflow is the **free category** on the displayed directed graph: its arrows are finite paths, including the empty paths that serve as identities. We impose no extra equations between paths. Concatenating paths is associative, so the construction satisfies the laws.

**Try it.** Add a direct step \(d: R \to  I\). Must \(d\) equal \(i \circ  g\) because both start at \(R\) and finish at \(I\)?

**Answer.** No. They are two parallel arrows. We could impose \(d = i \circ  g\) if we had a reason to count those procedures as the same, but that is extra information. A triangle whose direct route equals its two-step route is a **commutative diagram**. Drawing a triangle does not prove commutativity. In a philosophical reconstruction, the equation would need an argument about what the arrows preserve and what their equality means.

The exercise has this triangle, whose direct edge need not equal its two-step route:

\[
\begin{array}{ccc}R&\xrightarrow{g}&G\\&\searrow{\scriptstyle d}&\downarrow{\scriptstyle i}\\&&I\end{array}
\]

A diagram becomes informative when it specifies compositions, equations, or their failure.

### Two more categories, and why the arrows matter

The category **Set** has sets as objects and functions as arrows. A function sends every element of its source to exactly one element of its target. Composition is ordinary function composition. Identities send each element to itself.

Let \(E = \{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}\) be three distinct report records. Let \(M = \{\text{quiet},\text{active}\}\) be a set of two editorial tags. Define \(t: E \to  M\) by

\[
t(e_{1}) = \text{quiet},  t(e_{2}) = \text{quiet},  t(e_{3}) = \text{active}.
\]

These are tags assigned to records, not a classification of consciousness itself. The function deliberately omits most of each report. This makes it useful for organizing an archive and unsuitable for recovering the complete reports.

An object of \(\mathbf{Set}\) is the whole set \(E\), not an individual element \(e_{1}\). We can represent an element categorically by a function from a singleton set \(1 = \{\ast \}\): the function \(\ast  \mapsto  e_{1}\) picks out that element. Later, maps from other objects will let us examine more elaborate families of elements.

Our third example starts with three selected commitments \(p, q\), and \(r\). Consider every subset of \(\{p,q,r\}\). Put exactly one arrow \(U \to  V\) when \(U \subseteq  V\). For example,

\[
\{p\} \to  \{p,q\} \to  \{p,q,r\}.
\]

The composite exists because inclusion is transitive; identities exist because every subset includes itself. There is at most one arrow between any two objects. Such a category is called **thin**. Here the underlying order is a partially ordered set, or **poset**: inclusion is reflexive, transitive, and antisymmetric.

This category records adding selected commitments. It does not decide whether the commitments are true, compatible, or well sourced. If we were ordering actual theories instead, logical consequence could matter: adding an axiom that was already provable would not strengthen the theory. That would require a more considered definition of the ordering.

**Try it.** In this inclusion category, two different paths lead from \(\{p\}\) to \(\{p,q,r\}\), one through \(\{p,q\}\), another through \(\{p,r\}\). Are their composites equal?

**Answer.** Yes. There is only one arrow with those endpoints. Compare the free workflow category, where distinct paths stayed distinct unless we imposed an equation. Choosing a thin category has already discarded distinctions between ways of reaching a result. That can be appropriate when we study implication; it can be inappropriate when we study different arguments or paths of practice.

### Isomorphism: sameness with a reversible comparison

Suppose the three records are copied into a second catalog using new identifiers \(a, b\), and \(c\). The function \(f\) sends \(e_{1}\) to \(a, e_{2}\) to \(b\), and \(e_{3}\) to \(c\). There is an inverse \(g\) that sends each new identifier back to its corresponding record. Both round trips recover the starting element.

An arrow \(f: A \to  B\) is an **isomorphism** if there is an arrow \(g: B \to  A\) such that

\[
g \circ  f = 1_{A},     f \circ  g = 1_{B}.
\]

We write \(A \cong  B\) when some such isomorphism exists. In \(\mathbf{Set}\), the isomorphisms are precisely the bijections: functions that pair every source element with exactly one target element and leave no target element unpaired.

Now try to reverse the tagging function \(t\). We can define \(s: M \to  E\) by \(s(\text{quiet}) = e_{1}\) and \(s(\text{active}) = e_{3}\). Tagging the selected record returns the original tag, so \(t \circ  s = 1_{M}\). But \(s \circ  t\) is not \(1_{E}\): it sends \(e_{2}\) to \(e_{1}\). The second report has been lost.

A map can admit a return journey that works on one side while failing on the other. A common description of two records does not establish that the records were identical, or even that the description retained everything relevant about them.

Different occurrences of the number five raise several distinct questions. Two written inscriptions can be distinct tokens while referring to the same number. Two five-element sets can be isomorphic as sets while having different elements. Those are different mathematical statements. Neither by itself settles how an Advaitin identity statement refers to the Self. That depends on the text's account of reference, limiting conditions, and what is being denied or affirmed. Category theory gives us several kinds of comparison; it does not supply the correct interpretation merely by replacing an equals sign with \(\cong\).

Isomorphism also depends on the category. Take two labels \(x\) and \(y\). In one poset they are incomparable; in another \(x < y\). The identity-on-labels function from the first poset to the second preserves order, since the first has no nontrivial order to violate. Its inverse as a set-function does not preserve order. The two structures are bijective as sets but this map is not an isomorphism of posets. Cheng's official errata correct an overgeneralization about this point in the book. [Source](cite://eugenia-cheng/joy-of-abstraction/category-cheng)

**Try it.** If an arrow has an inverse, could it have two different inverses \(g\) and \(h\)?

**Answer.** No. Both inverse laws force

\[
\begin{gathered}g = g \circ  1_{B} = g \circ  (f \circ  h)\\= (g \circ  f) \circ  h = 1_{A} \circ  h = h.\end{gathered}
\]

The inverse of a specified isomorphism is unique. There can still be many different isomorphisms between the same two objects. Matching three unstructured records to three labels offers six bijections. To select the philosophically appropriate correspondence, we need additional structure and evidence.

### What the category theory contributes

The argument so far uses typed logic and sets. We can now use the category definition just established: objects, arrows with specified sources and targets, an identity at each object, and associative composition with those identities. A functor assigns objects and arrows between two categories while preserving sources, targets, identities and composition. Category theory can organize the resulting theories and their interpretations.

One starting point is the category of set-valued models of the selected theory. An object is an interpretation of the sorts \(X\),\(F\),\(T\) and the two relations satisfying the axiom. A morphism consists of a function on each sort that preserves the positive and negative relational facts. If \(h_{X}\), \(h_{F}\) and \(h_{T}\) are those functions, preservation means, for example:

\[
\operatorname{Pos}(x,f,t)\Longrightarrow\operatorname{Pos}^{\prime}\bigl(h_X(x),h_F(f),h_T(t)\bigr).
\]

The prime marks the relation in the target model. The same requirement applies to \(\operatorname{Neg}\). It is a forward implication; we have not required the converse. Both source and target are models satisfying the axiom. Identity functions preserve each fact. Composing two fact-preserving triples again preserves each fact, because we can apply the first preservation implication and then the second. Componentwise function composition is associative and has identities. That verifies the category axioms for this construction.

At this stage \(F\) is an opaque sort. We have described its elements as meaningful frames, but the formal signature has not yet represented their components. An arbitrary function on \(F\) need not preserve those descriptions. To make that demand mathematical, extend the signature with four sorts and four functions:

\[
\begin{gathered}\begin{aligned}L&=\text{loci},&R&=\text{relations},\\D&=\text{delimiters},&G&=\text{appraisal labels},\end{aligned}\\\operatorname{loc}:F\to L\qquad\operatorname{rel}:F\to R\\\operatorname{del}:F\to D\qquad\operatorname{grade}:F\to G.\end{gathered}
\]

Declare contact and inherence as constants in \(R\) and require them to be distinct. In the expanded category, a morphism has a function on each of the seven sorts. It must preserve those constants, preserve \(\operatorname{Pos}\) and \(\operatorname{Neg}\), and commute with the four component functions. For the locus component this means:

\[
h_L\bigl(\operatorname{loc}(f)\bigr)=\operatorname{loc}^{\prime}\bigl(h_F(f)\bigr).
\]

Either first take a frame’s locus and translate it, or first translate the frame and then take its locus: the result must agree. The same equation is required for relation, delimiter and appraisal label. Identities satisfy these equations; for a composite, substitute the first equation into the second. Associativity still comes from function composition. We have now justified the preservation of the declared component structure. Other particular labels must likewise be named as constants or fixed data if their preservation is required. \(G\) currently has no ordering relation, so this construction has not imposed a hierarchy of reality.

The earlier two-frame model extends to this signature: use one locus, one delimiter and one appraisal label, and let \(R\) contain the distinct elements contact and inherence. Map \(c\) to contact and \(i\) to inherence; the other three functions are constant. All stated constraints hold. We have not required the four components to determine a frame uniquely; that would be another axiom. None of these structural declarations by itself proves that a translation between historical schools preserves what they mean by locus or appraisal. The source-to-model interpretation must establish that separately.

Here is an actual morphism in the expanded category. Make a second model with two targets \(p_{1}\) and \(p_{2}\). Give each target the same two facts as \(p\) above: positive in \(c\) and negative in \(i\), at \(\text{now}\). Keep the frames, times and all four component sorts unchanged. Send both targets to \(p\), and use identity functions on the other six sorts. Every positive fact maps to \(\operatorname{Pos}(p,c,\text{now})\), every negative fact maps to \(\operatorname{Neg}(p,i,\text{now})\), and all four component equations hold. This is a morphism. It has no inverse because two distinct targets were mapped to one. Agreement of the displayed facts did not force equality of the two targets in the source model; the selected axioms simply did not include such an identification rule. This is a fact about this formal language, not an argument settling the individuality of conscious subjects.

The failed frame-erasing recipe is not a functor to the proposed frame-free model category, because it does not even send the displayed model to an object of that category. Its output violates the target axiom. Checking object admissibility comes before checking preservation of identities and composition.

For comparison, here is a preview of the later worked construction. We can also form the syntactic category of the theory, whose objects are definable collections and whose arrows are definable relations proved total and single-valued on their domains. That construction records what the theory permits us to define and infer. It is a different category from the category whose objects are whole models. Chapter 4 constructs one such definable arrow from the reference argument.

These two categories answer different questions. The model category compares realizations of the selected rules. The syntactic category organizes the structures and maps definable from them. Neither construction establishes that the entire metaphysics of a thinker has been captured. That depends on the adequacy of the selected signature and axioms to the connected argument.

### A simplification the author himself considers

It would still be unfair to present the indexed account as the only analysis Madhusūdana allows. He gives a long conditional alternative. Its conclusion is shorter because its antecedent changes the accepted account of absence.

**Advaitasiddhi, Second definition; main printed 70–73,77,80/PDF 90–93,97,100** [Source](cite://madhusudana/advaitasiddhi/inspectable-nav-as-conditional)

> yadi punaḥ dhvaṃsaprāgabhāvapratiyogitvam ivātyantābhāvapratiyogitvam ākāśādau na syāt; sādhakamānābhāvasya tulyatvāt, ihākāśo nāstīti pratyakṣapratītyasambhavāt, anumāne cānukūlatarkābhāvāt, sāmānyato dṛṣṭamātreṇa dhvaṃsaprāgabhāvapratiyogitvasyāpi siddhiprasaṅgāt, tadvyatirekeṇa kasyacit kāryasyānupapatter abhāvāc ca, evaṃ saṃyogasambandhena ghaṭavati bhūtale samavāyasambandhena ghaṭābhāvasattve mānābhāvāl lāghavena ghaṭātyantābhāvatvenaiva ghaṭasāmānādhikaraṇyavirodhitvakalpanāt sambandhaviśeṣapraveśe ca gauravāt ghaṭasamavāyamātraviṣayatayā pratīter upapatter ādhārādheyabhāvasya pratyakṣasiddhatvena ghaṭasyāvṛttitvaśaṅkānudayād uktayukteś ca na ghaṭāder atyantābhāvasāmānādhikaraṇyam; evaṃ ca saṃyogatadabhāvayor naikādhikaraṇyam; agre vṛkṣaḥ kapisaṃyogī mūle neti pratīter agramūlayor eva saṃyogatadabhāvavattayopapatteḥ, tadā sanmātraniṣṭhātyantābhāvapratiyogitvam eva mithyātvaṃ mantavyam.

If, however, space and the like were not counterpositives of absolute absence, just as they are not counterpositives of destruction or prior absence—because the lack of establishing evidence is the same; because direct apprehension “space is not here” is impossible; because inference lacks a supporting suppositional argument; because mere inference from general observation would also establish their being counterpositives of destruction and prior absence; and because no effect would be inexplicable without it—and likewise a pot and the like do not share a locus with [their] absolute absence, because there is no evidence for a pot’s absence through inherence on ground that has a pot through contact; because economy warrants positing incompatibility with sharing a locus with a pot simply through being absolute absence of a pot; because introducing a particular relation adds complexity; because the apprehension is explicable as having only the pot’s inherence as its object; because the directly established relation of support and supported prevents a doubt that the pot occurs nowhere; and because of the reasoning already stated; and likewise contact and its absence do not have one locus, since the apprehension “the tree has contact with a monkey at its top, not at its root” is explicable by the top and root themselves having contact and its absence—then falsity is to be understood simply as being the counterpositive of absolute absence located in existent reality as such.

The beginning *yadi*, “if,” and the conclusion *tadā*, “then,” govern the whole passage. The intervening reasons concern evidence, inference, explanatory need and economy. In particular, the tree’s top and root are treated as the loci of contact and its absence, rather than making their distinction a delimiter within one undifferentiated locus.

We therefore have two analyses to compare, not an instruction to delete every index. A mathematical comparison would need to show how the second analysis represents the cases expressed by the first. If a delimiter becomes part of a more finely specified locus, information may have been reorganized rather than erased. Whether that reorganization preserves every relevant judgment is a further proposition to state and prove. The frame-erasing counterexample above defeats indiscriminate deletion; it does not refute every possible reformulation.

This is a concrete method for the larger inquiry. Begin with the actual distinctions and reasons. State a proposed representation. Construct a case satisfying it, and test a plausible simplification that might lose something important. Then compare the theories through operations whose preservation properties are demonstrated.

## 3. Selfhood, instruction and appearance in Advaita

A qualified negation leaves open what the account positively says about its subject. Śaṅkara begins from the self that makes knowing possible. His accounts of instruction, reflection and causal nonotherness give different reasons for distinguishing a changing presentation from the reality it discloses.

### The Advaita questions

Advaita’s identity claims become clearer when we ask what is being identified, what is being denied, and what recognition changes. Knowing the self is not described simply as finding one more object. Saying that an effect is nonother than its cause does not transfer every feature of clay into Brahman. A divine form need not reduce the Lord to an ordinary embodied person. Nor does realization necessarily end devotion.

The passages below let those claims develop in the authors’ own words. The root texts come before the interpretations, and an opponent’s objection remains distinct from an author’s reply. The comparisons establish requirements for a later mathematical account.

### The self and the work of instruction

**Śaṅkara, Brahmasūtrabhāṣya, 2.3.7** ([source](cite://shankara/brahma-sutra-bhasya/inspectable-a-sh-self1)).

> nahyātmāgantukaḥ kasyacit, svayaṃsiddhatvāt / nahyātmātmanaḥ pramāṇamapekṣya sidhyati / tasya hi pratyakṣādīni pramāṇānyaprasiddhaprameyasiddhaya upādīyante / nahyākāśādayaḥ padārdhāḥ pramāṇanirapekṣāḥ svayaṃsiddhāḥ kenacidabhyupagamyante / ātmā tu pramāṇādivyavahārāśrayatvātprāgeva pramāṇādivyavahārātsidhyati / naceddṛśasya nirākaraṇaṃ saṃbhavati / āgantukaṃ hi vastu nirākriyate na svarūpam / ya eva hi nirākartā tadeva tasya svarūpam / nahyagnerauṣṇyamagninā nirākriyate /

Literal translation: For the self is not adventitious to anyone, being established by itself. For the self is not established by awaiting a means of knowledge about itself. Its means of knowledge, perception and the others, are employed to establish objects of knowledge not already established. For no one accepts things such as space as established by themselves independently of means of knowledge. The self, however, because it is the support of transactions involving means of knowledge and the rest, is established before those transactions. Nor is negation of such [a self] possible. An adventitious thing is negated, not one’s own nature. For whoever is the negator is precisely that one’s own nature. Fire’s heat is not negated by fire.

The electronic wording reproduced here has naceddṛśasya and aprasiddhaprameyasiddhaya upādīyante; the institutional text reads na cedṛśasya and asiddhaprameyasiddhaye. The translation follows the shared argument about the self’s already established status.

The proposal that selfhood is categorically special has direct textual support. Śaṅkara does not begin by postulating one more object whose existence must be inferred like an unobserved material thing. BSB2.3.7 argues that the self is already presupposed by the activity of establishing or denying objects ([source: Brahmasūtrabhāṣya 2.3.7](https://advaitasharada.sringeri.net/read/brahmasutra-bhashya/2/)). The attempted denier does not eliminate the denier’s own nature. This establishes an asymmetry between epistemic subject and established object.

His account of self-knowledge consequently refuses to turn the seer into a seen object:

**Bṛhadāraṇyakopaniṣadbhāṣya, 1.4.10** ([source](cite://shankara/brhadaranyaka-bhashya/inspectable-a-sh-nonobject)).

> na ca dṛśyā dṛṣṭirdraṣṭāraṃ viṣayīkartumutsahate, yatastāmākāṅkṣeta /
> na ca svarūpaviṣayākhāṅkṣāsvasyaiva /
> tasmādajñānādhyāropaṇanivṛttireva'atmānamevāvet ityuktam,nātmano viṣayīkaraṇam' /

Literal translation: Nor is the seeing that is seen capable of making the seer an object, so that one might desire it. Nor is there a desire on the part of oneself concerning one’s own nature as an object. Therefore “it knew only itself” means solely the cessation of superimposition through ignorance, not making the self an object.

The surrounding commentary distinguishes eternal unseen seeing from transient seen seeing. The quoted conclusion concerns the cessation of erroneous superimposition. It should not be rendered as a subject creating an accurate inner picture of another object called “the true self.”

The corresponding semantic instruction in the Upadeśasāhasrī is reciprocal correction:

**Upadeśasāhasrī, I 18.194, 18.195** ([source](cite://shankara/upadesa-sahasri/inspectable-a-sh-cor3)).

> tattvamos tulyanīḍārtham asīty etat padaṃ bhavet /
> tacchabdaḥ pratyagātmārthas tacchabdārthas tvamas tathā //

> duḥkhitvāpratyagātmatvaṃ vārayetām ubhāv api /
> evaṃ ca netinetyarthaṃ gamayetāṃ parasparam //

Literal translation: In “you are that,” the word “are” would mean that “that” and “you” have the same abode [referent]. The word “that” has the inward self as its meaning, and “you” likewise has the meaning of “that.” Both should ward off affliction and non-inward-selfhood; thus they should mutually convey the meaning of “not this, not this.”

The words share a referent while correcting the restrictions wrongly associated with each side. Thus the critique of crude identity is well placed: simply equating every empirical property of “you” with every predicate previously attributed to “that” would not reproduce the passage. But the correction also does not mean that the two expressions merely resemble each other. The text insists on their common reference. A future semantics needs to distinguish referent, modes of presentation, and rejected attribution before it can ask what mathematical equality expresses.

The unused-capacity proposal and Śaṅkara’s replies are distinguished in the passage:

**Śaṅkara, Brahmasūtrabhāṣya, 2.3.40** ([source](cite://shankara/brahma-sutra-bhasya/inspectable-a-sh-agency)).

> nanu sthitāyāmapi kartṛtvaśaktau kartṛtvakāryaparihārātpuruṣārthaḥ setsyati / tatparihāraśca nimittaparihārāt / yathāgnerdahanaśaktiyuktasyāpi kāṣṭhaviyogāddahanakāryābhāvāt /

> na / nimittānāmapi śaktilakṣaṇena saṃbandhena saṃbaddhānāmatyantaparihārāsaṃbhavāt /

> nanu mokṣasādhanavidhānānmokṣaḥ setsyati /

> na / sādhanāyattasyānityatvāt / apica nityaśuddhabuddhamuktātmapratipādanānmokṣasiddhirabhimatā / tādṛgātmapratipādanaṃ ca na svābhāvike kartṛtve 'vakalpeta / tasmādupādhidharmādhyāsenaivātmanaḥ kartṛtvaṃ na svābhāvikam /

Literal translation: [Objection:] Even while the capacity for agency remains, the human goal will be accomplished through avoiding agency’s effect; and avoiding that [effect] comes from avoiding its occasion, just as fire, though endowed with the capacity to burn, has no burning-effect through separation from wood. [Reply:] No, because it is impossible wholly to avoid even the occasions, connected through the relation characterized by capacity. [Objection:] Liberation will be accomplished because its means are prescribed. [Reply:] No, because what depends on means is impermanent. Moreover, liberation is held to be established through teaching the self as eternally pure, aware and free. Teaching such a self would not be possible if agency were intrinsic. Therefore the self’s agency is only through superimposition of properties of limiting conditions, not intrinsic.

BSB2.3.40 also records a serious opponent proposal: agency may remain as an unused capacity, like fire without fuel. Śaṅkara answers that the possibility of renewed occasions for suffering would defeat secure liberation ([source: Brahmasūtrabhāṣya 2.3.40](https://advaitasharada.sringeri.net/read/brahmasutra-bhashya/2/)). That objection cannot be borrowed as his own doctrine of intrinsic agency.

### The mind as a reflecting medium

The Upadeśasāhasrī gives a linguistic account of “knows,” rather than merely mentioning a mirror:

**Upadeśasāhasrī, I 18.53, 18.54** ([source](cite://shankara/upadesa-sahasri/inspectable-a-sh-ref1)).

> ātmābhāsas tu tiṅvācyo dhātvarthaś ca dhiyaḥ kriyā /
> ubhayaṃ cāvivekena jānātīty ucyate mṛṣā //

> na buddher avabodho 'sti nātmano vidyate kriyā /
> ato nānyatarasyāpi jānātīti ca yujyate //

Literal translation: The appearance of the self is what the finite verb ending expresses, and the root-meaning is the intellect’s activity. Both, through non-discrimination, are falsely spoken of as “knows.” There is no awareness belonging to the intellect, and no activity exists in the self. Therefore “knows” is not appropriate to either one [by itself].

The finite ending and verbal root are doing different explanatory work. The apparently unitary act of knowing involves consciousness and the intellect’s activity, with non-discrimination attributing a combination to the knower. The following reflection discussion makes the reciprocal attribution explicit:

**Upadeśasāhasrī, I 18.63, 18.64, 18.65, 18.66** ([source](cite://shankara/upadesa-sahasri/inspectable-a-sh-ref2)).

> ādarśamukhasāmānyaṃ mukhasyeṣṭaṃ hi mānavaiḥ /
> mukhasya pratibimbo hi mukhākāreṇa dṛśyate //

> yatra yasyāvabhāsas tu tayor evāvivekataḥ /
> jānātīti kriyāṃ sarvo loko vakti svabhavataḥ //

> buddheḥ kartṛtvam adhyasya jānātīti jña ucyate /
> tathā caitanyam adhyasya jñatvaṃ buddher ihocyate //

> svarūpaṃ cātmano jñānaṃ nityaṃ jyotiḥ śruter yataḥ /
> na buddhyā kriyate tasmān nātmanānyena vā sadā //

Literal translation: People accept a likeness of the face in a mirror, for the face’s reflection is seen in the form of the face. Where there is the appearance of one in another, through non-discrimination of just those two, all people naturally speak of the activity as “knows.” Having superimposed the intellect’s agency, the knower is said to know; likewise, having superimposed consciousness, knowingness is here spoken of as belonging to the intellect. Knowledge is the self’s own nature, eternal light, because of scripture; therefore it is never made by the intellect, by the self, or by another.

**Śaṅkara, Upadeśasāhasrī, I 18.70, 18.71** ([source](cite://shankara/upadesa-sahasri/inspectable-a-sh-ref3)).

> ādarśānuvidhāyitvaṃ chāyāyā asyate mukhe /
> buddhidharmānukāritvaṃ jñābhāsasya tatheṣyate //

> buddhes tu pratyayās tasmād ātmābhāsena dīpitāḥ /
> grāhakā iva bhāsante dahantīvolmukādayaḥ //

Literal translation: The shadow’s conformity to the mirror is cast upon the face; in the same way, conformity to the intellect’s properties is accepted of the appearance of awareness. Therefore the intellect’s cognitions, illuminated by the appearance of the self, appear as though apprehenders, just as firebrands and the like appear as though burning.

The next selected verses state an objection and the limits of the illustration:

**Śaṅkara, Upadeśasāhasrī, I 18.75, 18.85, 18.86** ([source](cite://shankara/upadesa-sahasri/inspectable-a-sh-ref4)).

> adhyakṣasya samīpe tu siddhiḥ syād iti cen matam /
> nādhyakṣe 'nupakāritvād anyatrāpi prasaṅgataḥ //

> nanv evaṃ dṛśisaṃkrāntir ayaḥpiṇḍe 'gnivad bhavet /
> mukhābhāsavad ity etad ādarśe tan nirākṛtam //

> kṛṣṇāyo rohitābhāsam ity etad dṛṣṭam ucyate /
> dṛṣṭadārṣṭāntatulyatvaṃ na tu sarvātmanā kvacit //

Literal translation: If it is thought that establishment would occur near the witness: no, because nothing is contributed to the witness, and because the consequence would extend elsewhere. [Objection:] Surely thus there would be a transfer of seeing, like fire into an iron ball? [Reply:] This has been ruled out by saying “like the appearance of a face in a mirror.” Black iron with a red appearance is stated as what is observed; the example and the illustrated case are nowhere alike in every respect.

This gives at least four things to distinguish: the self’s unproduced awareness, the changing intellect, the self’s appearance in that medium, and the attribution through which ordinary knowing is reported. The activity is not simply exported into the self, and consciousness is not literally manufactured by an inert intellect. The next verses describe a specific misattribution: the reflected image conforms to the mirror, and that conformity is attributed to the face. Similarly, the appearance of awareness conforms to the intellect’s properties. The illuminated cognitions then appear as though they themselves apprehend. This separates a change in the medium and its appearance from a change in the self, while explaining why ordinary cognition looks like an act of a knower ([source: Upadeśasāhasrī I 18.70, 18.71](http://gretil.sub.uni-goettingen.de/gretil/corpustei/sa_zaMkara-upadezasAhasrI.xml)). They also refuse a crude transfer model and restrict the analogy ([source: Upadeśasāhasrī I 18.75, 18.85, 18.86](http://gretil.sub.uni-goettingen.de/gretil/corpustei/sa_zaMkara-upadezasAhasrI.xml)).

That last restriction is mathematically consequential. We should not require a full isomorphism between a physical mirror and cognition. The analogy may preserve some explanatory relations while rejecting others. First identify the preserved relations; only then ask whether a functor, relation-preserving map, or different structure captures them.

### Clay, becoming many, and entry

The Chāndogya instruction offers a route from knowing one thing to knowing a range of effects. The root wording introduces the terms “known,” “modification,” and “real” that the commentary will interpret.

**Chāndogya Upaniṣad, 6.1.4** ([source](cite://upanishads/chandogya/inspectable-a-chu-clay)).

> yathā somyaikena mṛtpiṇḍena sarvaṃ mṛnmayaṃ vijñātaṃ syāt |
> vācārambhaṇaṃ vikāro nāmadheyaṃ mṛttikety eva satyam ||

Literal translation: Just as, dear one, through one lump of clay all that is made of clay would be known: a modification is a name, having speech as its commencement/support; clay alone is the real.

**Chāndogya Upaniṣad, 6.1.5–6** ([source](cite://upanishads/chandogya/inspectable-a-chu-metals)).

> yathā somyaikena lohamaṇinā sarvaṃ lohamayaṃ vijñātaṃ syāt |
> vācārambhaṇaṃ vikāro nāmadheyaṃ loham ity eva satyam ||

> yathā somyaikena nakhanikṛntanena sarvaṃ kārṣṇāyasaṃ vijñātaṃ syāt |
> vācārambhaṇaṃ vikāro nāmadheyaṃ kṛṣṇāyasam ity eva satyam |
> evaṃ somya sa ādeśo bhavatīti ||

Literal translation: Just as, dear one, through one lump/bead of metal all that is made of metal would be known: a modification is a name, having speech as its commencement/support; metal alone is the real. Just as, dear one, through one nail-cutter all that is made of black iron would be known: a modification is a name, having speech as its commencement/support; black iron alone is the real. Thus, dear one, that instruction is.

The next two examples are metal and black iron (6.1.5–6, [source: Chāndogya Upaniṣad 6.1.5–6](http://gretil.sub.uni-goettingen.de/gretil/corpustei/sa_chAndogyopaniSad.xml)). The local root has *loham*, while the institutional root has *lohitam*; “gold” is supplied in Śaṅkara’s explanatory reading, rather than given uniformly in the root witnesses. This variation does not remove the repeated explanatory structure, but it matters when we claim to quote a specific material.

The root first states its own position and then reports the contrary proposal:

**Chāndogya Upaniṣad, 6.2.1** ([source](cite://upanishads/chandogya/inspectable-a-chu-being)).

> sad eva somyedam agra āsīd ekam evādvitīyam |
> tad dhaika āhur asad evedam agra āsīd ekam evādvitīyam |
> tasmād asataḥ saj jāyata ||

Literal translation: Being alone, dear one, was this in the beginning, one alone without a second. Some say: “Nonbeing alone was this in the beginning, one alone without a second; from that nonbeing, being was born.”

The same chapter does not stop at an abstract material identity. After the initial statement of Being and the reported contrary proposal of nonbeing in 6.2.1 ([source: Chāndogya Upaniṣad 6.2.1](http://gretil.sub.uni-goettingen.de/gretil/corpustei/sa_chAndogyopaniSad.xml)), it rejects origin from nonbeing and speaks of becoming many:

**Chāndogya Upaniṣad, 6.2.2 and 6.2.3 opening** ([source](cite://upanishads/chandogya/inspectable-a-chu-origination)).

> kutas tu khalu somyaivaṃ syād iti hovāca |
> katham asataḥ saj jāyeta |
> sat tv eva somyedam agra āsīd ekam evādvitīyam ||

> tad aikṣata |
> bahu syāṃ prajāyeyeti |
> tat tejo 'sṛjata |

Literal translation: “But how indeed, dear one, could it be so?” he said. “How could being arise from nonbeing? Being alone, dear one, was this in the beginning, one alone without a second.” It saw: “May I be many; may I come forth.” It emitted heat/fire.

**Chāndogya Upaniṣad, 6.3.2** ([source](cite://upanishads/chandogya/inspectable-a-chu-entry)).

> seyaṃ devataikṣata |
> hantāham imās tisro devatā anena jīvenātmanānupraviśya nāmarūpe vyākaravāṇīti ||

Literal translation: That deity saw: “Come, having entered these three deities through this living self, let me differentiate name and form.”

These are distinct root claims: the initial priority of Being, becoming many, and differentiation of name and form through entry. The commentaries explain how entry is compatible with nonduality and how Being, deity, living self, and name/form are related.

Śaṅkara makes his inference from clay explicit, including the anticipated objection that knowing a cause should not reveal something else:

**Chāndogyopaniṣadbhāṣya, 6.1.4** ([source](cite://shankara/chandogya-bhashya/inspectable-a-sh-clay)).

> kathaṃ mṛtpiṇḍe kāraṇe vijñāte kāryamanyadvijñātaṃ syāt /
> naiṣa doṣaḥ /
> kāraṇenānanyatvātkāryasya /
> yanmanyase 'nyasminvijñāte 'nyanna jñāyata iti /
> satyamevaṃ syāt /
> yadyanyatkāraṇātkāryaṃ syānna tvevamanyatkāraṇātkāryam /
> kathaṃ tarhidaṃ loka idaṃ kāraṇamayasya vikāra iti /

> śṛṇu /
> vācā'rambhaṇaṃ vāgārambhaṇaṃ vāgālambanamityetat /
> ko 'sau vikāro nāmadheyaṃ nāmaiva nāmadheyaṃ svārthe dheyapratyayaḥ /
> vāgālambanamātraṃ nāmaiva kevalaṃ na vikāro nāma vastvasti paramārthato mṛttiketyeva tu mṛttiketyeva tu mṛttikaiva satyaṃ vastvasti //4 //

Literal translation: [Objection:] How, when the lump of clay, the cause, is known, would another thing, the effect, be known? [Reply:] This is no fault, because the effect is not other than the cause. You think that when one thing is known another is not known. That would indeed be so if the effect were other than the cause; but the effect is not thus other than the cause. [Objection:] How then [does one say] in the world, “this is the cause; this is its modification”? [Reply:] Listen. “Having speech as commencement” means having speech as commencement, having speech as support. What is that modification? A name: nāmadheya is simply nāman, the suffix dheya being in its own meaning. It is only a name supported by speech; there is no thing called modification ultimately. Clay alone, clay alone—clay alone exists as the real thing.

**Śaṅkara, Chāndogyopaniṣadbhāṣya, 6.2.1, gloss on advitīya** ([source](cite://shankara/chandogya-bhashya/inspectable-a-sh-secondcause)).

> mṛdvyatirekeṇa mṛdo yathānyadghaṭādyākāreṇa pariṇamayitṛkulālādinimittakāraṇaṃ dṛṣṭaṃ tathā sadvyatirekeṇa sataḥ sahakārikāraṇaṃ dvitīyaṃ vastvantaraṃ prāptaṃ pratiṣidhyate 'dvitīyamiti /
> nāsya dvitīyaṃ vastvantaraṃ vidyata ityadvitīyam /


Literal translation: Just as, apart from clay, another efficient cause such as the potter who transforms clay into the form of pots and the rest is seen, so [the assumption of] another thing, a second assisting cause apart from Being, is negated by “without a second.” It has no other thing as a second: thus “without a second.”

The conclusion is stronger than resemblance and narrower than indiscriminate predicate substitution. An effect does not establish a second ultimate thing apart from its cause; the conventional distinction “cause/effect” receives an explanation. At 6.2.1 he also refuses to transfer the potter’s external agency into this case: “without a second” excludes a second cooperating cause ([source: Chāndogyopaniṣadbhāṣya 6.2.1, gloss on advitīya](https://advaitasharada.sringeri.net/read/chandogya-bhashya/6/)). Therefore a model cannot infer a separate maker merely because pots ordinarily have potters.

At 6.3.2, however, he writes the affirmation a proposed realist integration must take seriously:

**Chāndogyopaniṣadbhāṣya, 6.3.2, complete concluding inference** ([source](cite://shankara/chandogya-bhashya/inspectable-a-sh-sadatman)).

> ataḥ sadātmanā sarvavyavahārāṇāṃ sarvavikārāṇāṃ ca satyatvaṃ sato'nyatve ca anṛtatvamiti na kaściddoṣaḥ tārkikairihānuvaktuṃ śakyaḥ, yathā itaretaraviruddhadvaitavādāḥ svabuddhivikalpamātrā atattvaniṣṭhā iti śakyaṃ vaktum ||

Literal translation: Therefore, since all transactions and all modifications are true as Being, and untrue in otherness from Being, no fault can be stated here by dialecticians, as it can be said that mutually opposed dualist theories are merely constructions of their own intellect and do not rest in reality.

“True as Being” and “untrue in otherness from Being” belong to one argument. A model that marks every name/form simply nonexistent loses its positive side. A model that treats name/form as ultimately separate substances loses its qualification. This is a specific dependence and nonotherness claim; it does not yet establish that every distinction is an equally ultimate feature of Being.

### Fullness, causal power, and the limits of an analogy

The fullness mantra reads:

**Bṛhadāraṇyakopaniṣad, 5.1.1, opening fullness mantra (two lines before khaṃ brahma prose)** ([source](cite://upanishads/brhadaranyaka/inspectable-a-up-fullness)).

> pūrṇam adaḥ pūrṇam idaṃ pūrṇāt pūrṇam udacyate |
> pūrṇasya pūrṇam ādāya pūrṇam evāvaśiṣyate |

Literal translation: That is full; this is full. From the full, the full arises. Having taken the full of the full, the full alone remains.

The root says *pūrṇasya*, “of the full,” rather than prescribing a mathematical subtraction operation. Śaṅkara’s ensuing explanation interprets taking through knowledge of one’s complete nature. His treatment affirms that appearing as an effect need not diminish the reality’s fullness:

**Bṛhadāraṇyakopaniṣadbhāṣya, 5.1.1, own exposition** ([source](cite://shankara/brhadaranyaka-bhashya/inspectable-a-sh-full1)).

> tat sampūrṇamākāśavad vyāpi nirantaraṃ nirupādhikaṃ ca tadevedaṃ sopādhikaṃ nāmarūpasthaṃvyavahārāpannaṃ pūrṇaṃ svena rūpeṇa paramātmanā vyāpyeva nopādhiparicchinnena viśeṣātmanā /
> tadidaṃ viśeṣāpannaṃ kāryātmakaṃ brahma pūrṇāt kāraṇātmana udacyata udricyata udgacchatītyetat /
> yadyapi kāryātmanodricyate tathāpi yat svarūpaṃ pūrṇatvaṃ paramātmabhāvaṃ tanna jāhāti pūrṇamevodricyate /

Literal translation: That is complete, pervasive like space, without gaps and without limiting conditions. This very [reality], with limiting conditions, situated in name and form, entered into transactions, is complete—pervasive through its own nature as the supreme Self, not through its particular nature delimited by limiting conditions. This Brahman, entered into particularity and having the nature of an effect, rises from the complete having the nature of a cause: rises up, comes forth—this is the meaning. Although it rises as an effect, it does not abandon what is its own nature, completeness, the condition of the supreme Self; it comes forth complete.

The rival interpretation introduced by “some explain” reads:

**Śaṅkara, Bṛhadāraṇyakopaniṣadbhāṣya, 5.1.1, rival thesis** ([source](cite://shankara/brhadaranyaka-bhashya/inspectable-a-sh-full2)).

> atraike varṇayanti-pūrṇātkāraṇātpūrṇaṃ kāryamudricyate /
> udriktaṃ kāryaṃ vartamānakāle 'pi pūrṇameva paramārthavastubhūtaṃ dvaitarūpeṇa /
> punaḥ pralayakāle pūrṇasya kāryasya pūrṇatāmādāyā'tmani dhitvā pūrṇamevāvaśiṣyate kāraṇarūpam /
> evamutpattisthitipralayeṣu triṣvapi kāleṣu kāryakāraṇayoḥ pūrṇataiva /
> sā caikaiva pūrṇatā kāryakāraṇayorbhedena vyapadiśyate /
> evaṃ ca dvaitādvaitātmakamekaṃ brahma /
> yathā kila samudro jalataraṅgaphenabudbudādyātmaka eva /
> yathā ca jalaṃ satyaṃ tadudbhavāśca taraṅgaphelabudbudādayaḥ samudrātmabhūtā evā'virbhāvatirobhāvadharmiṇaḥ paramārthasatyā eva /
> evaṃ sarvamidaṃ dvaitaṃ paramārthasatyameva jalataraṅgādisthānīyaṃ samudrajalasthānīyaṃ tu paraṃ brahma /


Literal translation: Here some explain: the complete effect rises from the complete cause. The arisen effect is complete even while present, a thing that is ultimately real in the form of duality. Again, at dissolution, taking the completeness of the complete effect and placing it in oneself, the complete alone remains as cause. Thus in all three times—origination, continuance and dissolution—cause and effect are complete. That completeness is one, designated through the distinction of cause and effect. Thus Brahman is one, with the nature of duality and nonduality. Just as, supposedly, the ocean consists precisely of water, waves, foam, bubbles and the rest; and just as water is real and its arising waves, foam, bubbles and the rest are the ocean itself, possess appearance and disappearance, and are ultimately real: so all this duality is ultimately real, occupying the place of waves and the rest, while highest Brahman occupies the place of the ocean’s water.

But the connected discussion then presents a rival theory in which duality and nonduality are both real, using ocean and waves ([source: Bṛhadāraṇyakopaniṣadbhāṣya 5.1.1, rival thesis](https://advaitasharada.sringeri.net/read/brihadaranyaka-bhashya/5/)). Śaṅkara’s reply to this rival explicitly limits the domain of its objection:

**Bṛhadāraṇyakopaniṣadbhāṣya, 5.1.1, reply** ([source](cite://shankara/brhadaranyaka-bhashya/inspectable-a-sh-full3)).

> na tviha tathā vastuviṣaye dvaitaṃ vā syādadvaitaṃ veti vikalpaḥ sambhavatyapuruṣatantratvādātmavastunaḥ /
> virodhācca dvaitādvaitatvayorekasya /
> tasmānna suvivakṣiteyaṃ kalpanā /


> nanu brahmaṇo dvaitādvaitātmakatve samudrādidṛṣṭāntā vidyante kathamucyate bhavataikasya dvaitādvaitatvaṃ viruddhamiti /
> na /
> anyaviṣayatvāt /
> nityāniravayavavastuviṣayaṃ hi viruddhatvamavocāma dvaitādvaitatvasya na kāryaviṣaye sāvayave /

Literal translation: But here, concerning reality, no option is possible of “it may be duality or it may be nonduality,” because the reality that is the self is not dependent on a person; and because duality and nonduality in one thing contradict. Therefore this conception has not been carefully intended. [Objection:] If Brahman has the nature of duality and nonduality, there are examples such as the ocean; how do you say that duality and nonduality of one thing contradict? [Reply:] No, because [those examples] concern another domain. We said that duality and nonduality contradict concerning an eternal, partless reality, not concerning a composite effect.

The restriction to eternal, partless reality matters. He is not declaring all composites or all cases of unity-with-difference logically impossible. Conversely, the admitted composite examples do not establish the same structure in the partless absolute. “Fullness survives appearance” and “the absolute is ultimately a dual/nondual composite” are not the same thesis.

**Brahmasūtra, 2.1.14** ([source](cite://badarayana/brahma-sutra/inspectable-a-bs-cause)).

> tadananyatvam ārambhaṇaśabdādibhyaḥ |

Literal translation: Non-otherness from that, because of the word ārambhaṇa and the rest.

**Śaṅkara, Brahmasūtrabhāṣya, 2.1.14, causal inference** ([source](cite://shankara/brahma-sutra-bhasya/inspectable-a-sh-cause)).

> kāryamākāśādikaṃ bahuprapañcaṃ jagat, kāraṇaṃ paraṃbrahma, tasmātkāraṇātparamārthato 'nanyatvaṃ vyatirekeṇābhāvaḥ kāryasyāvagamyate /

Literal translation: The effect is the world with its extensive manifold beginning with space; the cause is highest Brahman. The effect’s non-otherness ultimately from that cause, highest Brahman—its absence apart from it—is understood.

Brahmasūtra 2.1.14 gives the compressed root statement of nonotherness from the “speech-commencement” passages ([source: Brahmasūtra 2.1.14](https://advaitasharada.sringeri.net/read/brahmasutra-bhashya/2/)). Śaṅkara explains this as the effect’s absence apart from its cause ([source: Brahmasūtrabhāṣya 2.1.14, causal inference](https://advaitasharada.sringeri.net/read/brahmasutra-bhashya/2/)). He then explicitly answers the inference that the clay illustration commits Brahman to material transformation:

**Brahmasūtrabhāṣya, 2.1.14, reply to transformation inference** ([source](cite://shankara/brahma-sutra-bhasya/inspectable-a-sh-nottransformation)).

> nanu mṛdādidṛṣṭāntapraṇayanātpariṇāmavadbrahma śāstrasyābhimatamiti gamyate / pariṇāmino hi mṛdādayor'thā loke samadhigatā iti /

> netyucyate / 'sa vā eṣa mahājana ātmājaro 'maro 'mṛto 'bhayo brahma' (bṛ. 4.4.25) 'sa eṣa neti netyātmā' (bṛ. 3.9.23), 'asthūlamanaṇu' (bṛ. 3.8.8) ityādyābhyaḥ sarvavikriyāpratiṣedhaśrutibhyo brahmaṇaḥ kūṭasthatvāvagamāt / nahyekasya brahmaṇaḥ pariṇāmadharmatvaṃ tadrahitatvaṃ ca śkyaṃ pratipattum /

Literal translation: [Objection:] From the introduction of examples such as clay, Brahman possessing transformation is understood to be intended by scripture; for things such as clay are known in the world as transforming. [Reply:] No: Brahman’s invariance is understood from scriptural denials of all modification, such as “this great unborn self, unaging, deathless, immortal, fearless, Brahman,” “this self is not this, not this,” and “not gross, not minute.” For transformation and its absence cannot be understood of the one Brahman.

**Śaṅkara, Brahmasūtrabhāṣya, 2.1.14, role of creation teaching** ([source](cite://shankara/brahma-sutra-bhasya/inspectable-a-sh-causalpedagogy)).

> tatraitatsiddhaṃ bhavati- brahmaprakaraṇe sarvadharmaviśeṣarahitabrahmadarśanādeva phalasiddhau satyāṃ yattatrāphalaṃ śrūyate brahmaṇo jagadākārapariṇāmitvādi tadbrahmadarśanopāyatvenaiva viniyujyate, phalavatsaṃnidhāvaphalaṃ tadaṅgamitivat / natu svatantraṃ phalāya kalpyata iti /

Literal translation: This is established there: in the discussion of Brahman, since the fruit is accomplished only through seeing Brahman devoid of every particular property, whatever fruitless [teaching] is heard there—Brahman’s transformation into the form of the world and the like—is applied only as a means to seeing Brahman, according to “what is fruitless in proximity to what bears fruit is its subordinate.” It is not imagined as independently for a fruit.

The clean institutional reading *mahān aja*—“great, unborn”—is material to this argument. The electronic witness’s *mahājana* is defective. His ensuing explanation treats creation teaching as a means to the vision of Brahman ([source: Brahmasūtrabhāṣya 2.1.14, role of creation teaching](https://advaitasharada.sringeri.net/read/brahmasutra-bhashya/2/)). That pedagogical role does not make the entire causal discussion dispensable: the discussion specifies what nonseparateness means and which causal inferences fail.

Nor does Śaṅkara simply discard power. His 2.1.18 argument says:

**Brahmasūtrabhāṣya, 2.1.18, causal restriction argument** ([source](cite://shankara/brahma-sutra-bhasya/inspectable-a-sh-causalpower)).

> śaktiśca kāraṇasya kāryaniyamārthā kalpamānā nānyāsatī vā kāryaṃ niyacchet / asattvāviśeṣādanyatvāviśeṣācca / tasmātkāraṇasyātmabhūtā śaktiḥ śakteścātmabhūtaṃ kāryam /

Literal translation: And a power of the cause posited to account for restriction of the effect could not restrict the effect if it were other [than the cause] or nonexistent, because there would be no difference in nonexistence or in otherness. Therefore the power is the cause’s own nature, and the effect is the power’s own nature.

The argument needs an explanatory restriction: this cause produces this effect. A wholly separate or nonexistent power does not supply that restriction. Yet this passage must be read with 2.1.14, where name/form as māyā-power and the lordship/omniscience of Īśvara receive their explicit conditions:

**Brahmasūtrabhāṣya, 2.1.14, māyā/name-form/Īśvara explanation** ([source](cite://shankara/brahma-sutra-bhasya/inspectable-a-sh-powerstatus)).

> sarvajñasyeśvarasyātmabhūta ivāvidyākalpite nāmarūpe tattvānyatvābhyāmanirvacanīye saṃsāraprapañcabījabhūte sarvajñasyeśvarasya māyāśaktiḥ prakṛtiriti ca śrutismṛtyorabhilapyete /

> tadevamavidyātmakopādhiparicchedāpekṣameveśvarasyeśvaratvaṃ sarvajñatvaṃ sarvaśaktitvaṃ ca na paramārthato vidyāyāpāstasarvopādhisvarūpa ātmanīśitrīśitavyasarvajñatvādivyavahāra upapadyate /

Literal translation: Name and form, imagined through ignorance as though the very self of the omniscient Lord, indefinable as that or other than that, the seeds of the saṃsāric manifold, are called in śruti and smṛti the omniscient Lord’s māyā-power and prakṛti. Thus the Lord’s lordship, omniscience and omnipotence depend precisely on delimitation by ignorance-natured limiting conditions; ultimately, in the self whose nature is stripped of every limiting condition by knowledge, transactions of ruler and ruled, omniscience and the rest do not fit.
