[…] [Candrakīrti:] To turn away from unwholesomeness and so forth those less advanced pupils who perform unwholesome actions, the Blessed Buddhas in some cases also designated “self,” establishing it in the world. […] For those intermediate pupils, to loosen fixation on the view of a real personal identity and to generate desire for nirvāṇa, […] “nonself” too was taught. […] For those advanced pupils whose attachment to self has gone […] [the neither-self-nor-nonself teaching quoted again in the intervening text]. […] For just as the view of self is not reality, so the view of nonself that opposes it is itself not reality.

Candrakīrti’s first explanation treats self-teaching as a response to conduct that denies consequences and responsibility, nonself-teaching as a remedy for entrenched personal-identity views, and the neither-view teaching as appropriate to advanced pupils. These are distinct pedagogical functions. The separated audience clauses are excerpts, not one continuous sentence; the final incomplete clause is connected to the repeated root teaching through an explicit bracketed explanation. A further interpretation introduced afterward by “alternatively” is not fused with this one, and the account is not attributed to every Buddhist school.

The two truths likewise participate in teaching and attainment. Nāgārjuna writes:

Nāgārjuna, *Mūlamadhyamakakārikā / Prasannapadā*, [MMK 24.8 and 24.10; Vaidya 1960 pp 215–216/PDF 256–257](cite://nagarjuna/mula-madhyamaka-karika/inspectable-madhyamaka-two-truths-practice):

> dve satye samupāśritya buddhānāṃ dharmadeśanā / lokasaṃvṛtisatyaṃ ca satyaṃ ca paramārthataḥ // […] vyavahāram anāśritya paramārtho na deśyate / paramārtham anāgamya nirvāṇaṃ nādhigamyate //

The Buddhas’ teaching of dharma relies on two truths: worldly conventional truth and truth ultimately. […] Without relying on ordinary usage, the ultimate is not taught; without reaching the ultimate, nirvāṇa is not attained.

Ordinary usage is necessary for teaching the ultimate, and reaching the ultimate is necessary for nirvāṇa. The distinction does not authorize discarding practice. Nor does it itself establish two independently existing worlds or prove equivalence with Advaita’s reality distinctions. Candrakīrti makes the preservation of convention explicit through another reflection image:

Candrakīrti, *Mūlamadhyamakakārikā / Prasannapadā*, [PP on MMK 24.11; Vaidya 1960 p 216/PDF 257](cite://candrakirti/prasannapada/inspectable-madhyamaka-convention-not-substratum):

> pratibimbākārāyāś ca lokasaṃvṛter abādhanāt karmakarmaphaladharmādharmādikam api na bādhate. na cāpi paramārthaṃ bhāvasvabhāvatvena samāropayati.

And because [the yogin] does not negate worldly convention, which has the form of a reflection, he does not negate action, action’s result, merit, demerit and so forth either. Nor does he superimpose the ultimate as the intrinsic nature of a thing.

Here reflection explains why action, its fruits, merit, and demerit remain conventionally operative. The second sentence prevents that preservation from becoming a substantial ultimate underneath convention. A persistent consciousness reflected in a medium is not asserted by this image.

Dependent arising gives the positive relation between conditioned appearance and the absence of intrinsic arising:

Nāgārjuna; Candrakīrti, *Mūlamadhyamakakārikā / Prasannapadā*, [MMK 24.18 and PP opening explanation; Vaidya 1960 p 219/PDF 260](cite://nagarjuna-candrakirti/mulamadhyamakakarika-prasannapada/inspectable-madhyamaka-dependent-designation):

> yaḥ pratītyasamutpādaḥ śūnyatāṃ tāṃ pracakṣmahe / sā prajñaptir upādāya pratipat saiva madhyamā // yo ’yaṃ pratītyasamutpādo hetupratyayān apekṣya aṅkuravijñānādīnāṃ prādurbhāvaḥ, sa svabhāvenānutpādaḥ. yaś ca svabhāvenānutpādo bhāvānāṃ sā śūnyatā.

What is dependent arising, that we declare to be emptiness. That is designation in dependence [on something]; that itself is the middle way. This dependent arising—the appearance of sprouts, cognitions and so forth depending upon causes and conditions—is nonarising by intrinsic nature. And the nonarising of things by intrinsic nature is emptiness.

Nāgārjuna’s verse comes first; Candrakīrti’s prose then mentions sprouts and cognitions arising in dependence on causes and conditions. Two readings are consequential. The joined expression hetupratyayānapekṣya is divided as hetupratyayān apekṣya, “depending on causes and conditions,” not as independence from them. The printing supports svabhāvena anutpādaḥ, nonarising by intrinsic nature, against misleading spacing in the electronic witness. Conditioned arising and intrinsic arising therefore cannot be collapsed into one assertion that either everything happens or nothing happens.

Candrakīrti gives a concrete dependence example:

Candrakīrti, *Mūlamadhyamakakārikā / Prasannapadā*, [PP on MMK 24.18; Vaidya 1960 pp 219–220/PDF 260–261](cite://candrakirti/prasannapada/inspectable-madhyamaka-chariot):

> cakrādīny upādāya rathāṅgāni rathaḥ prajñapyate. tasya yā svāṅgāny upādāya prajñaptiḥ, sā svabhāvenānutpattiḥ, yā ca svabhāvenānutpattiḥ, sā śūnyatā.

The chariot is designated in dependence on its parts, wheels and so forth. Its designation in dependence on its own parts is nonarising by intrinsic nature; and nonarising by intrinsic nature is emptiness.

A chariot is designated in dependence on its parts. This is not ordinary identity between the chariot and each wheel, and it does not posit a universal consciousness beneath the parts. What it supplies is an argument about designation and intrinsic arising that a comparison must preserve on its own terms.

### Coproducts: retaining alternatives without premature identification

Suppose we have records from two commentarial dossiers \(A\) and \(B\). We want one archive into which either dossier can be placed, while retaining which dossier each record came from. The appropriate construction in \(\mathbf{Set}\) is the **disjoint union** \(A \sqcup  B\). Its elements are tagged records: \((\text{left},a)\) for \(a \in  A\) and \((\text{right},b)\) for \(b \in  B\). The source labels left and right are distinct even when \(A\) and \(B\) happen to be the same set.

The source tag matters even if both dossiers contain a record called “self.” The matching word does not yet establish matching referents, arguments, or doctrines.

The inclusion maps \(\iota _{A}: A \to  A \sqcup  B\) and \(\iota _{B}: B \to  A \sqcup  B\) place each record in the combined archive. Given any functions \(f: A \to  X\) and \(g: B \to  X\), there is exactly one function \(h: A \sqcup  B \to  X\) such that

\[
h \circ  \iota _{A} = f,     h \circ  \iota _{B} = g.
\]

Define it by cases: \(h(\text{left},a) = f(a)\) and \(h(\text{right},b) = g(b)\). Every record belongs to one tagged part, so the definition covers the entire archive without ambiguity.

This is the **coproduct** universal property. It reverses all the arrows in the product property. Products combine maps into their factors; coproducts combine maps out of their summands. In \(\mathbf{Set}\), a product value supplies both entries, whereas a coproduct value specifies an entry from one of the two tagged sources.

**Try it.** Why might ordinary untagged union fail to be a coproduct?

**Answer.** If \(A\) and \(B\) both contain an element labeled “self,” choose \(f\) and \(g\) that send that element to different outputs. An untagged union has only one “self” element, so no function from it can extend both assignments. The coproduct avoids this forced agreement by preserving provenance.

In the inclusion order of subsets, coproduct is ordinary union. Every pair of inclusions \(U \to  W\) and \(V \to  W\) uniquely factors through \(U \cup  V \to  W\). Again, the ambient category determines the construction. The union works there because maps are inclusions, not arbitrary assignments to outputs.

For comparing traditions, retaining tagged alternatives is often the responsible beginning. It is not yet integration. The next question is which cross-tradition identifications are warranted, and what follows when we impose them.

## 10. Divine fullness, pervasion and reciprocal love

Jīva and Baladeva connect causal power, individual awareness, divine form and reciprocal love. Their discussion of Yaśodā’s vision makes the relation between complete presence and limited apprehension particularly concrete.

### Jīva and Baladeva

**The cause, its powers and its changing states**

Baladeva begins from the promise of knowing effects through their cause and asks which causal relation could fulfill it. His explanation differentiates powers and uses a continuing named person to make identity through changing states intelligible.

*Govindabhāṣya, 1.4.23*

Baladeva’s own reply, citing Chāndogya 6.1.3–4.

> brahmaiva jagataḥ prakṛtir upādānam

*Literal translation:* Brahman itself is the material source, the material cause, of the world.

Baladeva argues that knowing a mere efficient cause does not yield knowledge of its effect: knowing the potter does not disclose the pot. The clay example and the teaching’s promise therefore require material causality as well.

His reply explains why an efficient cause alone would not fulfill the promise. The opening “that” refers to the one-known/all-known promise:

> sā kilādeśasya upādānatve sati sambhavet kāryasya tad-avyatirekāt | nimittāt tasyāvyatirekas tu na kulāla-ghaṭayor vyatirekāt | […] sa ca nimitta-mātratābhyupagame na sambhavet | na hi kulāle vijñāte ghaṭo vijñāyate |

*Literal translation:* That [promise] can indeed obtain if the taught [reality] is the material cause, because the effect is nonseparate from it. But its nonseparateness from an efficient cause does not [obtain], because potter and pot are separate. […] And that [knowledge of the effect through knowledge of its material cause] could not obtain if merely efficient causation were accepted. For, when the potter is known, the pot is not [thereby] known.

The ellipsis omits the cited Chāndogya clay sentence, not an objection or a change of speaker. This is the source’s causal argument; it does not make all generic dependence material nonseparation.

[Source witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-pl-g16-support-1).

*Govindabhāṣya, 1.4.26*

Baladeva’s own explanation after cited Śvetāśvatara and Viṣṇupurāṇa verses.

> tatrādyaṁ parākhya-śaktimad-rūpeṇa, dvitīyaṁ tu tad-anya-śakti-dvaya-dvāraiva

*Literal translation:* There the former [efficient causality] is through the form of the possessor of the power called supreme; the latter [material causality], however, is precisely through the other pair of powers.

The connected argument distinguishes intrinsic supreme power from jīva and material powers. The material transformation can be real without turning divine intrinsic nature into a diminished remainder.

[Source witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-pl-g17).

*Govindabhāṣya, 2.1.14*

Baladeva’s reply to the difference-of-cognition/word/function/form/time objection.

> yathaikasyaiva caitrasyāvasthā-viśeṣa-sambandhāt bāla-yuvādi-dhī-śabdāntarādi sambhavati

*Literal translation:* Just as, through connection with a particular state of the very same Caitra, different cognitions and words such as “child” and “youth” arise.

This is an explicit identity-through-change comparison: different shape, time, naming and use do not establish a second underlying substance. The author then distinguishes real manifestation from a shell-silver illusion.

[Source witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-pl-g18).

*Govindabhāṣya, 2.1.17*

Baladeva reply to asad-vyapadeśa objection.

> ekasyaiva dravyasya upādeyopādānobhayāvasthasya sthaulyaṁ saukṣmyaṁ cety avasthātmakaṁ dharma-dvayaṁ sad-asac-chabda-bodhyam

*Literal translation:* Of the very same substance in both effect and material-cause states, the two properties consisting in states—grossness and subtlety—are conveyed by the words “existent” and “nonexistent.”

The apparently conflicting precreation sat/asat statements are reconciled by state-indexing, not by making sheer nothing produce something. The following section distinguishes pot, shards, powder and fine particles in a connected transformation account.

[Source witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-pl-g19).

Material and efficient causation must be typed separately. So must the powers through which transformation is attributed. A state-based account should preserve the bearer while allowing predicates and names to change with its condition. The author’s example supplies this structure. Nor should every use of asat be translated as absolute absence.

**Agency, self-directed awareness and dependence**

Baladeva’s argument for agency is tied to injunction, knowing, action and experienced result. It includes liberated play, then explains non-independence without reducing the individual to an inert implement. His account of self-disclosure gives the subject a role that generic illumination cannot fill.

*Govindabhāṣya, 2.3.31*

> śāstraṃ kila phala-hetutā-buddhim utpādya karmasu tat-phala-bhoktāraṃ puruṣaṃ pravartayate | na ca tad-buddhir jaḍānāṃ guṇānāṃ śakyotpādayitum

*Literal translation:* For scripture, producing an understanding of being a cause of the result, sets the person who experiences that result into action. And that understanding cannot be produced in the insentient qualities.

The injunction argument requires a conscious recipient who understands and acts for a result; moving agency entirely into insentient guṇas defeats the mechanism of instruction.

[Source witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-pl-g01).

*Govindabhāṣya, 2.3.32*

> sa tatra paryeti jakṣan krīḍan ramamāṇa [chā.u. 8.12.3] ity ādinā muktasyāpi krīḍābhidhānād ity arthaḥ | ataḥ kartṛtvam atra na duḥkhāvahaṃ, kintu guṇa-sambandham eva, tasya svarūpa-glāni-karatvāt

*Literal translation:* The meaning is: because play is stated even of the liberated one by “there he moves about, eating, playing, delighting” and so forth. Therefore agency here is not what brings suffering, but precisely connection with the qualities, because that impairs intrinsic nature.

The following argument cites play in liberation; suffering therefore cannot follow from agency as such. It follows from the guṇa connection that impairs intrinsic nature.

[Source witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-pl-g02).

*Govindabhāṣya, 2.3.34*

> evaṃ sati kvacid akartṛtva-vacanam asvātantryāt

*Literal translation:* This being so, a statement somewhere of non-agency is on account of lack of independence.

Baladeva expressly disambiguates non-agency language as non-independence in this setting. This supports a translation bridge, with a textual license, rather than a global rewriting of every non-agency statement.

[Source witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-pl-g03).

*Govindabhāṣya, 2.3.40*

> tasmāj jīvaḥ prayojya-kartā pareśas tu hetu-kartā tad-anumatim antarāsau kartuṃ na śaknotīti sarvam avadātam

*Literal translation:* Therefore the individual self is the agent who is impelled, while the supreme Lord is the causing agent; without His permission this one cannot act: thus everything is clear.

The prior sentence rejects treating the individual as wood or a clod. Divine enabling takes the individual’s performed effort into account, like rain producing different results through different seeds.

Baladeva explains the two agents' contributions in 2.3.40:

> jīvena kṛtaṃ dharmādharma-lakṣaṇaṃ prayatnam apekṣya pareśas taṃ kārayaty ato nokta-doṣāvatāraḥ

*Literal translation:* Taking into account the effort performed by the individual, characterized as right or wrong action, the supreme Lord causes him to act; therefore the stated fault does not arise.

His counterfactual in the same passage shows why the individual's contribution cannot disappear:

> yadi vidhau niṣedhe ca pareśa eva kāṣṭha-loṣṭra-tulyaṃ jīvaṃ niyuñjyāt tarhi tasya vākyasya prāmāṇyaṃ hīyeta kṛtimato niyojyatvāt

*Literal translation:* If, in injunction and prohibition, the supreme Lord alone were to impel an individual like wood or a clod, then the validity of that sentence would be lost, since one possessing effort is eligible to be enjoined.

[Source witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-pl-g04-support-2).

*Govindabhāṣya, 2.3.41*

> brahma khalu śaktimad ekaṃ vastu brahma-śaktir jīvo brahmaika-deśatvāt brahmāṃśo bhavatīti tad-upasṛṣṭatvaṃ sughaṭam

*Literal translation:* Brahman is indeed one entity possessed of power; the individual self is Brahman’s power. Because it is one portion of Brahman, it is a part of Brahman; thus its subordinate status is readily intelligible.

The context expressly rejects a chipped-off stone fragment and a māyā-delimited God. Part-language is grounded in the power–powerful unity while preserving governed/governor and dependent/independent roles.

[Source witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-pl-g05).

*Govindabhāṣya, 2.3.43*

> ātmā tu svayaṃ paraṃ ca prakāśayan svaṃ prati prakāśate | ataḥ svasmai svayaṃ prakāśaḥ yad asau cid-rūpa iti

*Literal translation:* The self, however, while illuminating itself and another, shines for itself. Therefore it is self-illuminating for itself, because it is consciousness in nature.

The lamp comparison is carefully limited: the lamp manifests but does not manifest for itself, being inert. The self’s own-directed disclosure is a substantive difference.

The lamp provides the contrasting case. Baladeva does not deny that it makes its own form manifest:

> dīpaś cakṣuḥ prakāśayan svarūpa-sphūrtiṃ ca svayam eva karoti, na tu ghaṭādi-prakāśavat tad-ādi-sāpekṣaḥ | […] tathāpi svaṃ prati na prakāśate svasmin jāḍyāt |

*Literal translation:* The lamp, illuminating the eye, itself also brings about the manifestation of its own form; unlike the manifestation of a pot and the rest, it is not dependent on that [eye] and the rest. […] Nevertheless it does not shine for itself, because of inertness in itself.

The omitted intervening short sentence begins tasmād yaṃ in this electronic witness; it is not conjecturally repaired. The two selected clauses are secure and preserve the actual lamp/self contrast.

[Source witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-pl-g06-support-1).

A single Agent label would omit the connection between epistemic uptake, motivation, action and result. Liberated play blocks the inference from agency to bondage or suffering. Intrinsic agency differs from causal independence; individual effort and divine direction are distinct but composable causal roles. Power-inclusion does not mean cutting off a material piece, and being manifest is not yet being disclosed to oneself. The lamp comparison makes the last distinction especially important for any theory of selfhood.

**A complete person can manifest powers selectively**

The account of aṃśa now turns to divine forms. Its examples distinguish class-membership from a shared label, and then distinguish possessed capacity from current exhibition. The same learned teacher can teach a limited selection without becoming a different or deficient knower.

*Govindabhāṣya, 2.3.45*

> sarva-śakti-vyakty-avyakti-savyapekṣo hi tat-tad-vyapadeśaḥ

*Literal translation:* For the respective designation depends upon the manifestation and nonmanifestation of all the powers.

The same Kṛṣṇa is called whole, part or fraction according to the powers expressed; Matsya and other divine forms are not distinct entities in the way jīvas are. The surrounding scholar-teaching-one-or-many-disciplines example concerns one possessor’s varied expression.

[Source witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-pl-g07).

*Govindabhāṣya, 2.3.47*

> pūrṇam adaḥ pūrṇam idam ity ādyā tu matsyādeḥ pūrtim

*Literal translation:* But [the śruti] beginning “that is full, this is full” [states] the fullness of Matsya and the others.

The same paragraph contrasts the individual self’s nonfullness with divine forms’ fullness. This is a positive school-specific application of the fullness mantra, not license to transfer all divine powers to every finite self.

[Source witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-pl-g08).

*Govindabhāṣya 2.3.44*

Baladeva’s own explanation of the sūtra, after raising whether Matsya-type portions differ from jīva-type portions.

> aṃśa-śabditatve’pi paro matsyādir na evaṃ jīvavan na bhavati | tatra dṛṣṭāntam āha prakāśeti | yathā tejo’ṃśo raviḥ khadyotaś ca tejaḥ-śabditatve’pi naikarūpya-bhāk | yathā jalāṃśaḥ sudhā-madyādiś ca jala-śabditatve’pi na sāmyaṃ labhate, tadvat ||44||

*Literal translation:* Although called a portion, the supreme Matsya and the rest are not thus—not like the jīva. Here he gives the example “like light.” Just as the sun and a firefly, portions of radiance, do not have the same character despite both being called radiance; and just as nectar, liquor and the rest, portions of liquid, do not attain equality despite both being called liquid—so it is.

The examples establish why a shared designation does not settle the nature of the referents. They prepare the positive account of divine own-forms in 45; they do not prove that avatāras are merely bigger jīvas.

The wording retains the repeated negative na evaṃ…na bhavati. “Portions of radiance/liquid” follows the commentator’s illustrative language and is not a modern physical explanation.

[Primary witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-df-g01).

*Govindabhāṣya 2.3.45, own explanatory paragraph*

Baladeva’s own explanatory reasoning, after an explicitly introduced smṛti quotation.

> na te jīvavat tato bhidyante, tasyaiva vaidūryādivat tat-tad-bhāvāviṣkārāt | sarva-śakti-vyakty-avyakti-savyapekṣo hi tat-tad-vyapadeśaḥ | yaḥ kṛṣṇaḥ kṛtsna-ṣāḍguṇya-vyañjako’ṃśī, sa evākṛtsna-tad-vyañjako dvy-eka-vyañjako vāṃśaḥ kalā cety ucyate | yathaikaḥ kṛtsna-ṣaṭ-śāstra-pravaktā sarvavid ucyate, sa eva kvacid akṛtsna-tad-vaktā dvy-eka-śāstra-vaktā ca sarvavit-kalpo’lpajñaś ceti |

*Literal translation:* They do not differ from him as the jīva does, because that very one reveals the respective forms, like a vaidūrya gem and the like. For the respective designation depends on the manifestation or nonmanifestation of all powers. That very Kṛṣṇa who, displaying all six divine excellences, is the possessor of portions, when not displaying all of them, or displaying two or one, is called a portion or a fraction. Just as one person who teaches all six disciplines is called all-knowing, that very person, somewhere teaching not all of them or teaching two or one, is called almost all-knowing or little-knowing.

The knowledgeable-teacher example is an actual source-given counterexample to an inference from limited exhibition to limited capacity. It clarifies the gem example: the designation responds to what is manifested, not to severing a piece from a material whole.

The six excellences and six teaching disciplines belong to the example’s traditional vocabulary. The paragraph does not by itself assign exact subsets to every avatāra. The source’s spelling savyapekṣa is retained.

[Primary witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-df-g02).

*Govindabhāṣya 2.3.45, concluding clause*

Baladeva’s own conclusion; excerpt begins inside a longer enumeration whose preceding mention of powers and qualities supplies the grammatical subject.

> sarvātiśayi-prema-pūrṇa-parikaratva-druhiṇādi-vidvattama-vismāpaka-vaṃśa-mādhurya-sva-paryanta-sarva-vismāpaka-rūpa-mādhurya-niratiśaya-kāruṇyādayo yaśodā-stanandhaye kṛṣṇa eva nityāvirbhūtāḥ santi, na tu matsyāditve satīti tasyaiva tat-tad-bhāvāviṣkārān na matsyāder jīvavat tattvāntaratvaṃ, kintu tad-ātmakatvam eveti ||45||

*Literal translation:* Having associates filled with love surpassing all; the sweetness of the flute that astonishes even the most learned, Brahmā and others; the sweetness of form that astonishes everyone, up to himself; unsurpassed compassion and the rest—these are eternally manifest in Kṛṣṇa alone, the suckling of Yaśodā, not in the condition of Matsya and the rest. Thus, because that very one reveals those respective forms, Matsya and the rest are not another reality as a jīva is, but have precisely his nature.

Fullness is not used to erase Kṛṣṇa’s distinctive flute, beauty, compassion or loving associates. Nor do those distinctions turn Matsya into an ontologically other jīva. A theory must preserve both propositions, rather than selecting only sameness or only difference.

The Sanskrit is a long descriptive compound; hyphens are editorial aid. It does not say that Matsya lacks divine power or is a defective God.

[Primary witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-df-g03).

*Govindabhāṣya 2.3.46–47, selected clauses*

Baladeva on two successive sūtras; explicit omission separates 46 from 47.

> naivaṃ matsyādi-rūpasya, kintu deha-sambandha-rāhityaṃ sākṣāt pareśatvaṃ ca tasya śrūyate, ato mahān viśeṣaḥ | […] jīvasyāsantater apūrṇatvād avyatikaraḥ | pūrṇena matsyādinā sāmyaṃ nety arthaḥ | bālāgra-śata-bhāgasya [śve.u. 5.9] ity ādyā śrutir jīvasyāpūrtim āha | pūrṇam adaḥ pūrṇam idam ity ādyā tu matsyādeḥ pūrtim ||47||

*Literal translation:* It is not so for the Matsya and other forms; rather, absence of association with a body and direct being-the-supreme-Lord are heard of it. Therefore there is a great distinction. […] There is no intermixture, because of the jīva’s nonextension, its incompleteness. The meaning is: there is no equality with complete Matsya and the rest. The revelation beginning “of the hundredth part of a hair-tip” declares the jīva’s incompleteness; that beginning “That is complete, this is complete” declares the completeness of Matsya and the rest.

The absence of deha-sambandha in 46 cannot mean absence of all visible form: its subject is precisely Matsya-type form. The prior clause concerns the jīva’s beginningless avidyā-expanded bodily association.47 then positively attributes fullness to the avatāra. This supports the refusal to treat concreteness itself as a deficiency.

Asantati is glossed by the author as apūrṇatva here; do not infer a general spatial definition of every self from the English “nonextension.” The quotations of Upaniṣads are Baladeva’s cited forms, not new independent editions of those Upaniṣads.

[Primary witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-df-g04).

Aṃśa cannot be represented by one untyped part-of relation. The divine own-form retains its bearer while the displayed power-profile changes; partial manifestation need not mean a deficient possessor. Yet Kṛṣṇa’s flute, beauty and loving associates remain specifically and permanently manifested in the account. Own-nature unity does not make all presentations qualitatively interchangeable. The distinction between intrinsic divine form and acquired bodily association also preserves Baladeva’s explicit restriction: fullness belongs to Matsya-type divine forms in this argument, not indiscriminately to every finite dependent self.

**Liberated capacities and the limit of cosmic governance**

The positive account of release preserves intrinsic qualities and genuine activity while distinguishing the finite participant’s freedom from universal governance.

*Govindabhāṣya, 4.4.1*

> jñāna-vairāgya-niṣevitayā bhaktyā paraṃ jyotir upasampannasya jīvasyeha karma-bandha-vinirmukta-guṇāṣṭaka-viśiṣṭa-svarūpodaya-lakṣaṇo’vasthāna-viśeṣaḥ svarūpāvirbhāvaḥ kathyate

*Literal translation:* Here a particular state of the individual self who has attained the highest light through devotion served by knowledge and dispassion, characterized by the arising of its intrinsic nature qualified by the eight qualities and freed from karmic bondage, is called manifestation of intrinsic nature.

The nature is prior; its unbound manifested state is newly attained. Practice is meaningful because it produces this state, not a replacement self.

[Source witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-pl-g09).

*Govindabhāṣya, 4.4.17*

> nikhila-cid-acit-sṛṣṭi-sthiti-niyamana-rūpaṃ brahmaikāntaṃ jagad-vyāpāraṃ vihāya bodhyam

*Literal translation:* [It] is to be understood excluding the world-operation exclusive to Brahman, consisting in the creation, maintenance and governance of all the conscious and nonconscious.

The liberated one’s capacities are real but do not include universal cosmic governance. Baladeva argues from the context and the definition of Brahman, not merely from a finite model’s chosen limitation.

[Source witness](cite://baladeva/govinda-bhasya/inspectable-pl-g10).

The transition changes obstruction and manifestation while preserving the bearer and intrinsic qualities. Liberated creativity therefore remains possible, but it does not entail universal governorship. A model of fulfillment must preserve this typed exception rather than infer every divine power from the removal of bondage.

**Yaśodā’s vision: the world within the child**

The Bhāgavata narrative gives the concrete case Jīva will interpret. The speaker changes matter: the narrator describes Kṛṣṇa and the vision; Yaśodā considers possible explanations, turns to surrender and criticizes her possessive understanding; the narrator then describes the restoration of maternal affection.

*Bhāgavatapurāṇa 10.8.36*

Narrator; the initial command is Yaśodā’s reported speech.

> yady evaṃ tarhi vyādehīty uktaḥ sa bhagavān hariḥ / vyādattāvyāhataiśvaryaḥ krīḍāmanujabālakaḥ //

*Literal translation:* Being told, “If so, then open [your mouth],” that Bhagavān Hari, whose sovereignty was unimpaired, who was a human child in play, opened [it].

The verse does not first remove the child-form and replace it with an unrelated infinite being. It keeps the child at play and describes his sovereignty as unimpaired. “In play” is not itself a claim that the form is unreal.

The bracketed mouth comes from the preceding conversation 35.

[Primary witness](cite://bhagavata/bhagavata-purana/inspectable-df-bp01).

*Bhāgavatapurāṇa 10.8.37–39*

Narrator describing Yaśodā’s vision.

> sā tatra dadṛśe viśvaṃ jagat sthāsnu ca khaṃ diśaḥ / sādridvīpābdhibhūgolaṃ savāyvagnīndutārakam // jyotiścakraṃ jalaṃ tejo nabhasvān viyad eva ca / vaikārikāṇīndriyāṇi mano mātrā guṇās trayaḥ // etad vicitraṃ sahajīvakālasvabhāvakarmāśayaliṅgabhedam / sūnos tanau vīkṣya vidāritāsye vrajaṃ sahātmānam avāpa śaṅkām //

*Literal translation:* She saw there the whole—the moving and the stationary, space, the directions, the earth-globe with mountains, islands and oceans, with wind, fire, moon and stars; the circle of luminaries, water, radiance, wind and ether, the transformed senses, mind, subtle measures and the three qualities. Seeing this manifold, together with living beings, time, nature, action, dispositions and differences of subtle bodies, in her son’s body, in the opened mouth, and Vraja together with herself, she came to doubt.

The detail vrajaṃ sahātmānam matters. The vision does not merely show a large collection somewhere inside a smaller container: it includes the mother and village from which she is viewing it. Jīva will use this mutual inclusion to challenge ordinary outside/inside confinement.

The middle verse is an enumerative construction with mixed case forms in the transmitted Sanskrit. The long 39 compound permits interpretive segmentation; the translation keeps its enumerated terms rather than inventing one causal mechanism. “Subtle bodies” renders liṅga contextually.

[Primary witness](cite://bhagavata/bhagavata-purana/inspectable-df-bp02).

*Bhāgavatapurāṇa 10.8.40*

Yaśodā’s deliberation; alternatives are not all asserted as the narrator’s conclusion.

> kiṃ svapna etad uta devamāyā kiṃ vā madīyo bata buddhimohaḥ / atho amuṣyaiva mamārbhakasya yaḥ kaścan autpattika ātmayogaḥ //

*Literal translation:* Is this a dream, or divine māyā? Or, alas, a bewilderment of my own understanding? Or is it some innate power of this very child of mine?

Jīva’s commentary must be read as selecting and arguing for an explanation, not as simply repeating a root sentence that already labels every alternative false. The final possibility is the child’s innate power.

Ātmayoga is rendered “own/innate power” in this context, supported by autpattika and Jīva’s subsequent gloss; it is not a general definition of all uses of yoga.

[Primary witness](cite://bhagavata/bhagavata-purana/inspectable-df-bp03).

Before the narrator calls Yaśodā one who had understood the reality, her own deliberation turns to surrender and questions possessive household understanding. In 10.8.41d she says:

> sudurvibhāvyaṃ praṇatāsmi tat-padam

*Literal translation:* I have bowed to that station, very hard to conceive.

[Primary witness,10.8.41](https://vedabase.io/en/library/sb/10/8/41/).

Her next verse makes the particular understanding under criticism explicit:

> ahaṃ mamāsau patir eṣa me suto / vrajeśvarasyākhila-vittapā satī / gopyaś ca gopāḥ saha-godhanāś ca me / yan-māyayetthaṃ kumatiḥ sa me gatiḥ //

*Literal translation:* “I; that one is my husband; this is my son; [I am] the guardian of all the wealth of the lord of Vraja; the cowherd women and cowherd men, together with the cattle, are mine”—he through whose māyā [there is] this wrong understanding [of mine] is my refuge.

[Primary witness,10.8.42](https://vedabase.io/en/library/sb/10/8/42/).

This is Yaśodā’s critical reflection, not a timeless narrator’s proposition that every family relationship is simply unreal. The sequence matters: cosmic vision, unsettled alternatives, surrender and criticism of I/mine appropriation precede the Lord’s restoration of maternal affection. The later return to personal love therefore cannot be described as though this intervening understanding never occurred.

*Bhāgavatapurāṇa 10.8.43–45*

The narrator continues:

> itthaṃ viditatattvāyāṃ gopikāyāṃ sa īśvaraḥ / vaiṣṇavīṃ vyatanon māyāṃ putrasnehamayīṃ vibhuḥ // sadyo naṣṭasmṛtir gopī sāropyāroham ātmajam / pravṛddhasnehakalilahṛdayāsīd yathā purā // trayyā copaniṣadbhiś ca sāṅkhyayogaiś ca sātvataiḥ / upagīyamānamāhātmyaṃ hariṃ sāmanyatātmajam //

*Literal translation:* When the cowherd woman had thus known the truth, that mighty Lord extended Vaiṣṇava māyā consisting of affection for a son. Immediately losing the memory, she, the cowherd woman, placed her son on her lap and became, as before, one whose heart was filled with increased affection. Hari, whose greatness is sung by the threefold Veda, the Upaniṣads, Sāṅkhya and Yoga, and the Sātvatas, she regarded as her son.

This is a further obstacle to a simple monotonic model in which greater explicit cosmic information is always the sole measure of realization. The text names knowledge of truth, divinely extended affection, loss of that episode’s memory, and intensified mother-child relation. These are distinct events and predicates.

The supplied phrase “that episode’s memory” in the interpretation is contextual; smṛti in 44 is unqualified. The root 43 says Vaiṣṇava māyā; the sharper Yoga-māyā/Mahā-māyā taxonomy belongs to commentary and is not inserted as root wording. Sātvata may designate the relevant teachers/tradition/texts; the literal translation retains the term.

[Primary witness](cite://bhagavata/bhagavata-purana/inspectable-df-bp04).

The sequence moves from cosmic disclosure through the mother’s explicit reflection to renewed maternal affection. Jīva now develops an explanation of how form and pervasion coexist.

**Jīva: the error is confinement, not the child’s form**

Jīva reads the concrete narrative as evidence against the inference from a determinate appearance to confinement within the world. His argument concerns simultaneous inclusion and continuing divine form; it does not treat every example of reflection or appearance in every context alike.

*Bhagavat-sandarbha old GRETIL§35 / current GGM 115§34, on BhP 10.14.16*

Jīva’s own explanation after quoting Brahmā’s BhP 10.14.16. The preceding paragraph reports another interpretation with vyākurvanti; that alternative is not fused with this explanation.

> atraiva tāvat śrī-kṛṣṇākhye māyopaśamane’vatāre prādurbhāve, bahiś cāntar-jaṭhare ca sphuṭasya dṛṣṭasya kṛtsnasya jagataḥ sambandhe pūrvoktaṃ yan māyātvam, prapañca-kṛta-tvat-paricchedyatvasya mithyātvam | taj-jananyā jananyai te tvayā prakaṭīkṛtaṃ darśitam | tasmād bhavān jagad-antaḥstha eva, jagat tu bhavad-bahir-bhūtam ity evaṃ māyā-dharmaḥ | vastutas tu durvitarkya-svarūpa-śaktyā madhyamatve’pi vyāpako’sīti bhāvaḥ |

*Literal translation:* Here itself, in the appearance called Śrī Kṛṣṇa that stills māyā, regarding the whole world clearly seen both outside and within the belly, the māyā-character mentioned before is the falsity of your being delimited by the manifold. That was disclosed—shown—by you to the mother. Thus, “You are only within the world, while the world is outside you”—such is a property of māyā. But in reality, through the intrinsic power difficult to reason out, you are pervasive even with middling size: this is the purport.

The predicate “false” is not carelessly attached to the visible divine body or to every distinction in the cosmos. Jīva explicitly identifies the target: treating the world as an external boundary enclosing Kṛṣṇa. His positive conclusion retains madhyamatva, determinate intermediate size, while affirming vyāpakatva.

Madhyamatva here contrasts a determinate perceptible magnitude with all-pervasiveness; it does not supply a numerical measurement. “Difficult to reason out” retains durvitarkya, without using it to authorize every arbitrary contradiction.

[Primary witness](cite://jiva/bhagavat-sandarbha/inspectable-df-j01).

*Bhagavat-sandarbha old GRETIL§36 / current GGM 115§35, on BhP 10.14.17 citing 10.8.40*

Jīva’s explanatory prose; the first-person “I am” is his formulation of what Bhagavān disclosed, not an additional quotation in the Bhāgavata root.

> svasya vraje’ntar-bhūtatā-darśanenaiva samaṃ vrajasya svasminn antar-bhūtatāṃ darśayan, tac cāntar-bahir-darśanaṃ kiṃ svapna etad uta deva-māyā [bhā.pu. 10.8.40] ity-ādau śrī-jananyā eva vicāre svāpnikatva-māyikatva-bimba-pratibimbatvānām ayogyatvād ekam evety abhijñāpayan, kiṃ svapna ity-ādāv eva, yaḥ kaścana autpattika ātma-yogaḥ [bhā.pu. 10.8.40] ity anena carama-pakṣāvasitayā durvitarkya-svarūpa-śaktyaiva madhyama-parimāṇa-viśeṣa eva sarva-vyāpako’smīti svayam eva bhagavān jananīṃ prati yugapad ubhayātmakaṃ nija-dharma-viśeṣaṃ darśitavān |

*Literal translation:* Showing Vraja’s being within himself simultaneously with the display of his own being within Vraja, and making known that this inside-and-outside vision is one and the same—because dreamhood, illusionhood and original-and-reflection status are unsuitable in the mother’s own deliberation beginning “Is this a dream, or divine māyā?”—Bhagavān himself showed the mother his own particular property as having both characters simultaneously: “Precisely as a particular middling magnitude, I am all-pervading,” through the intrinsic power difficult to reason out, settled upon as the last alternative by “some innate power” in that same verse.

The exclusion of bimba-pratibimba here is consequential. The same visual vocabulary used elsewhere for dependence or reflection cannot automatically explain this scene. The mother and Vraja are present in the vision while the child is present in Vraja; Jīva takes the simultaneity and the final innate-power alternative to reveal a single divine property with both aspects.

This argument does not establish that every human or every god has this joint property. The clauses denying dream/illusion/reflection are Jīva’s interpretation of the episode;10.8.40 itself asks questions.

[Primary witness](cite://jiva/bhagavat-sandarbha/inspectable-df-j02).

*Bhagavat-sandarbha old GRETIL§37 / current GGM 115§36, on BhP 10.14.18*

Jīva commenting on Brahmā’s description of the multiplied friends/calves and four-armed manifestations; not the narrator’s bare 10.8 wording.

> vraja-suhṛd-ādi-rūpaṃ yad yasmād āvirbhūtaṃ, tat tad akhilam adhunā tirodhāna-samaye yena punar anena śrī-vigraha-rūpeṇāvaśiṣyate, tad advayaṃ brahmaivety arthaḥ | aśeṣa-prāpañcikāprāpañcika-vastūnāṃ prādurbhāva-sthiti-tirobhāva-darśanena tal-lakṣaṇākrāntatvād iti bhāvaḥ | tataś cāsya brahmatve siddhe vyāpakatvam api sidhyatīti tātparyam |

*Literal translation:* That from which the forms of Vraja’s friends and the rest appeared, and by which all of that, at the time of disappearance, remains again as this divine form—that is precisely nondual Brahman: this is the meaning. The purport is that it falls under that characterization through the observed appearance, persistence and disappearance of all worldly and nonworldly things. Consequently, when its being Brahman is established, its pervasiveness too is established: this is the intention.

This is a connected positive inference: appearance, continued existence and withdrawal identify the divine form as their source/remaining reality; that establishes Brahmanhood, from which pervasiveness is inferred. The conclusion is not that Brahmanhood requires the final disappearance of form.

The current GGM reading advayam is clear; older held GRETIL has tad dvayam, a consequential dropped-a defect in context. The present edition also supplies prāpañcikāprāpañcika, where the older file lacks the second qualification. The quotation follows the current witness.

[Primary witness](cite://jiva/bhagavat-sandarbha/inspectable-df-j03).

A faithful representation needs to distinguish displayed dimensions from external ontological confinement. The child’s presence in Vraja and Vraja’s presence in the child cannot be explained here as ordinary containment between external finite objects. Appearance, persistence and withdrawal also concern the disclosure of the divine form rather than the production and destruction of an ordinary body. These claims give a positive account of concrete fullness without making every observer’s perception exhaustive.

**Own-aware fulfillment and reciprocal love**

Jīva’s account of ordered disclosure reaches its practical force in liberation and love. He retains an experiencer who knows fulfillment as his own and rejects several proposed reductions of the love enjoyed by God and devotees.

*Prītisandarbha, §1*

> tac ca paramaṃ tattvaṃ dvidhāvirbhavati | aspaṣṭa-viśeṣatvena spaṣṭa-svarūpa-bhūta-viśeṣatvena ca

*Literal translation:* And that supreme reality manifests in two ways: with indistinct differentiations, and with clear differentiations belonging to its intrinsic nature.

Jīva treats Brahman-disclosure as disclosure of the same supreme reality, but does not regard indistinct and distinctly qualified realization as equal in fullness. The argument then gives loving disclosure the highest place.

[Source witness](cite://jiva/priti-sandarbha/inspectable-pl-g11).

*Prītisandarbha, §5*

> tad evaṃ tasya raśmi-paramāṇu-sthānīyāṃśatve siddhe tadvat sarvasyām api daśāyāṃ kartṛtva-bhoktṛtvādi-svarūpa-dharmā api sidhyanti

*Literal translation:* Thus, when its status as a part occupying the position of a ray-particle has been established, likewise intrinsic properties such as agency and experiencership are established in every state.

Part-language sustains enduring properties; the next sentence makes their operative efficacy depend on divine power. Natural embodiment and spiritual experience involve different power-assistance.

[Source witness](cite://jiva/priti-sandarbha/inspectable-pl-g12).

*Prītisandarbha, §5*

> na ca "sukham ahaṃ syām" iti kasyacid icchā, kintu "sukham aham anubhavāmi" ity eva

*Literal translation:* Nor is anyone’s desire “may I be happiness”; rather it is precisely “I experience happiness.”

Jīva argues that undisclosed happiness could not function as a human end or motivate scripture-directed practice. The current GGM witness reads aham; the earlier electronic ahan is recorded as a corrected transcription defect.

[Source witness](cite://jiva/priti-sandarbha/inspectable-pl-g13).

*Prītisandarbha, §65*

> tasyā hlādinyā eva kāpi sarvānandātiśāyinī vṛttir nityaṃ bhakta-vṛndeṣv eva nikṣipyamāṇā bhagavat-prīty-ākhyayā vartate

*Literal translation:* A certain operation of that very hlādinī, surpassing all bliss, continually deposited precisely in the company of devotees, exists under the name love of Bhagavān.

The preceding objections reject material sattva-bliss, undifferentiated divine bliss and the individual’s small intrinsic bliss as sufficient explanations of love’s intensification. A special operation of divine intrinsic power supplies the explanation.

Jīva first eliminates three explanations of love (Prītisandarbha 65). The accounts he attributes to others remain reports within his argument:

> na tāvat sāṅkhyānām iva prākṛta-sattva-maya-māyikānanda-rūpā | bhagavato māyānabhibhāvyatva-śruteḥ svatas-tṛptatvāc ca | na ca nirviśeṣa-vādinām iva bhagavat-svarūpānanda-rūpā, atiśayānupapatteḥ | ato natarāṃ jīvasya svarūpānanda-rūpā, atyanta-kṣudratvāt tasya

*Literal translation:* First, it is not, as [on the view] of the Sāṅkhyas, in the form of māyā-made bliss consisting of material sattva, because scripture declares that the Lord cannot be overcome by māyā, and because He is satisfied in Himself. Nor is it, as [on the view] of the advocates of the undifferentiated, in the form of the bliss of the Lord’s intrinsic nature, because an intensification would be inexplicable. Still less, therefore, is it in the form of the individual self’s intrinsic bliss, because that is exceedingly small.

He then infers the special operation described in his conclusion (Prītisandarbha 65). The bracketed subject in the English is supplied from that conclusion:

> atha tasyā api bhagavati sadaiva vartamānatayātiśayānupapattes tv evaṃ vivecanīyam | śrutārthānyathānupapatty-arthāpatti-pramāṇa-siddhatvāt

*Literal translation:* Now, because even that [hlādinī] is always present in the Lord, intensification would be inexplicable; rather it is to be discriminated in this way, because [the special operation] is established by the means of knowledge called postulation, through the impossibility of explaining the meaning heard in scripture otherwise.

[Source witness](cite://jiva/priti-sandarbha/inspectable-pl-g14-support-2).

*Prītisandarbha, §65*

> atas tad-anubhavena śrī- bhagavān api śrīmad-bhakteṣu prīty-atiśayaṃ bhajata iti

*Literal translation:* Therefore, through experiencing that, the Lord too attains an intensification of love toward the blessed devotees.

The action is reciprocal: divine love responds to the devotee’s divinely sustained love. Section 66 develops mutual being-under-another’s-sway; this is not loss of ontological divine status.

[Source witness](cite://jiva/priti-sandarbha/inspectable-pl-g15).

One reality can have ordered disclosures without every profile becoming interchangeable. Enduring capacity must be distinguished from condition-specific exercise, and experienced bliss must retain the experiencer rather than becoming a bare predicate. The account of love requires a particular operation of intrinsic power with reciprocal effects; generic bliss or material emotion would omit the reasons Jīva gives. Affective responsiveness is consequently not identical to ontological dependence or bondage.

### What a faithful common account must preserve

A common first language can distinguish a bearer, its configurations, its powers, its manifestations, an experiencing subject and the result of a cognition. That is already more demanding than a collection of true/false labels. The selected passages require maps that say which of these structures they preserve. They also identify transitions: a pot configuration becomes available; a cloth is unfolded; a power begins to operate; an obstruction is removed; a referent is more fully understood; love intensifies. The transitions do not all do the same work.

A proposed category of configurations can therefore take its objects to be a bearer together with a determinate state, and its arrows to be licensed changes. Composition must mean that a sequence of licensed changes is again intelligible for the same bearer. The mapping to a bearer can forget a configuration while preserving continuity. This is an intelligible candidate for the clay and Caitra arguments. It is not yet a theorem that every school has this category, nor that every possible configuration is accessible from every other. Śrīnivāsa’s determinate-production argument specifically forbids arbitrary transitions. Vallabha’s cloth/breath distinction requires separate observational and operative coordinates. Baladeva’s power distinction requires a map to preserve which power undergoes the relevant manifestation.

The self cases demand a richer fiber than ordinary material states. Rāmānuja’s common knowledge-nature does not erase intrinsic self-distinction. Jayatīrtha’s test is actual self-recognition at some time, not equality of a list of outward qualities. Baladeva’s self is disclosed for itself, unlike a lamp. Thus a forgetful map that retains only “conscious” can compare an aspect of these accounts, but it cannot recover their self theories. A faithful interpretation must preserve the relations used in their own arguments, including the difference between owning an episode and recognizing a subject as oneself.

The reference construction is another kind of map. For Rāmānuja, the local qualifying ground and the completed denotation have different roles. A word’s designation can reach the indweller without making all properties of the locally named mode properties of the indweller in the same respect. Śrīnivāsa gives a related inner-Self inference, while also affirming broad all-word denotability. The common selected naming proof should remain a fragment of these accounts. It must not manufacture a historical disagreement between universal and merely local reference by mistaking the reconstruction’s smaller signature for an author’s limitation.

There are positive overlaps worth constructing. Real dependent agency is compatible with non-independence in Madhva and Baladeva. Intrinsic nature can remain unproduced while its unobstructed manifestation becomes newly actual in several accounts. A change in the operative or disclosed profile need not mean destruction of its bearer. But these shared structures leave important questions open: whether all subjects have one ultimate intrinsic identity; which powers belong to the finite participant; whether realization removes difference or only obstruction; whether knowing the source yields exhaustive information or the relevant fruits of knowing subordinate objects. Those questions determine whether the proposed functors preserve the actual arguments.

These arguments support constructing and comparing richer fragments. A counterexample should target an author’s inference: a pot dependent on a potter refutes the inference from generic dependence to identity; a never-bound dependent Śrī refutes the inference from dependence to bondage; a folded cloth refutes the inference from nonmanifestation to nonexistence; liberated play refutes the inference from agency to suffering. Each is constructive evidence about what a successor theory must be able to express.

The divine-form arguments add a further distinction. Determinate displayed shape need not imply external confinement, intrinsic divine form need not be an acquired body, and a limited exhibition need not mean limited capacity. Jīva’s simultaneity argument, Baladeva’s continuing teacher and Madhva’s own-form classification each give a positive warrant. Their scopes still differ: the accounts retain distinctions between divine forms and dependent selves, between particular manifest qualities and a common bearer, and between cosmic knowledge and personal love. A useful common construction must preserve these differences in its maps rather than abolish them in its initial vocabulary.

A river remaining itself through lawful change is a useful modern question to bring to these texts. It is not a quotation from the passages on divine manifestation. Their own controls are the changing configuration, unfolding cloth, activity of breath, continuing learned teacher, appearance and withdrawal of form, and the distinction between owning an experience and recognizing a subject as oneself. Those examples specify which kind of continuity has to be explained.

### Textual qualifications that affect the argument

The following witness differences affect the argument. In Bhāskara 2.1.13 the selected ocean comparison is secure, but its difficult concluding typography is excluded from quotation; the disputed reading should not decide the argument. The selected 2.1.14 state-of-cause clause is likewise not expanded by guessing the dense continuation. The positive-bliss conclusion retains *anumeyam*, “to be inferred”; *eva* does not exclude every other knower. Vallabha’s Aṇubhāṣya citations follow the inspected printed pages, not noisy OCR. The nearby variants āpadyate/āpadyeta and naitāvatā/naiva tāvatā do not change the selected premises.

Jīva’s Prītisandarbha quotation follows the current GGM 119 reading *aham*, correcting the older electronic *ahan* before *anubhavāmi*. For Bhagavat-sandarbha old GRETIL§§35–37/current GGM 115§§34–36, the current GGM 115 witness reads *māyikatva*, *bhagavān*, *tad advayaṃ brahma* and *prāpañcikāprāpañcika*, where the older electronic copy respectively had malformed *māyolatva*, *bhavān*, *tad dvayaṃ brahma* and merely *prāpañcika*. The difference between *advayam* and *dvayam* is plainly consequential. The quotation follows GGM 115 Version 3.01 (2016.06.23); its header records updating with Puridas’s 1951 Bhagavat-sandarbha and 1953 Sarva-saṃvādinī editions and their variants, alongside the Chatterjee 1972, HaridasShastri 1983 and Adhikari 1999 sources. This identifies the electronic witness’s editorial basis.

Madhva/Jayatīrtha’s cited *ete svāṃśakalāḥ* is retained as their witnessed quotation, rather than silently substituted for the commonly printed Bhāgavata 1.3.28 *ete cāṃśakalāḥ*. The malformed electronic *svaguṇaśatmakam* in the Anuvyākhyāna passage is replaced by the directly witnessed Devanagari *svaguṇātmakam*, supported also by Jayatīrtha’s immediate analysis of *sva* and *guṇātmaka*. Its numbering differs between the two electronic presentations. The Tarkatāṇḍava excerpts likewise keep Vyāsatīrtha’s root argument distinct from Rāghavendra’s interleaved Nyāyadīpa.

## 11. Reflexivity, contraction and complete apprehension in Trika

The preceding accounts distinguish powers from their exercise and complete reality from finite apprehension. Trika argues for reflexivity and intrinsic power, then explains contraction, recognition and complete apprehension. Its mirror arguments also specify what an image does not transfer to its receiver.

### Power, manifestation and devotion

Trika, Śākta and modern nondual accounts distinguish consciousness, its power, the conditions of finite awareness and the recognition of what one is. Their agreements do not erase their differences. Memory, reflection, complete apprehension, divine manifestation and fulfilled devotion each supply a different argument about how an abiding reality relates to determinate experience.

Sanskrit and Bengali quotations are followed by literal English renderings. Source-script passages also include IAST or romanized Bengali. English-language passages retain their authors’ original wording. Ramakrishna’s Bengali is M.’s recorded speech, not an autograph treatise or an audio transcription; its romanization is a readable convention rather than a reversible graphemic edition. Root texts, commentaries, interlocutors and modern interpretations are identified separately.

### Owned experience, reflexivity and power

Utpaladeva begins with the ownership of experience. Remembering “I experienced” is more than recovering an object-like content. His subsequent arguments distinguish reflexive consciousness from an insentient image-bearing crystal and then identify its intrinsic independence. These are successive claims: an account that accepts self-luminosity need not yet accept the further account of agency.

Utpaladeva, **Īśvarapratyabhijñākārikā 1.3.6**. [Source](cite://utpaladeva/isvara-pratyabhijna-karika/inspectable-sakti-context-01).

> evam anyonyabhinnānām aparasparavedinām
> jñānānām anusaṃdhānajanmā naśyej janasthitiḥ // 1.3.6 //

Literal rendering: Thus, for cognitions distinct from one another and not knowing one another, the continuance of human life, born of their connection, would perish.

The conditional is completed by the following verse.

Utpaladeva, **Īśvarapratyabhijñākārikā 1.3.7**. [Source](cite://utpaladeva/isvara-pratyabhijna-karika/inspectable-sakti-context-02).

> na ced antaḥkṛtānantaviśvarūpo maheśvaraḥ
> syād ekaś cidvapur jñānasmṛtyapohanaśaktimān // 1.3.7 //

Literal rendering: If there were not one Great Lord, whose body is consciousness, containing within himself the forms of the infinite universe, possessed of the powers of knowledge, memory and exclusion.

**Īśvarapratyabhijñākārikā 1.4.4.** Utpaladeva. [Source](cite://utpaladeva/isvara-pratyabhijna-karika/inspectable-sm-memory-unity).

> naiva hy anubhavo bhāti smṛtau pūrvo 'rthavat pṛthak  prāg anvabhūvam aham ity ātmārohaṇabhāsanāt

Literal rendering: For the earlier experience does not appear separately in memory as an object does, because it appears as resting on oneself: “I experienced previously.”

The conditional argument in 1.3.6–7 denies that mutually inaccessible cognitions can sustain connected practice. Remembering an experience as one’s own requires more than another cognition with matching content. Continuity within one subject’s experience does not by itself prove the identity of all subjects.

**Īśvarapratyabhijñākārikā 1.5.11.** Utpaladeva. [Source](cite://utpaladeva/isvara-pratyabhijna-karika/inspectable-sm-reflexivity-crystal).

> svabhāvam avabhāsasya vimarśaṃ vidur anyathā  prakāśo 'rthoparakto 'pi sphaṭikādijaḍopamaḥ

Literal rendering: They know reflexive apprehension as the nature of manifestation; otherwise illumination, though colored by an object, would be like an insentient crystal and the like.

Utpaladeva’s own Vṛtti, **Own Vṛtti on 1.5.11**. [Source](cite://utpaladeva/isvara-pratyabhijna-vrtti/inspectable-sakti-context-03).

> prakāśasya mukhya ātmā pratyavamarśaḥ, taṃ vinā arthabheditākārasyāpy asya svacchatāmātraṃ na tv ajāḍyaṃ camatkṛter abhāvāt

Literal rendering: The principal nature of illumination is reflexive apprehension. Without it, even when its form is differentiated by objects, there is mere clarity, but no non-inertness, because savoring is absent.

Utpaladeva, **Īśvarapratyabhijñākārikā 1.5.12**. [Source](cite://utpaladeva/isvara-pratyabhijna-karika/inspectable-sakti-context-59).

> ātmāta eva caitanyaṃ citkriyā citikartṛtā
> tātparyeṇoditas tena jaḍāt sa hi vilakṣaṇaḥ // 1.5.12 //

Literal rendering: Precisely for this reason the self is spoken of, with that intention, as consciousness, the activity of consciousness, and agency in consciousness; for by that it is distinct from the inert.

The Vṛtti distinguishes conscious savoring from mere clarity; the following verse explicitly adds agency. Utpaladeva’s premise is that conscious manifestation is reflexive. His further identification of reflexivity with agency requires its own argument; the inert-crystal comparison alone does not refute an account of an inactive, self-luminous witness.

**Īśvarapratyabhijñākārikā 1.5.13, with own Vṛtti on 1.5.13 and 1.5.15.** Utpaladeva. [Source](cite://utpaladeva/isvara-pratyabhijna-karika/inspectable-sm-reflexivity-freedom).

> citiḥ pratyavamarśātmā parā vāk svarasoditā  svātantryam etan mukhyaṃ tad aiśvaryaṃ paramātmanaḥ

Literal rendering: Consciousness, whose nature is reflexive apprehension, is supreme speech arisen of its own impulse. This is independence in the primary sense; that is the sovereignty of the highest Self.

Utpaladeva’s own Vṛtti, **Own Vṛtti on 1.5.13**. [Source](cite://utpaladeva/isvara-pratyabhijna-vrtti/inspectable-sakti-context-04).

> bhāvāntarānapekṣaṃ śuddham etat svātantryam aiśvaryasaṃjñam

Literal rendering: This pure independence, called sovereignty, does not depend upon another entity.

Utpaladeva’s own Vṛtti, **Own Vṛtti on 1.5.15**. [Source](cite://utpaladeva/isvara-pratyabhijna-vrtti/inspectable-sakti-context-05).

> etādṛśaśuddhasvātantryavaśān naiṣa pṛthag eva labdhapratiṣṭhaṃ vastv avaiti, api tv ajñeyam ātmānam aniyantritaprabhāvatayā jñeyīkaroti / bhinnajñeyasāpekṣatve jñānakartṛtā mlāyet

Literal rendering: Through such pure independence, this [subject] does not know an entity established separately; rather, through unrestricted power, it makes its own self, which is not an object of knowledge, an object of knowledge. If it depended upon a distinct knowable, its agency in knowing would wither.

Utpaladeva’s own Vṛtti says independence does not await another thing; 15 rejects dependence on a separately established knowable. The independence asserted here concerns consciousness’s intrinsic operation, which must be distinguished from particular conditioned acts. It is a substantive account of consciousness, not a consequence of the word’s grammatical form or of luminosity alone.

**Īśvarapratyabhijñākārikā 1.5.7, with objection and reply from 1.5.4,6 and own Vṛtti 9.** Utpaladeva. [Source](https://gretil.sub.uni-goettingen.de/gretil/corpustei/sa_utpaladeva-IzvarapratyabhijJAkArikA-with-vRtti.xml).

Utpaladeva stating the objection, **Īśvarapratyabhijñākārikā 1.5.4, objection**. [Source](cite://utpaladeva/isvara-pratyabhijna-karika/inspectable-sakti-context-58).

> tattadākasmikābhāso bāhyaṃ ced anumāpayet
> na hy abhinnasya bodhasya vicitrābhāsahetutā // 1.5.4 //

Literal rendering: If [one objects that] the unforeseen appearance of each [object] leads to inference of an external [thing]—for undifferentiated awareness cannot be the cause of varied appearances—

The conditional objection is answered by the displayed 1.5.6 and own-Vṛtti 1.5.9 passages, not endorsed as the author’s conclusion.

Utpaladeva, **Īśvarapratyabhijñākārikā 1.5.6**. [Source](cite://utpaladeva/isvara-pratyabhijna-karika/inspectable-sakti-context-06).

> syād etad avabhāseṣu teṣv evāvasite sati
> vyavahāre kim anyena bāhyenānupapattinā // 1.5.6 //

Literal rendering: This might be so; [but] when practical dealings are completed in those very appearances, what use is another, external [thing], which cannot be established?

The opening concedes the preceding proposal for argument; the question challenges the need for its external cause.

> cidātmaiva hi devo 'ntaḥsthitam icchāvaśād bahiḥ  yogīva nirupādānam arthajātaṃ prakāśayet

Literal rendering: For the God whose nature is consciousness itself would manifest outwardly, by will, the multitude of objects situated within, like a yogin, without a material cause.

Utpaladeva’s own Vṛtti, **Own Vṛtti on 1.5.9**. [Source](cite://utpaladeva/isvara-pratyabhijna-vrtti/inspectable-sakti-context-07).

> ghaṭādyābhāsād bāhyasyānupapatter nāsīd ābhāsaḥ, tatas tatra nānumānād api siddhiḥ

Literal rendering: Because [an entity] external to the appearance of a pot and the like cannot be established, there has been no appearance [of it]; therefore it is not established even by inference.

The objection proposes an external cause; the reply questions its need and independent establishment. Universal manifestation requires the additional premises of intrinsic power and no independently established outside. Here nirupādānam denies an additional material ingredient, not every explanatory condition.

### Contraction and stable recognition

Kṣemarāja explains the restricted knower and mind through consciousness’s contraction, rather than placing an independently constituted observer outside it. Restricted illumination nevertheless differs from full recognition. Living release permits cognized contents, while repeated recognition after absorption supplies a practice for stabilization. Spanda and the Vijñānabhairava then clarify what persists when outward effort or determinate representation is set aside.

**Pratyabhijñāhṛdaya 1.** Kṣemarāja. [Source](cite://ksemaraja/pratyabhijna-hrdayam/inspectable-sm-citi-free).

> citiḥ svatantrā viśvasiddhihetuḥ || 1 ||

Literal rendering: Consciousness is independent, the cause of the establishment of the universe.

Kṣemarāja’s commentary, **Pratyabhijñāhṛdaya commentary on 1**. [Source](cite://ksemaraja/pratyabhijna-hrdayam/inspectable-sakti-context-08).

> viśvasya sadāśivādeḥ bhūmyantasya siddhau niṣpattau prakāśane sthityātmani parapramātṛviśrāntyātmani ca saṃhāre parāśaktirūpā citir eva bhagavatī svatantrā anuttaravimarśamayī śivabhaṭṭārakābhinnā hetuḥ kāraṇam

Literal rendering: In the establishment of the universe, from Sadāśiva to earth—in production, in manifestation consisting in continuance, and in withdrawal consisting in repose in the supreme knower—the cause is the Goddess consciousness alone: independent, consisting of unsurpassed reflexive apprehension, having the form of supreme power, nondifferent from Lord Śiva.

Kṣemarāja’s commentary, **Pratyabhijñāhṛdaya commentary on 1**. [Source](cite://ksemaraja/pratyabhijna-hrdayam/inspectable-sakti-context-09).

> anyasya tu māyāprakṛtyādeḥ citprakāśabhinnasya aprakāśamānatvena asattvān na kvacid api hetutvam prakāśamānatve tu prakāśaikātmyāt prakāśarūpā citir eva hetuḥ na tv asau kaścit

Literal rendering: But another [principle], such as māyā or primordial nature, distinct from the illumination of consciousness, is nowhere a cause, since, not appearing, it does not exist. If it appears, because of its identity with illumination, consciousness whose form is illumination alone is the cause, not some such other [principle].

The commentary distinguishes production, manifestation and reabsorption, and excludes an independently established outside. Grammatical gender does not assign an independent or dependent metaphysical role. Kṣemarāja’s assertion should not be generalized into a doctrine shared by every Śākta or Sāṅkhya author.

**Pratyabhijñāhṛdaya 2.** Kṣemarāja. [Source](cite://ksemaraja/pratyabhijna-hrdayam/inspectable-sm-world-self-surface).

> svecchayā svabhittau viśvam unmīlayati || 2 ||

Literal rendering: By her own will she unfolds the universe on her own surface.

Kṣemarāja’s commentary, **Pratyabhijñāhṛdaya commentary on 2**. [Source](cite://ksemaraja/pratyabhijna-hrdayam/inspectable-sakti-context-10).

> svabhittau na tu anyatra kvāpi | prāk nirṇītaṃ viśvaṃ darpaṇe nagaravat abhinnam api bhinnam iva unmīlayati

Literal rendering: On her own surface, not anywhere else: she unfolds the universe determined above, like a city in a mirror, as though different although nondifferent.

The commentary describes the world as nondifferent although appearing different. Manifestation on consciousness’s own surface is stronger than dependence on an external support. It does not follow that every finite subject has explicit access to every manifestation.

**Pratyabhijñāhṛdaya 4.** Kṣemarāja. [Source](cite://ksemaraja/pratyabhijna-hrdayam/inspectable-sm-contraction-mind).

> citisaṃkocātmā cetano 'pi saṃkucitaviśvamayaḥ || 4 ||

Literal rendering: The conscious subject too, whose nature is contraction of consciousness, consists of the contracted universe.

The following sūtra explains mind through contraction toward knowables. The restriction of a local subject and its relation to the whole are both part of this explanation. Neither can replace the other.

**Pratyabhijñāhṛdaya 5.** Kṣemarāja. [Source](cite://ksemaraja/pratyabhijna-hrdayam/inspectable-sm-mind-status).

> citir eva cetanapadād avarūḍhā cetyasaṃkocinī cittam || 5 ||

Literal rendering: Consciousness itself, descended from the condition of the conscious subject and contracting through the knowable, is mind.

The commentary distinguishes predominance of bare illumination from illumination and reflexive apprehension. Quiet, restricted awareness and complete recognition are different conditions. The description of vijñānākala does not establish that this condition invariably lacks all cognized content.

**Pratyabhijñāhṛdaya commentary on 5.** Kṣemarāja’s commentary. [Source](cite://ksemaraja/pratyabhijna-hrdayam/inspectable-sm-restricted-illumination).

> citprādhānyapakṣe sahajaṃ prakāśamātrapradhānatve vijñānākalatā | prakāśaparāmarśapradhānatve tu vidyāpramātṛtā

Literal rendering: On the side of consciousness’s predominance, when mere illumination naturally predominates, there is the condition of vijñānākala; but when illumination and reflexive apprehension predominate, there is the condition of the vidyā-knower.

The contrasting locatives specify predominance, not nonexistence of consciousness. Bare illumination is not described as having the full scope of recognition in which consciousness’s agency is disclosed. The absence of that full disclosure need not mean blankness.

**Pratyabhijñāhṛdaya 16.** Kṣemarāja. [Source](cite://ksemaraja/pratyabhijna-hrdayam/inspectable-sm-living-release).

> cidānandalābhe dehādiṣu cetyamāneṣv api cidaikātmyapratipattidārḍhyaṃ jīvanmuktiḥ || 16 ||

Literal rendering: On obtaining consciousness-bliss, firmness of the apprehension of identity with consciousness, even while body and so forth are being cognized, is liberation while living.

Concessive api blocks objectlessness as necessary to living release. Recognition is therefore compatible with cognized contents. The following verse addresses the stability of recognition.

**Pratyabhijñāhṛdaya 19.** Kṣemarāja. [Source](cite://ksemaraja/pratyabhijna-hrdayam/inspectable-sm-recognition-repetition).

> samādhisaṃskāravati vyutthāne bhūyo bhūyaḥ cidaikyāmarśān nityoditasamādhilābhaḥ || 19 ||

Literal rendering: In emergence bearing the impression of absorption, from repeatedly apprehending unity with consciousness, there is attainment of ever-arisen absorption.

The carried impression of absorption and repeated apprehension of unity each contribute to this account of stable recognition.

Utpaladeva asks how experiences can belong to a connected life. Cognitions that never know one another cannot by themselves explain that connection; remembering “I experienced” presents an experience as one's own. He then asks what makes awareness different from a crystal that merely takes on an image. His answer is *vimarśa*, reflexive apprehension: manifestation includes the apprehending of it, rather than bare clarity alone. His further account of *svātantrya*, independence, holds that this activity does not await a separately established knowable. Kṣemarāja develops the account of manifestation: consciousness unfolds a world on its own surface, while the finite knower and mind are contractions of consciousness. Practice addresses this restriction. Living release is firm recognition of identity with consciousness even while body and other contents are known; repeated recognition after absorption stabilizes it. The positive account thus connects ownership, reflexivity, manifestation, restricted access and cultivated recognition. Its claim that the world is manifested in consciousness remains distinct from the question of what a particular finite subject knows.

**Spandakārikā 1.10.** Spandakārikā, disputed attribution. [Source](cite://spanda-karika/spanda-karika/inspectable-sm-spanda-abiding-agency).

> tadāsyākṛtrimo dharmo jñatvakartṛtvalakṣaṇaḥ | yatas tadepsitaṃ sarvaṃ jānāti ca karoti ca || 10 ||

Literal rendering: Then its uncontrived character is characterized by knowing and acting, since it knows and does all that is desired.

Spandakārikā, disputed Vasugupta/Kallaṭa attribution, **Spandakārikā 1.14**. [Source](cite://spanda-karika/spanda-karika/inspectable-sakti-context-11).

> avasthāyugalaṃ cātra kāryakartṛtvaśabditam |
> kāryatā kṣayiṇī tatra kartṛtvam punar akṣayam || 14 ||

Literal rendering: And here the pair of conditions is designated effecthood and agency. Of these, effecthood perishes; agency, however, is imperishable.

**Spandakārikā 1.15.** Spandakārikā, disputed Vasugupta/Kallaṭa attribution. [Source](cite://spanda-karika/spanda-karika/inspectable-sm-spanda-persistence).

> kāryonmukhaḥ prayatno yaḥ kevalaṃ so 'tra lupyate | tasmin lupte vilupto 'smīty abudhaḥ pratipadyate || 15 ||

Literal rendering: Only that effort which is directed toward an effect disappears here. When it disappears, the unawakened one understands, “I have disappeared.”

Spandakārikā, disputed Vasugupta/Kallaṭa attribution, **Spandakārikā 1.16**. [Source](cite://spanda-karika/spanda-karika/inspectable-sakti-context-12).

> na tu yo 'ntarmukho bhāvah sarvajñātvaguṇāspadam |
> tasya lopaḥ kadācit syād anyasyānupalambhanāt || 16 ||

Literal rendering: But the inward-directed condition, the abode of the quality of omniscience, could never disappear because of the nonapprehension of another [thing].

The source’s bhāvah and sarvajñātva spellings are retained; the argument concerns absence of another object, not annihilation of the inward condition.

Verse 14 and 16 contrast perishing effecthood with imperishable agency and persistence of the inward condition. Capacity, its outward exercise and the persistence of the subject must be distinguished. The Spandakārikā’s attribution to Vasugupta or Kallaṭa remains disputed; its affirmative agency-language is not equivalent to an inactive puruṣa.

Verse 14–16 distinguish outward-effort cessation from imperishable agency. Knowing and acting are positive features of this account. Their affirmation remains a point of comparison with traditions that describe the liberated self as inactive.

Bhairava’s reply, **Vijñānabhairava 8b–10**. [Source](cite://vijnana-bhairava/vijnana-bhairava/inspectable-sakti-context-13).

> yatkiṃcit sakalaṃ rūpaṃ bhairavasya prakīrtitam || 8 ||

> tad asāratayā devi vijñeyaṃ śakrajālavat |
> māyāsvapnopamaṃ caiva gandharvanagarabhramam || 9 ||

> dhyānārtham bhrāntabuddhīnāṃ kriyāḍambaravartinām |
> kevalaṃ varṇitam puṃsāṃ vikalpanihatātmanām || 10 ||

Literal rendering: Whatever form with parts has been proclaimed of Bhairava is to be understood, O Goddess, as without substance, like a magical display, comparable to illusion and dream, an apparition of a city of the Gandharvas. It is described only for the meditation of people of confused understanding, engaged in the bustle of ritual acts, whose selves are struck down by conceptual construction.

Bhairava’s reply, **Vijñānabhairava 14**. [Source](cite://vijnana-bhairava/vijnana-bhairava/inspectable-sakti-context-14).

> dikkālakalanonmuktā deśoddeśāviśeṣinī |
> vyapadeṣṭum aśakyāsāv akathyā paramārthataḥ || 14 ||

Literal rendering: Freed from the reckoning of direction and time, not differentiated by place or designation of place, that [state] cannot be designated and is inexpressible in ultimate truth.

**Vijñānabhairava, 15–17.** Bhairava’s reply. [Source](cite://vijnana-bhairava/vijnana-bhairava/inspectable-df-trika-full-state).

> antaḥsvānubhavānandā vikalponmuktagocarā |
> yāvasthā bharitākārā bhairavī bhairavātmanaḥ || 15 ||

> tad vapus tattvato jñeyaṃ vimalaṃ viśvapūraṇam |
> evaṃvidhe pare tattve kaḥ pūjyaḥ kaś ca tṛpyati || 16 ||

> evaṃvidhā bhairavasya yāvasthā parigīyate |
> sā parā pararūpeṇa parā devī prakīrtitā || 17 ||

Literal rendering: That state of Bhairava’s self, Bhairavī, whose bliss is one’s own inward experience, whose domain is freed from conceptual construction, whose form is fullness—that is to be known as [his] body in truth, stainless, filling the universe. In such a supreme reality, who is to be worshipped and who is satisfied? That state of Bhairava which is celebrated in this manner—she is the Supreme; in the supreme manner [she] is proclaimed the supreme Goddess.

The preceding reply in 8–10 qualifies representations with parts as meditative aids, while 14 removes spatial and temporal delimitation. Fullness here belongs to a state whose domain is freed from conceptual construction, called Bhairava’s true body and the supreme Goddess. Vapus must not be turned into the unqualified claim that every visibly delimited image is already this state precisely as delimited. The ultimate-register question about worship does not cancel the practices that follow, or establish an avatāra classification.

Bhairava’s reply, **Vijñānabhairava 18**. [Source](cite://vijnana-bhairava/vijnana-bhairava/inspectable-sakti-context-15).

> śaktiśaktimator yadvad abhedaḥ sarvadā sthitaḥ |
> atas taddharmadharmitvāt parā śaktiḥ parātmanaḥ || 18 ||

Literal rendering: Since the nondifference of power and the possessor of power always abides, therefore, because of their being attribute and attribute-bearer, the supreme power belongs to the supreme Self.

**Vijñānabhairava 19a.** Vijñānabhairava, transmitted dialogue. [Source](cite://vijnana-bhairava/vijnana-bhairava/inspectable-sm-power-fire).

> na vahner dāhikā śaktir vyatiriktā vibhāvyate |

Literal rendering: The burning power of fire is not conceived as separate.

**Vijñānabhairava 20.** Vijñānabhairava, transmitted dialogue. [Source](cite://vijnana-bhairava/vijnana-bhairava/inspectable-sm-power-route).

> śaktyavasthāpraviṣṭasya nirvibhāgena bhāvanā | tadāsau śivarūpī syāt śaivī mukham ihocyate || 20 ||

Literal rendering: For one who has entered the condition of power, contemplation without division: then he would be of Śiva’s form. Śaivī is called the entrance here.

The genitive specifies the practitioner; syāt is optative. The verse specifies entry into the condition of power and contemplation without division. Merely having some cognitive content does not meet those conditions.

Verse 18 states abiding nonseparation; 19 uses fire; 20 calls Śaivī the entrance. The power’s constitutive nonseparation and the practitioner’s route of recognition are distinct claims. The earlier qualification of determinate representations remains essential to reading both.

### The mirror and its limits

A mirror offers a concrete restriction on the language of inclusion: visible form appears without the original’s touch or mountain-weight. The receiving support also differs from the conditions for knowing the image. Memory and imagination motivate a further account of cognition without requiring a presently existing original. The extension to universal manifestation still invokes intrinsic power and the denial of an independent outside; the mirror alone does not establish it.

**Tantrāloka with Viveka 3.9, commentary printed 10.** Jayaratha; rejected consequence in a reductio. [Source](cite://jayaratha/tantraloka-viveka/inspectable-sm-receiver-scope).

> anyathā ‘sarvatra sarvaṃ bhāyāt’ iti darpaṇe ’pi sparśaḥ pratibimbaṃ gṛhṇīyāt

Literal rendering: Otherwise, “everything would appear everywhere”; touch too would take a reflection in a mirror.

The quoted consequence is rejected: mirror clarity is aspect-limited, whereas consciousness is declared clear in all respects. A mirror admits some aspects of its original and excludes others. The further claim that consciousness is unrestricted requires the argument about consciousness’s freedom, rather than this counterexample alone.

**Viveka on Tantrāloka 3.16, printed 18.** Jayaratha’s Viveka. [Source](cite://jayaratha/tantraloka-viveka/inspectable-sm-reflection-real).

> tatra darpaṇādau pratibimbitaṃ sat vastveva, na punaravastu

Literal rendering: Being reflected there in a mirror and the like, it is indeed a thing, not a non-thing.

The context identifies a presentation differing from its original while denying physical transfer of every property. The image can have reality as a presentation without the original’s being physically present in the mirror.
