Its difference from the original does not make it an independently existing second substance.

**Viveka on Tantrāloka 3.18, printed 20.** Jayaratha; reductio. [Source](cite://jayaratha/tantraloka-viveka/inspectable-sm-mountain-weight).

> gurutvādidharmasaṃbhave hi tadyogāt darpaṇo ’py acālyaḥ syāt, na caivam

Literal rendering: For if properties such as weight occurred, through their conjunction the mirror too would be immovable; but it is not so.

An explicit rejected consequence restricts the prior subordination-language. The original’s being represented does not transfer all its physical properties to the receiving medium.

**Tantrāloka 3.19.** Abhinavagupta. [Source](cite://abhinavagupta/tantraloka/inspectable-sm-support-access).

> ādhārastatra tūpāyā dīpadṛksaṃvidaḥ kramāt

Literal rendering: The support [is so called]; there, however, lamp, sight and cognition are means in succession.

Abhinavagupta, **Tantrāloka 3.19a**. [Source](cite://abhinavagupta/tantraloka/inspectable-sakti-context-36).

> tasmāttu naiṣa bhedena yadbhāti tata ucyate

Literal rendering: Because this [reflection] does not appear separately from that [mirror], therefore [the mirror] is called [the support].

The sentence continues in the half-verse quoted above: ādhāraḥ, support, is the predicate of the mirror.

Jayaratha, **Jayaratha on Tantrāloka 3.19, printed 21**. [Source](cite://jayaratha/tantraloka-viveka/inspectable-sakti-context-37).

> atra punarutpannasya sataḥ pratibimbasya jñaptāvālokādaya upāyā, iti -- tebhyo.asya viśeṣaḥ

Literal rendering: Here, however, light and the rest are means for knowing a reflection that has already arisen and exists: thus this [support] differs from those.

The first half says the image does not appear separately from the mirror; Jayaratha distinguishes occurring reflection from its later being known. The support in which reflection occurs differs from the successive means by which it becomes known. Reflection’s occurrence and someone’s access to it are separate explanatory matters.

**Tantrāloka with Viveka 3.61–65, especially 64.** Abhinavagupta’s root verse, with Jayaratha’s explanation distinguished below. [Source](cite://abhinavagupta/tantraloka/inspectable-sm-memory-no-original).

> ata evāntaraṃ kiṃciddhīsaṃjñaṃ bhavatu sphuṭam

Literal rendering: For that very reason let there be clearly something intermediate called cognition.

Jayaratha, **Jayaratha on Tantrāloka 3.61, printed 68**. [Source](cite://jayaratha/tantraloka-viveka/inspectable-sakti-context-38).

> tena bimbaṃ vināpi pratibimbaṃ bhavet -- tadutpādanasamarthasya tatpratinidhibhūtasya kāraṇāntarasyāpi bhāvāt

Literal rendering: Therefore a reflection could occur even without an original, because there can also be another cause, capable of producing it, that takes the place of that [original].

Jayaratha, **Jayaratha on Tantrāloka 3.62, printed 69**. [Source](cite://jayaratha/tantraloka-viveka/inspectable-sakti-context-39).

> ghaneneti -- bhāvanātmatāmāpannenetyarthaḥ, anyathā hi sarvasyaiva smartuḥ sarvadaiva puraḥ smaryamāṇaṃ bhāyāt

Literal rendering: “Dense” means: having attained the character of sustained imaginative contemplation. Otherwise, what is remembered would appear in front of every person who remembers, at all times.

Jayaratha, **Jayaratha on Tantrāloka 3.64, printed 70**. [Source](cite://jayaratha/tantraloka-viveka/inspectable-sakti-context-60).

> kiṃcitsaṃkucitapramātrātma susphuṭaṃ nirvikalparūpaṃ jñānasaṃjñamāntaraṃ parasaṃvitprameyayormadhyavarti bhavatu

Literal rendering: Let there be something internal called cognition, whose nature is a somewhat contracted knower, very clear, nonconceptual in form, situated between supreme awareness and the knowable.

Jayaratha on 61 permits an alternative operative cause; 62 insists intense recollection, not every memory, explains the vivid image; 64 places dhī between supreme consciousness and the knowable. The external original, operative condition and cognitive presentation are not interchangeable. The absence of a presently existing original does not by itself prove that consciousness creates all reality.

**Tantrāloka 3.65.** Abhinavagupta. [Source](cite://abhinavagupta/tantraloka/inspectable-sm-cosmic-power-bridge).

> ato nimittaṃ devasya śaktayaḥ santu tādṛśe

Literal rendering: Therefore let the powers of the God be the operative cause in such [world-reflection].

Jayaratha, **Jayaratha on Tantrāloka 3.65, printed 71**. [Source](cite://jayaratha/tantraloka-viveka/inspectable-sakti-context-40).

> devasya dyotanātmanaścittattvasya, tādṛśe viśvapratibimbane, jñānakriyādyāḥ śaktayo nimittaṃ bhavantu

Literal rendering: For the God, the consciousness-principle whose nature is illumination, let the powers of knowledge, action and the rest be the operative cause in such reflection of the universe.

Jayaratha, **Jayaratha on Tantrāloka 3.65, printed 72**. [Source](cite://jayaratha/tantraloka-viveka/inspectable-sakti-context-41).

> anyeti -- anyamukhaprekṣitve hyasya svātantryaṃ khaṇḍyeteti bhāvaḥ, svātantryaṃ hi vimarśa ityucyate, sa cāsya mukhyaḥ svabhāvaḥ, nahi nirvimarśaḥ prakāśaḥ saṃbhavatyupapadyate vā

Literal rendering: “Another”: the purport is that, if it looked to another, its independence would be broken. For independence is called reflexive apprehension, and that is its principal nature. Illumination without reflexive apprehension does not occur or become intelligible.

Jayaratha identifies knowledge/action powers and argues against dependence on something outside. This is stronger than ordinary mirror theory. The extension to cosmic manifestation invokes the God’s intrinsic powers and the denial of an independent outside. The imperative santu gives the argument’s proposed conclusion; it does not turn the mirror comparison into direct observation of the entire cosmos.

### Complete apprehension and fullness

Abhinavagupta does not begin by declaring that every thing contains every other thing. He asks what must be included in a complete account of a particular manifestation. Earth has a qualitative constitution; its account therefore leads through subtle qualities, sensory articulation and determination, intellect and the undivided root of nature. Nature as experienced requires a conscious experiencer. A contracted experiencer requires an account both of consciousness and of the power through which contraction occurs. That power cannot be exhausted by the contracted result if it is to explain the possibility of contraction and expansion. The inquiry consequently reaches free, complete consciousness. These are the premises of Abhinavagupta’s argument.

This route is asymmetric. Being a śiṃśapā tree requires being a tree, while being a tree does not require being a śiṃśapā. Likewise, showing that a manifestation requires its constituents does not yet show that every constituent contains every manifestation. Abhinavagupta supplies a separate operation: complete apprehension of an earlier principle retains the completed field of the later one. His example moves from earth to water without abandoning the earth-field already apprehended, then continues through the articulation. The claim is stronger than remembering an arbitrary image: complete apprehension corresponds to a fullness intrinsically present in consciousness. These two arguments—constitutive prerequisite and retained fullness—must be represented separately before they can jointly support an all-in-all conclusion.

[Source](cite://abhinavagupta/paratrimsika-vivarana/category-trika-fullness)

Here a *tattva* is a principle in the author’s explanatory ordering. Earth and water enter this argument through that account of constitution.

Abhinavagupta makes complete apprehension a condition, rather than assuming that every ordinary perception is exhaustive. His prerequisite argument has a direction; the tree comparison blocks its careless reversal. Retention of what has already been completely apprehended supplies another premise. Complete constitution and present finite access therefore remain distinct. A further passage separates reversal of a manifested ordering from reversal of Bhairava’s intrinsic nature.

**Parātriṃśikāvivaraṇa extended 5–8 block, electronic 716.** Abhinavagupta. [Source](cite://abhinavagupta/paratrimsika-vivarana/inspectable-sm-fullness-conditional).

> dharāsaṃvid dhi tathā dharāṃ viṣayatayāpy abhedenābhāsayed vimṛśec ca yadi tatsvarūpasarvasvāvabhāsavimarśayoḥ vyāpriyeta

Literal rendering: For earth-cognition would thus manifest and reflexively apprehend earth, even as object, without difference, if it engaged in the manifestation and reflexive apprehension of the entirety of its nature.

The word yadi makes complete apprehension an explicit condition. The condition concerns disclosure of the whole nature of the object. An ordinary cognition that merely has earth as its object need not satisfy it.

**Parātriṃśikāvivaraṇa electronic 726.** Abhinavagupta. [Source](cite://abhinavagupta/paratrimsika-vivarana/inspectable-sm-fullness-asymmetry).

> yac ca yena vinā na bhavati tat tāvatsvarūpaṃ yathā śiṃśapātvaṃ vṛkṣatvasvarūpaṃ

Literal rendering: And what does not occur without something has that much as its nature, as being a śiṃśapā has being a tree as its nature.

Abhinavagupta, **Parātriṃśikāvivaraṇa, electronic paragraph 726**. [Source](cite://abhinavagupta/paratrimsika-vivarana/inspectable-sakti-context-18).

> yat tu yāvatsvarupaṃ na bhavati tat tena vinā bhavaty eva

Literal rendering: But what does not have that much as its nature does indeed occur without that.

The source spelling svarupaṃ is retained. The following electronic tree-example ending is compressed and uncertain; the prose makes the direction of the converse explicit without silently repairing that ending.

The following prose explicitly distinguishes what can occur without another from what requires it. Thus the tree example warrants the forward dependency without making every dependency reciprocal.

**Parātriṃśikāvivaraṇa electronic 728.** Abhinavagupta. [Source](cite://abhinavagupta/paratrimsika-vivarana/inspectable-sm-fullness-chain).

> sarvam eva cedaṃ prathamānaṃ svatantraparipūrṇaprathāsārabhairavaṃ vinā kiṃ cid eva neti svasaṃvitsiddho 'yaṃ tattvakramaḥ

Literal rendering: And all this that manifests is nothing at all without Bhairava, whose essence is independent complete manifestation; thus this order of principles is established in one’s own awareness.

The paragraph traces a sequence from earth through water, subtle qualities, senses and determination, root nature, and the experiencer, to contraction and power. The intermediate reasons are part of Abhinavagupta’s argument, rather than dispensable steps on the way to naming Bhairava.

**Parātriṃśikāvivaraṇa electronic 728, beginning of the prerequisite chain.** Abhinavagupta. [Source](cite://abhinavagupta/paratrimsika-vivarana/inspectable-sm-fullness-prerequisites).

> na bhavanti ca dharādīny uttarottaratattvāni jalādi pūrvapūrvaṃ vineti tāvatsvarūpāṇy eva / dharā hi na jalaṃ vinā bhavet sruter eva kāṭhinye darśanād ity evaṃkrameṇa bhūtāni tanmātrair vinā katham, tāny api indriyajṛmbhayā vinā, indriyāṇy api tattathāvidhādhyavasāyena vinā / sarvāṇi caitadādyāvibhaktānvitasūkṣmarūpamūlakāraṇavinākṛtāni na bhavanti / mūlaprakṛtir api bhogyā bhoktāraṃ tadbhogyavibhāgabhāgitvād eva saṃkucitaṃ saṃkocavaśād eva ca svātmārohitakālakalādipāśajālaṃ saṃvidātmakaṃ cāntareṇa kathaṃ / saṃvidaś cākhaṇḍarūpāyāḥ kathaṃ saṃkocakāraṇasvātantryaṃ māyāparaparyāyaṃ vinā saṃkucitatvam

Literal rendering: Earth and the other successively later principles do not exist without the successively earlier water and so on; therefore they have that much as their nature. For earth would not exist without water, since what flows itself is seen in hardness. Thus, in this order: how [could] the elements [exist] without the subtle elements, those without the unfolding of the senses, and the senses without determination of that particular kind? And all these do not exist deprived of the root cause whose subtle form is connected with them undividedly at their beginning. And how [could] root nature, which is to be experienced, [exist] without an experiencer whose nature is awareness, contracted precisely through sharing in that division of objects of experience, and, through contraction itself, bearing upon itself the net of bonds beginning with time and limited agency? And how [could] undivided awareness have contraction without freedom, the cause of contraction, also designated māyā?

The repeated vinā/antareṇa constructions supply the dependence steps; rhetorical katham denies the proposed separation. Adhyavasāya is determinative judgment; kalā here belongs to the bonds of contraction, not an artistic faculty. The next compressed compound concerning degrees of contraction remains textually difficult; the secure final sentence gives the conclusion concerning Bhairava. The directed dependence relations must remain distinct from their converse. The latter needs the additional argument about retention and complete apprehension.

**Parātriṃśikāvivaraṇa electronic 712.** Abhinavagupta. [Source](cite://abhinavagupta/paratrimsika-vivarana/inspectable-sm-fullness-retention).

> taduttararūpaparipūrṇatām ajahat

Literal rendering: Not abandoning the completeness of the subsequent form.

Abhinavagupta, **Parātriṃśikāvivaraṇa, electronic paragraph 712**. [Source](cite://abhinavagupta/paratrimsika-vivarana/inspectable-sakti-context-16).

> tatpurvo 'pi bhāgas taduttarabhāgapṛṣṭhapātivṛttapūrvaparipūrṇabhāsāsāravimarśatādātmyāt taduttararūpaparipūrṇatām ajahat svayaṃ ca svarūpanāntarīyakatāhaṭhakṛṣṭasvapūrvapūrvatarādibhāgāntarābhogo bhāsamāno vimṛśyamānaś ca tathaivākhaṇḍita

Literal rendering: The preceding portion too, because of its identity with reflexive apprehension whose essence is the complete illumination that previously occurred upon the subsequent portion, does not abandon the completeness of that subsequent form; itself also encompassing the other portions prior and still prior to itself, forcibly drawn in by their necessary connection with its intrinsic nature, it is illuminated and reflexively apprehended, likewise undivided.

The excerpt stops before iti. “Previously occurred” is crucial: retention is not inferred from bare causal dependence.

Abhinavagupta, **Parātriṃśikāvivaraṇa, electronic paragraph 720**. [Source](cite://abhinavagupta/paratrimsika-vivarana/inspectable-sakti-context-17).

> tad evaṃ prathamaṃ tāvad dharā tato 'pi jalaṃ tathaiva svarūpasākalyena bhāsamānaṃ vimṛśyamānaṃ ca tadbhāsāvimarśacamatkāram antaḥkṛtatathāvidhadharaṇitattvasaṃskārasattākaṃ pūrayed eveti yāvad ante saiva pūrṇasaṃvidbhagavatī śivātmaiva

Literal rendering: Thus, first earth, then water, likewise being illuminated and reflexively apprehended in the entirety of its intrinsic nature, would fill out the savoring of that illumination and reflexive apprehension, which contains within it the existing impression of earth-principle of that kind—until, at the end, that very complete Goddess awareness has precisely Śiva as her nature.

The earth-impression is of the previously specified complete apprehension; this is not a claim about every ordinary memory.

The complete sentence ties retention to earlier complete illumination and reflexive apprehension. The earth-then-water example makes that condition concrete. Retention contributes a premise distinct from dependence. It does not imply that every ordinary act of cognition explicitly contains every other possible content.

**Parātriṃśikāvivaraṇa electronic 738.** Abhinavagupta. [Source](cite://abhinavagupta/paratrimsika-vivarana/inspectable-sm-fullness-identity-order).

> bhagavadbhairavabhaṭṭārakas tu sadā pūrṇo 'nantasvatantra eva na viparyasyate jātucid api cidrūpātirekādyabhāvād

Literal rendering: But the venerable Lord Bhairava, always full and infinitely independent, is never reversed at any time, because there is no being beyond consciousness and so forth.

Abhinavagupta, **Parātriṃśikāvivaraṇa, electronic paragraph 738, before the invariant-Bhairava clause**. [Source](cite://abhinavagupta/paratrimsika-vivarana/inspectable-sakti-context-61).

> yat parasaṃvidi śaktitattvaṃ tad eva parāparātmani pṛthivītattvaṃ, yat tu dharātattvaṃ tac chaktitattvam iti kṣakārāt prabhṛti dharādīnāṃ sthitiḥ

Literal rendering: What is the power-principle in supreme awareness is that very earth-principle in the mixed supreme-and-nonsupreme [condition]; what is the earth-principle is the power-principle. Thus the placement of earth and the rest begins with the letter kṣa.

This is the specified reversal of articulated principle/letter order. It precedes the denial that Bhairava’s own nature undergoes reversal.

The specified earth/power ordering changes between the supreme and mixed conditions. The reversal of an articulated ordering does not reverse Bhairava’s intrinsic nature. This does not establish that every predicate of each manifestation is invariant.

## 12. Śākta and modern accounts of power and devotion

The following sources develop power, manifestation and devotion through distinct arguments and forms of teaching. Their sequence keeps each author’s account intact before bringing the claims into the common comparison.

### The Goddess: manifestation, power and knowledge

The Devīgītā presents a Goddess who appears in a determinate form, promises a familial birth, teaches inherent power and nonobjectifiable consciousness, and also describes māyā as sublated by knowledge. Its account of highest devotion combines nonotherness with nonhostility and culminates in knowledge. Naṭanānanda’s Cidvallī gives willing, knowing and acting a central role in reflexivity and interprets paired Śiva–Śakti imagery through the Lord’s own power. These accounts can be compared without turning them into one generic Śākta doctrine.

Devīgītā chapter 1 corresponds to Devībhāgavata 7.31; its following chapters continue through 7.40. The first passages below are from the root narrative. The 37-page doctrinal extract linked below begins at printed 176 and does not identify its editor or edition on a title page; its page images establish the quoted readings without establishing a critical text. The later doctrinal extract follows the scanned Sanskrit reading where its wording differs materially from the held electronic text.

**Devībhāgavata / Devīgītā, 7.31.29–31a = Devīgītā 1.29–31a.** Vyāsa’s narration. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-df-sakti-same-light).

> ādyantarahitaṃ tattu na hastādyaṅgasaṃyutam /
> na ca strīrūpamathavā na puṃrūpamathobhayam //
> dīptyā pidhānaṃ netrāṇāṃ teṣāmāsīnmahīpate /
> punaśca dhairyamālambya yāvatte dadṛśuḥ surāḥ //
> tāvattadeva strīrūpeṇābhāddivyaṃ manoharam /

Literal rendering: But that was without beginning and end, not furnished with limbs such as hands; nor was it a female form, nor a male form, nor both. Through its brilliance their eyes were covered, O lord of the earth. And when the gods, again taking courage, looked, then that very same [luminosity] shone in a female form, divine and captivating.

Verse 26–28 introduce the supreme luminosity. The emphatic tad eva, “that very same,” preserves its referent through the contrast between non-gendered luminosity and determinate female appearance. The manifested Goddess is not introduced as a second unrelated ultimate. This does not make the female outline itself spatially endless or assign every divine predicate to every finite body. The excerpt ends at 31a; 31b begins a feminine accusative description completed by “they saw” in 41. [Printed witness](https://archive.org/details/SrimadDeviBhagavatamWithTheTikaOfNilankantha), PDF 601.

**Devībhāgavata / Devīgītā, 7.31.39–41 = Devīgītā 1.39–41.** Vyāsa’s narration. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-df-sakti-personal-form).

> pāśāṅkuśavarābhīticaturbāhuṃ trilocanām /
> raktavastraparīdhānāṃ dāḍimīkusumaprabhām //
> sarvaśṛṅgāraveṣāḍhyāṃ sarvadevanamaskṛtām /
> sarvāśāpūrikāṃ sarvamātaraṃ sarvamohinīm //
> prasādasumukhīmambāṃ mandasmitamukhāmbujām /
> avyājakaruṇāmūrtiṃ dadṛśuḥ purataḥ surāḥ //

Literal rendering: The gods saw before them the three-eyed [Goddess], with four arms [bearing] noose, goad, boon and fearlessness, clothed in red, shining like a pomegranate flower; rich in every adornment of beauty, saluted by all the gods, fulfilling all hopes, mother of all, enchanting all; the Mother whose face was gracious, whose lotus-face bore a gentle smile, the embodiment of unfeigned compassion.

The narrative’s determinate appearance includes bodily features, gracious expression and responsiveness. Boon and fearlessness are compressed emblems or gestures in the four-arm description. At40 the held IAST and the actual PDF 602 print read sarvāśāpūrikāṃ; the [Satsangdhara electronic Sanskrit](https://www.hindi.satsangdhara.net/deviP/deviP-07-31.htm) instead reads sarvāśāparipūrikāṃ. Both support fulfilling all hopes. These are the root narrator’s words; Nīlakaṇṭha’s surrounding commentary is a separate voice. [Printed witness](https://archive.org/details/SrimadDeviBhagavatamWithTheTikaOfNilankantha), PDF 602.

**Devībhāgavata / Devīgītā, 7.31.63–65 = Devīgītā 1.63–65.** Vyāsa’s speech introduction; then the Goddess herself. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-df-sakti-power-birth).

> iti teṣāṃ vacaḥ śrutvā provāca parameśvarī /
> mama śaktistu yā gaurī bhaviṣyati himālaye //
> śivāya sā pradeyā syāt sā vaḥ kāryaṃ vidhāsyati /
> bhaktiryaccaraṇāmbhoje bhūyādyuṣmākamādarāt //
> himālayo hi manasā māmupāste 'tibhaktitaḥ /
> tatastasya gṛhe janma mama priyakaraṃ matam //

Literal rendering: Having heard their words, the supreme Lady said: “My power who is Gaurī will come to be in Himālaya. She should be given to Śiva; she will accomplish your purpose. May your devotion to [her] lotus-feet arise with earnestness. For Himālaya worships me mentally with exceeding devotion. Therefore birth in his house is considered pleasing to me.”

The gods seek the conditions for Śiva’s son to defeat Tāraka. The Goddess calls Gaurī “my power” and finds birth in Himālaya’s household pleasing. At64 yaccaraṇāmbhoje is literally “at whose lotus-feet”; the antecedent Gaurī is retained rather than silently replacing it with the Hindi paraphrase’s “my feet.” At65 mama can attach to pleasing-to-me rather than uniquely to birth. The daughter-identification is supplied explicitly by Himālaya’s response, not by forcing a single syntactic attachment. [Printed witness](https://archive.org/details/SrimadDeviBhagavatamWithTheTikaOfNilankantha), PDF 603.

**Devībhāgavata / Devīgītā, 7.31.69–70 = Devīgītā 1.69–70.** Himālaya’s response within the narrative. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-df-sakti-universe-daughter).

> adya prapañce kīrtiḥ syājjaganmātā sutābhavat /
> aho himālayasyāsya dhanyo 'sau bhāgyavāniti //
> yasyāstu jaṭhare santi brahmāṇḍānāṃ ca koṭayaḥ /
> saiva yasya sutā jātā ko vā syāttatsamo bhuvi //

Literal rendering: Today there would be this fame in the world: “The mother of the universe became a daughter! Ah, this Himālaya is fortunate, blessed!” But she in whose belly there are crores of cosmic eggs—she herself has become his daughter: who on earth could be his equal?

Himālaya responds to the promised birth with anticipatory praise. Sā eva, “she herself,” identifies the universe-containing mother with his daughter. Cosmic inclusion and a local family relation are thus affirmed together. Koṭayaḥ denotes plural crores, not a mathematical proof of infinity. This is a daughter-and-belly passage after a promise, not a child’s mouth-vision; comparison with such a scene must preserve the different narrative setting. [Printed witness](https://archive.org/details/SrimadDeviBhagavatamWithTheTikaOfNilankantha), PDF 604.

The birth narrative establishes continuity of a divine referent through form and family relation. The Goddess’s subsequent teaching must now determine how power, consciousness and differentiated appearance are understood; the narrative alone cannot settle those relations.

**Devīgītā 2.5, with 2.3–4.** Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sm-devi-inherent-power).

> pāvakasyoṣṇateveyamuṣṇāṃśoriva dīdhitiḥ / candrasya candrikeveyaṃ mameyaṃ sahajā dhruvā

Literal rendering: Like heat of fire, like the sun’s radiance, like moonlight of the moon, this [power] of mine is innate and enduring.

Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker, **Devīgītā 2.3–4**. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sakti-context-19).

> apratarkyam anirdeśyamanaupamyamanāmayam /
> tasya kācitsvataḥ siddhā śaktirmāyeti viśrutā // 2.3 //
> na satī sā nāsatī sā nobhayātmā virodhataḥ /
> etadvilakṣaṇā kācidvastubhūtāsti sarvadā // 2.4 //

Literal rendering: [That self-nature is] beyond reasoning, indescribable, incomparable and free from affliction. It has a certain power, established of itself, known as māyā. She is not real, not unreal, nor both, because of contradiction; differing from these, a certain existent [power] always is.

Verse 2.3 names māyā; 2.4 denies simple real/unreal/both classification. 2.11 also says it is destroyed by knowledge. The power’s being innate and enduring must be read together with the later claim that knowledge sublates it. This entire māyā account cannot simply be exchanged for Trika’s account of intrinsic freedom.

**Devīgītā 2.11.** Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sm-devi-maya-sublation).

> tasyā jaḍatvaṃ dṛśyatvājjñānanāśāttato 'satī

Literal rendering: Its insentience is due to being seen; from destruction by knowledge, therefore it is not real.

Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker, **Devīgītā 2.9–10**. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sakti-context-20).

> kecittāṃ tapa ityāhustamaḥ kecijjaḍaṃ pare /
> jñānaṃ māyāṃ pradhānaṃ ca prakṛtiṃ śaktimapyajām // 2.9 //
> vimarśa iti tāṃ prāhuḥ śaivaśāstraviśāradāḥ /
> avidyāmitare prāhurvedatattvārthacintakāḥ // 2.10 //

Literal rendering: Some call her austerity; some darkness; others the inert, knowledge, māyā, the principal [principle], primordial nature, power, and the unborn. Those skilled in Śaiva scripture call her reflexive apprehension; others, thinkers on the true meaning of the Veda, call her ignorance.

These are reported names, not an assertion that all the named traditions give the same theory of the power.

The antecedent is the power named in 2.3 and differently named in 9–10, including vimarśa in a report of Śaiva terminology. A reported use of vimarśa does not import the entire Trika theory into this passage. Its judgments of insentience and unreality belong to the Goddess’s own surrounding argument.

**Devīgītā 2.12–16.** Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sm-devi-nonobject).

> caitanyasya na dṛśyatvam dṛśyatve jaḍameva tat / svaprakāśaṃ ca caitanyaṃ na pareṇa prakāśitam

Literal rendering: Consciousness is not an object of seeing; if it were an object of seeing, it would be insentient. And consciousness is self-luminous, not illuminated by another.

Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker, **Devīgītā 2.13–14**. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sakti-context-21).

> anavasthādoṣasattvānna svenāpi prakāśitam /
> karmakartrīvirodhaḥ syāttasmāttaddīpavatsvayam // 2.13 //
> prakāśamānamanyeṣāṃ bhāsakaṃ viddhi parvata /
> ata eva ca nityatvaṃ siddhasaṃvittanormama // 2.14 //

Literal rendering: Because there would be the fault of infinite regress, [consciousness is not illuminated by another]; nor is it illuminated by itself, for a contradiction between object and agent would follow. Therefore know it, like a lamp, as shining of itself and illuminating others, O mountain. For that very reason the eternity of me, whose body is established awareness, [is established].

The bracketed clause carries forward 2.12. The sentence about the lamp continues across the verse boundary; it is not cut at 2.13.

Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker, **Devīgītā 2.15–16**. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sakti-context-22).

> jāgratsvapnasuṣuptyādau dṛśyasya vyabhicārataḥ /
> saṃvido vyabhicāraśca nānubhūto 'sti karhicit // 2.15 //
> yadi tasyāpyanubhavastarhyayaṃ yena sākṣiṇā /
> anubhūtaḥ sa evātra śiṣṭaḥ saṃvidvapuḥ purā // 2.16 //

Literal rendering: Because the seen varies in waking, dream, deep sleep and the other [states], whereas variation of awareness has never been experienced. If that too were experienced, then the witness by which this was experienced is itself what remains here, having awareness as its body, as before.

The initial because continues the preceding eternity argument; tasyāḥ refers to awareness.

Verse 13 blocks regress and subject-object self-illumination; 15–16 witness persistence across varying states. Consciousness’s intrinsic presence is not produced by a cognitive episode. Self-luminosity alone does not yet establish formative agency.

**Devīgītā 2.19–21.** Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sm-devi-knowledge-nature).

> tac ca jñānaṃ nātmadharmo dharmatve jaḍatātmanaḥ

Literal rendering: And that knowledge is not a property of the self: if it were a property, insentience would belong to the self.

Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker, **Devīgītā 2.20–21**. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sakti-context-23).

> jñānasya jaḍaśeṣatvaṃ na dṛṣṭaṃ na ca saṃbhavi /
> ciddharmatvaṃ tathā nāsti citaścinna hi bhidyate // 2.20 //
> tasmādātmā jñānarūpaḥ sukharūpaśca sarvadā /
> satyaḥ pūrṇo 'py asaṅgaśca dvaitajālavivarjitaḥ // 2.21 //

Literal rendering: Knowledge’s subordination to the inert is neither seen nor possible. Nor can it be a property of consciousness, for consciousness does not differ from consciousness. Therefore the self always has the form of knowledge and happiness, is real, full and unattached, devoid of the net of duality.

Verses 2.20–21 follow the electronic witness; the quotation of 2.19 follows the scan.

Verse 20 says consciousness does not differ from consciousness; 21 concludes self is knowledge/bliss/full/unattached. Knowledge is here asserted to be the self’s nature, rather than a property belonging to an otherwise noncognitive substance. The printed scan has nātmadharmo, with nominative dharmaḥ before dh-; the held GRETIL/TextGrid transcription has nātmadharmā. The quotation follows the scan’s reading, also printed in the [SanskritDocuments transcription](https://sanskritdocuments.org/doc_giitaa/deviigiitaa.html).

**Devīgītā 3.2, with 3.1.** Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sm-devi-registers).

> vyavahāradṛśā seyaṃ māyāvidyeti viśrutā / tattvadṛṣṭyā tu nāstyeva tattvam evāsti kevalam //

Literal rendering: From the standpoint of transaction this is known as māyā, avidyā; but from the standpoint of truth it does not exist at all: reality alone exists.

Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker, **Devīgītā 3.1**. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sakti-context-24).

> manmāyāśaktisaṃklṛptaṃ jagatsarvaṃ carācaram /
> sāpi mattaḥ pṛthaṅmāyā nāstyeva paramārthataḥ // 3.1 //

Literal rendering: The whole world, moving and unmoving, is fashioned by my māyā-power. And that māyā too does not exist apart from me in ultimate truth.

Verse 3.1 denies māyā apart from the Goddess; 3.19 grounds world’s reality in her reality. The exact vidyā māyā reading is witness-specific. Transactional appearance is retained while independently existing ultimate differentiation is denied. The printed extract reads māyāvidyeti, here separated as māyā–avidyā; [GRETIL](https://gretil.sub.uni-goettingen.de/gretil/1_sanskr/3_purana/dbhp_dgu.htm) and [Satsangdhara](https://www.hindi.satsangdhara.net/deviP/deviP-07-33.htm) read vidyā māyeti. Both preserve the transactional/ultimate contrast, but the first half-lines are not interchangeable. No critical apparatus has established which reading is preferable.

**Devīgītā 3.19.** Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sm-devi-world-reality).

> adhiṣṭhānātirekeṇa kalpitaṃ tanna bhāsate / tasmānmatsattayaivaitat sattāvannānyathā bhavet

Literal rendering: What is imagined does not appear apart from its support. Therefore this has existence only through my existence; it would not be so otherwise.

Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker, **Devīgītā 3.18**. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sakti-context-25).

> rajjuryathā sarpamālābhedairekā vibhāti hi /
> tathaiveśādirūpeṇa bhāmyahaṃ nātra saṃśayaḥ // 3.18 //

Literal rendering: Just as the one rope appears in the distinctions of snake, garland and so forth, so I shine in the forms of the Lord and the rest; there is no doubt here.

The rope/snake/garland example in 18 and ultimate/transactional distinctions 1–2 control this statement. Existence through the Goddess does not make differentiation independently or ultimately separate. The rope analogy and the distinction of standpoints prevent this sentence from functioning as a complete substitute for Trika’s theory of manifestation.

**Devīgītā 4.22a = Devībhāgavata 7.34.22a.** Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sm-devi-conflicting-direct-meanings).

> vācyārthayorviruddhatvāt aikyaṃ naiva ghaṭeta ha /

Literal rendering: Because the two direct meanings are opposed, unity would indeed not be possible.

The genitive dual vācyārthayoḥ and ablative viruddhatvāt identify both the items and the reason for rejecting their initial identification. The following verse supplies consciousness alone as the indicated meaning. This first half belongs to the discussion in 4.21–24. The statement of unity consequently cannot preserve every opposed attribute of the initial direct meanings. The move to the indicated meaning does explanatory work.

**Devīgītā 4.23, with 4.24.** Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sm-devi-indicated-reference).

> cinmātraṃ tu tayorlakṣyaṃ tayoraikyasya sambhavaḥ / tayoraikyaṃ tathā jñātvā svābhedenādvayo bhavet

Literal rendering: But consciousness alone is the indicated [meaning] of the two; their unity is possible. Having thus known their unity, one would be nondual through nondifference from oneself.

Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker, **Devīgītā 4.24**. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sakti-context-26).

> devadattaḥ sa evāyam iti vallakṣaṇā smṛtā /
> sthūlādi deharahito brahma sampadyate naraḥ // 4.24 //

Literal rendering: Indication is understood as in “this is that very Devadatta.” Free from the gross and other bodies, the person attains Brahman.

The joined electronic iti vallakṣaṇā is read as iti-vat lakṣaṇā, as required by the stated example; no change to its quoted letters.

Verse 22 explicitly rejects unity of conflicting direct meanings; 24 uses the Devadatta recognition analogy. The correction of meanings is part of the argument: not every predicate attached to the initial direct meanings survives.

**Devīgītā 7.17, with 7.15b.** Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sm-devi-nonhostility).

> madrūpatvena jīvānāṃ cintanaṃ kurute tu yaḥ / yathā svasyātmani prītistathaiva ca parātmani

Literal rendering: But one who contemplates individual beings as my form: as there is love for one’s own self, so indeed for another’s self.

Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker, **Devīgītā 7.15b**. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sakti-context-62).

> svābhedenaiva māṃ nityaṃ jānāti na vibhedataḥ

Literal rendering: [One] always knows me precisely through nondifference from oneself, not through difference.

Verse 18 prohibits hostile intention because difference is relinquished; 15 says knowing the Goddess as nonother. The devotion described includes a relation to other beings, not only inward absorption. Its ethical content remains alongside the later qualifications concerning knowledge and the path.

**Devīgītā 7.18.** Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sm-devi-nonhostility-exact).

> na kutrāpi drohabuddhiṃ kurute bhedavarjanāt

Literal rendering: One forms no hostile intention anywhere, because of relinquishing difference.

Read with 16–17’s regarding beings as divine forms and sharing love. Nonhostility is an explicit consequence of relinquished difference. The passage makes a substantive ethical claim, rather than merely naming an inward state.

**Devīgītā 7.28.** Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sm-devi-devotion).

> bhaktestu yā parā kāṣṭhā saiva jñānaṃ prakīrtitam / vairāgyasya ca sīmā sā jñāne tadubhayaṃ yataḥ

Literal rendering: But that which is the highest limit of devotion is itself declared knowledge; it is also the limit of dispassion, since both are in knowledge.

Verse 15–18 combine knowing the Goddess as nonother, treating beings as her forms and non-hostility; 29–33 distinguish delayed knowledge and already attained reality. The culmination of devotion in knowledge does not mean that every devotional act is already complete knowledge. This definition also need not coincide with every other author’s distinction between bhakti and jñāna.

**Devīgītā 7.33.** Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sm-devi-already-attained).

> kaṇṭhacāmīkarasamam ajñānāttu tirohitam / jñānādajñānanāśena labdhameva hi labhyate

Literal rendering: Like gold at the neck, it is concealed through ignorance; through knowledge, by the destruction of ignorance, what is already attained is indeed attained.

Devīgītā, Goddess as speaker, **Devīgītā 7.29–31a**. [Source](cite://devi/devi-gita/inspectable-sakti-context-27).

> bhaktau kṛtāyāṃ yasyāpi prārabdhavaśato naga /
> na jāyate mama jñānaṃ maṇidvīpaṃ sa gacchati // 7.29 //
> tatra gatvākhilānbhogānanicchannapi carcchati /
> tadante mama cidrūpajñānaṃ samyagbhavennaga // 7.30 //
> tena muktaḥ sadaiva syājjñānānmuktirna cānyathā /

Literal rendering: Even for one who has practiced devotion, if knowledge of me does not arise because of already-begun karma, O mountain, he goes to Maṇidvīpa. Having gone there, he obtains all enjoyments even without desiring them. At their end, knowledge of my consciousness-form would arise fully, O mountain. Through that he would be forever liberated: liberation is through knowledge and not otherwise.

The source spelling carcchati is retained; the route is conditional on knowledge not yet arising despite practiced devotion.

The preceding conditional route reaches knowledge after enjoyments in Maṇidvīpa; the necklace analogy explains the character of knowledge’s attainment. Knowledge discloses what is already present rather than producing a new Self. Its prior presence does not mean that every finite subject already has effective knowledge of it.

The Cidvallī offers a different emphasis. Its first-person account belongs to Naṭanānanda’s commentary, while the subsequent verse belongs to Puṇyānanda’s Kāmakalāvilāsa. In the commentary on 2, the 1918 print reads duḥkhāsaṃbhinna, without admixture of suffering, where the typed witness has duḥkhasaṃbhinna; that consequential difference must not be erased, although the clause is not used as a premise below.

**Cidvallī on Kāmakalāvilāsa on 1, printed 2–3.** Naṭanānanda commentary. [Source](cite://natanananda/cidvalli/inspectable-sm-cidvalli-first-person).

> icchāmi jānāmi karomi iti uttamapuruṣāntargatasphuraṇarūpāhaṃparāmarśa eva

Literal rendering: Precisely the I-apprehension consisting in manifestation within the first person: “I will, I know, I act.”

The commentary explains the vimarśa contained within root verse 1. The first-person willing, knowing and acting are substantive features of this account. Neither generic dependence on another thing nor grammatical gender captures their force.

**Cidvallī on Kāmakalāvilāsa on 2, printed 4.** Naṭanānanda commentary. [Source](cite://natanananda/cidvalli/inspectable-sm-cidvalli-own-power).

> evaṃ parameśvaro ’pi svādhīnabhūtāṃ svātmaśaktiṃ samyak avalokya svasvarūpam avagacchati

Literal rendering: Thus the supreme Lord too, having fully looked upon his own self-power that is under his own control, apprehends his own nature.

The quoted sentence describes the Lord apprehending his own nature through his own power, under his own control. It does not describe an independently constituted external instrument.

**Kāmakalāvilāsa 3, printed 4.** Puṇyānanda. [Source](cite://punyananda/kamakala-vilasa/inspectable-sm-kamakala-pair).

> sphuṭaśivaśaktisamāgamabījāṅkurarūpiṇī parāśaktiḥ / aṇutararūpānuttaravimarśalipilakṣyavigrahā bhāti // 3 //

Literal rendering: The supreme power, having the form of seed and sprout of the manifest conjunction of Śiva and Śakti, shines, exceedingly subtle in form, her form indicated by the letters of anuttara and reflexive apprehension.

Naṭanānanda’s commentary, **Cidvallī on Kāmakalāvilāsa 3, printed 5**. [Source](cite://natanananda/cidvalli/inspectable-sakti-context-33).

> अत्र
> शिवशब्देन ज्ञानशक्तिरभिधीयते ज्ञानशक्त्यधिष्ठानत्वात् शिवतत्त्वस्य
> शक्तिशब्देनापि क्रियाशक्तिरभिधीयते क्रियाशक्त्यधिष्ठानत्वात् शक्तितत्त्वस्य
> चिदानन्दस्वरूपिण्याः सर्वत्र ज्ञानक्रियाभ्यामेव निर्माणौचित्यदर्शनात्

IAST: atra śivaśabdena jñānaśaktir abhidhīyate jñānaśaktyadhiṣṭhānatvāt śivatattvasya śaktiśabdenāpi kriyāśaktir abhidhīyate kriyāśaktyadhiṣṭhānatvāt śaktitattvasya cidānandasvarūpiṇyāḥ sarvatra jñānakriyābhyām eva nirmāṇaucityadarśanāt

Literal rendering: Here the word Śiva denotes the power of knowledge, because the Śiva-principle is the seat of the power of knowledge. The word Śakti too denotes the power of action, because the Śakti-principle is the seat of the power of action; for, in the case of her whose nature is consciousness and bliss, the fittingness of construction everywhere through knowledge and action alone is seen.

Naṭanānanda’s commentary, **Cidvallī on Kāmakalāvilāsa 3, printed 5**. [Source](cite://natanananda/cidvalli/inspectable-sakti-context-34).

> बीजादङ्कुरं अङ्कुराद्बीजम् इतिवत्
> शिवतत्त्वादिक्षितितत्त्वपर्यन्तं तत्त्वजालम् उत्तरोत्तरम् उद्भावयेत् इत्यर्थः

IAST: bījād aṅkuraṃ aṅkurād bījam itivat śivatattvādikṣititattvaparyantaṃ tattvajālam uttarottaram udbhāvayet ity arthaḥ

Literal rendering: The meaning is: as a sprout from a seed and a seed from a sprout, [she] would bring forth successively the multitude of principles from the Śiva-principle to the earth-principle.

Naṭanānanda’s commentary, **Cidvallī on Kāmakalāvilāsa 3, printed 5**. [Source](cite://natanananda/cidvalli/inspectable-sakti-context-35).

> अणुतरम् अत्यन्तसूक्ष्मरूपम् अस्या इति अनुत्तरलिपिः अकारः विमर्शलिपिः हकारः
> ताभ्यां लक्ष्यं विग्रहं स्वरूपं यस्याः तथोक्ता

IAST: aṇutaram atyantasūkṣmarūpam asyā iti anuttaralipiḥ akāraḥ vimarśalipiḥ hakāraḥ tābhyāṃ lakṣyaṃ vigrahaṃ svarūpaṃ yasyāḥ tathoktā

Literal rendering: Her form is exceedingly subtle: thus [she is] aṇutara. The letter of anuttara is a; the letter of reflexive apprehension is ha. She whose body—intrinsic form—is indicated by those two is so described.

The commentary on 3 identifies Śiva with knowledge-power, Śakti with action-power and explains mutual seed/sprout development. The commentary separately parses aṇutara as exceedingly subtle in her form, and identifies the anuttara-letter as a and the vimarśa-letter as ha; the adjective does not qualify the letters. Functional differentiation and intrinsic unity are both expressed. The verse neither establishes two independent beings nor erases the distinct operations explained through the pair.

### Ramaṇa: devotion and self-abidance

Ramaṇa’s sequence begins with offered action and service of the world as divine, proceeds through continuous and nondivisive contemplation, and calls abiding in true being highest devotion. Temporary subsidence differs from the end of what causes renewed mistaken self-ascription. The later verses distinguish I-thought from self-manifestation and explain self-vision as abidance. Worship of all as the divine form can then be read with undivided love without making every devotional address a second independent substance.

**Upadeśasāram 3.** Ramaṇa. [Source](cite://ramana/upadesa-saram/inspectable-sm-ramana-offering).

> īśvarārpitaṃ necchayā kṛtam / citta-śodhakaṃ mukti-sādhakam

Literal rendering: Done as an offering to the Lord, not from desire, [action is] purifying of mind and a means to liberation.

Purification and fulfillment perform different roles. The verse does not describe every action, regardless of intention, as intrinsically liberating.

**Upadeśasāram 5.** Ramaṇa. [Source](cite://ramana/upadesa-saram/inspectable-sm-ramana-world-service).

> jagata īśadhī-yukta-sevanam / aṣṭa-mūrti-bhṛd-deva-pūjanam

Literal rendering: Service of the world joined with the understanding [that it is] the Lord is worship of the God bearing eight forms.

 It is the specified understanding that qualifies this service as worship; activity alone is not the whole condition.

**Upadeśasāram 7.** Ramaṇa. [Source](cite://ramana/upadesa-saram/inspectable-sm-ramana-continuity).

> ājya-dhārayā srotasā samam / sarala-cintanaṃ viralataḥ param

Literal rendering: Unbroken contemplation like a stream of ghee or a current is superior to intermittent [contemplation].

The comparison distinguishes continuous from intermittent contemplation.

**Upadeśasāram 8.** Ramaṇa. [Source](cite://ramana/upadesa-saram/inspectable-sm-ramana-nondivisive-contemplation).

> bheda-bhāvanāt so'ham ity asau / bhāvanābhidā pāvanī matā

Literal rendering: Compared with contemplation of difference, that contemplation “I am he,” without division, is considered purifying.

The joined form bhāvanābhidā is read as bhāvanā abhidā, contemplation without division, matching the contrast with bhedabhāvanā and the Tamil counterpart’s aṉaṉiya-bhāvam. The change concerns how one understands oneself in contemplation.

**Upadeśasāram 9–10.** Ramaṇa. [Source](cite://ramana/upadesa-saram/inspectable-sm-ramana-highest-bhakti).

> bhāva-śūnya-sad-bhāva-susthitiḥ / bhāvanā-balād bhaktir uttamā

Literal rendering: Firm abiding in true being, empty of thought, through the strength of contemplation, is highest devotion.

Ramaṇa, **Upadeśasāram 10**. [Source](cite://ramana/upadesa-saram/inspectable-sakti-context-28).

> hṛt-sthale manaḥ svasthatā kriyā / bhakti-yoga-bodhāś ca niścitam

Literal rendering: The mind’s being in its own place in the heart is action, devotion, yoga and knowledge: this is certain.

Verse 8 prefers nondivisive “I am he” contemplation; 10 unifies action/devotion/yoga/knowledge in mind’s abiding in its source. The endpoint is abiding in true being, not unconsciousness. This definition of highest devotion should not be presumed to govern every occurrence of bhakti in other authors.

**Upadeśasāram 13–14.** Ramaṇa. [Source](cite://ramana/upadesa-saram/inspectable-sm-ramana-laya).

> laya-vinaśane ubhaya-rodhane / laya-gataṃ punar bhavati no mṛtam

Literal rendering: In the two restraints, subsidence and destruction: what has subsided arises again; what has died does not.

Ramaṇa, **Upadeśasāram 14**. [Source](cite://ramana/upadesa-saram/inspectable-sakti-context-29).

> prāṇa-bandhanāl līna-mānasam / eka-cintanān nāśam ety adaḥ

Literal rendering: This mind, subsided through restraint of breath, reaches destruction through contemplation of one [thing].

Verse 14 adds contemplation of one to the subsidence brought about by breath-restraint. Temporary quiet differs from removal of what allows the mind to arise again. The figurative death of mind does not assert physical death or the deletion of awareness.

**Upadeśasāram 18–20.** Ramaṇa. [Source](cite://ramana/upadesa-saram/inspectable-sm-ramana-i-thought).

> vṛttayas tv ahaṃ-vṛttim āśritāḥ / vṛttayo mano viddhy ahaṃ manaḥ

Literal rendering: But the modifications depend upon the I-modification. The modifications are mind; know the “I” [thought] as mind.

Ramaṇa, **Upadeśasāram 19**. [Source](cite://ramana/upadesa-saram/inspectable-sakti-context-30).

> aham ayaṃ kuto bhavati cinvataḥ / ayi pataty ahaṃ nija-vicāraṇam

Literal rendering: For one investigating “From where does this I arise?”, ah, the I falls. This is inquiry into oneself.

Ramaṇa, **Upadeśasāram 20**. [Source](cite://ramana/upadesa-saram/inspectable-sakti-context-31).

> ahami nāśa-bhāji aham-ahaṃtayā / sphurati hṛt svayaṃ parama-pūrṇa-sat

Literal rendering: When the I undergoes destruction, the heart shines of itself as I–I: supreme, full being.

Verse 19 asks its source; 20 contrasts its fall with the self-shining fullness of I-I. The I-thought and intrinsic self-manifestation are distinguished. The mere use of a first-person expression does not establish an independently substantial ego.

**Upadeśasāram 24–25.** Ramaṇa. [Source](cite://ramana/upadesa-saram/inspectable-sm-ramana-self-god).

> īśa-jīvayor veṣa-dhī-bhidā / sat-svabhāvato vastu kevalam

Literal rendering: Between Lord and individual there is difference of guise and cognition; by nature as being, reality is single.

Ramaṇa, **Upadeśasāram 25**. [Source](cite://ramana/upadesa-saram/inspectable-sakti-context-32).

> veṣa-hānataḥ svātma-darśanam / īśa-darśanaṃ svātma-rūpataḥ

Literal rendering: Through removal of the guise, seeing one’s own Self is seeing the Lord, because [the Lord] has the form of one’s own Self.

Verse 25 identifies seeing Self with seeing Lord when guise is removed; 26 understands vision as abidance. The assertion that reality is single does not transfer every initial predicate of Lord and individual indiscriminately. The contrast between guise/cognition and nature as being is part of the claim itself.

**Upadeśasāram 26.** Ramaṇa. [Source](cite://ramana/upadesa-saram/inspectable-sm-ramana-vision-abidance).

> ātma-saṃsthitiḥ svātma-darśanam / ātma-nirdvayād ātma-niṣṭhatā

Literal rendering: Abiding in Self is seeing one’s own Self; because Self is nondual, [it is] steadfastness in Self.

25’s self/Lord vision is interpreted through 26. Self-vision is explained as abidance, rather than inspection of a separate object.

**Aruṇācalapañcaratnam 5; official Monday PDF 55/printed 63 Tamil counterpart.** Ramaṇa. [Source](cite://ramana/arunachala-pancharatnam/inspectable-sm-ramana-all-form-love).

> tvayy arpita-manasā tvāṃ paśyan sarvaṃ tavākṛtitayā satatam / bhajate ’nanya-prītyā sa jayaty aruṇācala tvayi sukhe magnaḥ //

Literal rendering: O Aruṇācala, seeing you with a mind surrendered in you, he who continually worships everything as your form with undivided love triumphs, immersed in you, in happiness.

ananyaprīti allows undivided/nonother love; Tamil aṉṉiyamil aṉbu makes nonotherness explicit. Second-person address and undivided love can coexist with recognition of nonotherness.

### Ramakrishna: divine manifestation and retained love

Ramakrishna’s analogies approach related questions from different directions. Gem and radiance give reciprocal conceptual inseparability; still water and waves distinguish activity from another underlying reality; the Captain remains the same bearer under different activities. His recorded accounts of avatāra add infinite divine manifestation in a human frame, while explicitly distinguishing the avatāra from an ordinary practitioner. Later teachings permit devotion after knowledge for instruction and enjoyment, distinguish a harmless devotional I from the binding ego, and qualify return.

**Śrīśrīrāmakṛṣṇakathāmṛta 19 August 1883, chapter 16 section 15, pp 253–254.** Ramakrishna, as recorded by M. [Source](cite://ramakrishna/kathamrita/inspectable-sm-ramakrishna-gem).

> maṇi nā bhāble maṇir jyotiḥ bhābte pārā yāẏ nā — maṇir jyotiḥ nā bhāble maṇi bhābte pārā yāẏ nā.

Literal rendering: Without thinking of the gem one cannot think of its radiance; without thinking of the gem’s radiance one cannot think of the gem.

Reciprocal conceptual implication does not by itself define a relation of ontological grounding. The analogy should not be rewritten as a theory of two independently existing substances.

**Śrīśrīrāmakṛṣṇakathāmṛta 19 August 1883, pp 253–254.** Ramakrishna, as recorded by M. [Source](cite://ramakrishna/kathamrita/inspectable-sm-ramakrishna-water).

> kintu jal sthir thākleo jal, taraṅga, bhuṛbhuṛi haleo jal.

Literal rendering: But water is water even when still; even when there are waves and bubbles, it is water.

Still water, waves and bubbles distinguish abiding reality from activity.

**Śrīśrīrāmakṛṣṇakathāmṛta 19 August 1883, pp 253–254.** Ramakrishna, as recorded by M. [Source](cite://ramakrishna/kathamrita/inspectable-sm-ramakrishna-captain).

> yeman kāpten yakhan kon kāj karen nā takhano yini, ār kāpten pūjā karchen, takhano tini; ār kāpten lāṭ sāheber kāche yācchen, takhano tini; kebal upādhi biśeṣ.

Literal rendering: For example, the Captain when he does no work is the same one; when the Captain is worshipping, he is still that one; and when the Captain goes to the Governor, he is still that one—only a particular designation/condition.

The demonstrative pronouns preserve the bearer while upādhi marks the differentiated presentation. The example does not itself specify a law governing the changes.

**Śrīśrīrāmakṛṣṇakathāmṛta, 13 June 1885, chapter 48 §3, pp 846–848.** Ramakrishna’s speech as recorded by M. (Mahendranath Gupta). [Source](cite://ramakrishna/kathamrita/inspectable-df-rk-infinite-form).

> Pūrṇa avatār bojhāno baṛa śakta, ki bala? Caudda poyār bhitar ananta āsā!

Literal rendering: It is very difficult to explain a full avatāra, what do you say? The infinite coming within fourteen poas!

The expression “fourteen poas,” a traditional weight idiom evoking the human bodily frame, presents the explanatory difficulty rather than measuring infinity. This remark does not identify every saint as a full avatāra.

**Śrīśrīrāmakṛṣṇakathāmṛta, 12 April 1885, chapter 44 §3, pp 799–800.** Ramakrishna’s speech as recorded by M. (Mahendranath Gupta). [Source](cite://ramakrishna/kathamrita/inspectable-df-rk-practice-distinction).

> Avatār bā avatārer aṃśa, eder bale īśvarakoṭi ār sādhāraṇ lokder bale jīv bā jīvakoṭi. Yārā jīvakoṭi tārā sādhanā kare īśvaralābh karte pāre; tārā samādhistha haye ār phere nā.

Literal rendering: An avatāra or a part of an avatāra—these are called īśvarakoṭi; ordinary people are called jīva or jīvakoṭi. Those who are jīvakoṭi can attain God through practice; having entered samādhi they do not return.

The question is whether everyone can become like Śrī Kṛṣṇa. Ramakrishna answers with two classes; the quoted jīvakoṭi account states nonreturn after samādhi. The thin-I passage below directly describes return for avatāras and the like. He explicitly allows God-attainment through practice. God-attainment does not erase every distinction of spiritual biography.

**Śrīśrīrāmakṛṣṇakathāmṛta, 12 April 1885, chapter 44 §7, pp 809–811.** Ramakrishna’s speech as recorded by M. (Mahendranath Gupta). [Source](cite://ramakrishna/kathamrita/inspectable-df-rk-contact-nonexhaustion).

> Tomāke chũle ki tomār sab śarīrṭā chũte habe? Yadi gaṅgāsnān kari tā hale haridvār theke gaṅgāsāgar paryanta ki chũye yete habe?
> […]
> ‘Āmi’ gele ki raila tā keu jānte pāre nā, — mukhe balte pāre nā. Yā āche tāi āche! Takhan khānikṭā ẽte prakāś hayeche ār bādabākiṭā okhāne prakāś hayeche, — e-sab mukhe balā yāy nā.

Literal rendering: If I touch you, must I touch your whole body? If I bathe in the Ganges, must I then go on touching from Haridwar to Gangasagar? […] When the I goes, no one can know what remains—cannot say it in words. What is, is! Then, “a portion has manifested in this one and the remainder has manifested over there”—such things cannot be said in words.

Trailokya denies manifestation of infinite power in a human being; Girish argues for worship of the great person. Ramakrishna’s contact analogy challenges the requirement of exhaustive encounter. His subsequent reply also denies the language of distributing a portion here and a remainder there after the I has gone. Neither step proves that every finite part has all properties of the whole, or that every saint manifests unlimited power. The ellipsis omits the intervening maxim and statement that difference-consciousness lasts while the I remains.

**Śrīśrīrāmakṛṣṇakathāmṛta, 12 April 1885, chapter 44 §7, pp 809–811.** Ramakrishna’s speech as recorded by M. (Mahendranath Gupta). [Source](cite://ramakrishna/kathamrita/inspectable-df-rk-thin-i).

> Avatārādir ‘āmi’ pātlā ‘āmi’. E ‘āmi’r bhitar diye īśvarke sarvadā dekhā yāy.
> […]
> Er māne, dehadhāraṇ karleo tārā sarvadā yogetei thāke!
> […]
> Samādhistha haleo ābār neme āste pāre.

Literal rendering: The I of avatāras and the like is a thin I. Through this I, God can always be seen. […] This means that even though they assume a body, they always remain in yoga! […] Even after entering samādhi, they can come down again.

The thin-I description explicitly permits embodied yoga and return from samādhi. The passage concerns avatāras and the like, identified nearby as īśvarakoṭi; it does not assign equal transparency to every personality or every liberated person.

The following conversations make the purposes and conditions of retained devotion explicit.

**Śrīśrīrāmakṛṣṇakathāmṛta 11 March 1886, chapter 53 section 6, pp 1019–1021.** Ramakrishna, as recorded by M. [Source](cite://ramakrishna/kathamrita/inspectable-sm-ramakrishna-after-knowledge).

> jñānī jñānalābh karbār paro vidyāmāẏā niẏe thākte pāre — bhakti, daẏā, bairāgya — ei sab niẏe thākte pāre. er duṭi uddeśya. pratham, lokśikṣā haẏ, tārpar rasāsvādaner janya.

Literal rendering: Even after attaining knowledge the knower may remain with vidyāmāyā—with devotion, compassion, dispassion, all these. There are two purposes: first, teaching people; then, for tasting rasa.

The modal pāre matters: possibility is not a universal compulsory return. Knowledge, retained devotional dispositions and their purposes are distinguished.

**Śrīśrīrāmakṛṣṇakathāmṛta 11 March 1886, pp 1019–1021.** Ramakrishna, as recorded by M. [Source](cite://ramakrishna/kathamrita/inspectable-sm-ramakrishna-burnt-rope).

> poṛā daṛi dekhtei daṛir ākār, phũ dile uṛe yāẏ. jñānāgnite ahaṅkār puṛe geche. ekhan ār kārao aniṣṭa kare nā. nāmamātra “āmi”.

Literal rendering: A burnt rope has a rope’s shape to the eye; blow and it flies away. The ego has been burnt in knowledge’s fire. Now it does no harm to anyone. An “I” in name only.

The surrounding passage distinguishes vidyā-I/devotee-I from the harmful ego. A relational I need not entail the harmful appropriation associated with a binding ego. The analogy of shape without the former causal force does not establish eternal substantial individuality.

Ramakrishna as recorded by M., **Kathāmṛta, 1March 1885, chapter 42§1, pp 748–749**. [Source](cite://ramakrishna/kathamrita/inspectable-sakti-context-56).

> জীবকোটি ও ঈশ্বরকোটি। জীবকোটির ভক্তি, বৈধভক্তি।

Romanized Bengali: Jīvakoṭi o īśvarakoṭi. Jīvakoṭir bhakti, baidhabhakti.

Literal rendering: Jīvakoṭi and īśvarakoṭi. The devotion of the jīvakoṭi is prescribed devotion.

**Śrīśrīrāmakṛṣṇakathāmṛta 1 March 1885, pp 748–749.** Ramakrishna, as recorded by M. [Source](cite://ramakrishna/kathamrita/inspectable-sm-ramakrishna-no-return).

> ei baidhībhaktir par jñān. tārpar laẏ. ei laẏer par ār phere nā.

Literal rendering: After this prescribed devotion, knowledge; then dissolution. After this dissolution one does not return.

This is expressly the jīvakoti account; the following īśvarakoti account permits ascent/descent. The nonreturn statement concerns the class specified here. It cannot be transferred without qualification to the contrasted īśvarakoṭi account.

Ramakrishna as recorded by M., **Kathāmṛta, 1March 1885, chapter 42§1, pp 748–749**. [Source](cite://ramakrishna/kathamrita/inspectable-sakti-context-57).

> ঈশ্বরকোটির আলাদা কথা, — যেমন অনুলোম বোলুম। ‘নেতি’ ‘নেতি’ করে ছাদে পৌঁছে যখন দেখে, ছাদও যে জিনিসে তৈরী, — ইঁট, চুন, সুরকি, সিঁড়িও সেই জিনিসে তৈরী। তখন কখন ছাদেও থাকতে পারে, আবার উঠা নামাও করতে পারে।

Romanized Bengali: Īśvarakoṭir ālādā kathā, — yeman anulom bolum. “Neti” “neti” kare chāde pãuche yakhan dekhe, chādao ye jinise tairī, — ĩṭ, cun, surki, sĩṛio sei jinise tairī. Takhan kakhana chādeo thākte pāre, ābār uṭhā nāmāo karte pāre.

Literal rendering: The īśvarakoṭi is a different matter—like going in order and in reverse. Having reached the roof by “not this, not this,” when one sees that the stairs too are made of the same material as the roof—brick, lime and brick-dust—then one may sometimes remain on the roof, and may also go up and down.

The Bengali retains the colloquial anuloṃ boloṃ wording অনুলোম বোলুম.

**Śrīśrīrāmakṛṣṇakathāmṛta 1 March 1885, chapter 42 section 1, pp 748–749.** Ramakrishna, as recorded by M. [Source](cite://ramakrishna/kathamrita/inspectable-sm-ramakrishna-return-guard).

> yatkṣaṇ kumbha āche, āmi tumi āche; tumi ṭhākur, āmi bhakta; tumi prabhu, āmi dās; eo āche.

Literal rendering: As long as the pot exists, I and you exist: you are the Lord, I the devotee; you are the master, I the servant—this too exists.

The class distinction governs the contrast between jīvakoti nonreturn and īśvarakoti ascent/descent. The relational condition is expressly limited by the pot’s continuance; the pot is not declared eternal. Neither relational return nor permanent disappearance is imposed universally.

### Vivekananda: fulfilled worship and transformed relations

Vivekananda rejects two independent Gods while retaining particular devotional relations and their culmination. Fulfilled purpose can coexist with continued worship. The wheels and mirage explain continued appearance after false identification is corrected; the ethical test rules out using realization-language to license harmful conduct. His account of love after realization is another positive claim, rather than merely permission to continue a preparatory practice.

**Bhakti-Yoga, The Philosophy of Ishvara opening discussion.** Vivekananda. [Source](cite://vivekananda/complete-works/inspectable-sm-vivekananda-one-reality).

> No, it is the same Sat-chit-ananda who is also the God of Love, the impersonal and personal in one.

Vivekananda, **Bhakti-Yoga, The Philosophy of Ishvara, before the introduced Rāmānuja quotation**. [Source](cite://vivekananda/complete-works/inspectable-sakti-context-42).

> In the fourth Pâda of the fourth chapter of his Sutras, after stating the almost infinite power and knowledge which will come to the liberated soul after the attainment of Moksha, Vyâsa makes the remark, in an aphorism, that none, however, will get the power of creating, ruling, and dissolving the universe, because that belongs to God alone.

This is Vivekananda’s introductory report of the Brahmasūtra restriction; the translated commentary follows in his text.

His introductory account expressly reserves universal governance. A common basis does not license unrestricted transfer of manifested roles. His ensuing translation presents Rāmānuja’s argument.

**Bhakti-Yoga, Para-Bhakti IV forms of love; tadīyatā paragraph.** Vivekananda. [Source](cite://vivekananda/complete-works/inspectable-sm-vivekananda-fulfilled-worship).

> All his purpose in life then becomes fulfilled. Yet many such Bhaktas live on just to worship Him.

“Many” restricts the claim; the surrounding text concerns perfection and attainment of God, not merely preparatory ritual. Fulfilled purpose can therefore coexist with continued worship, without making this the compulsory biography of every liberated knower.

**Bhakti-Yoga, Para-Bhakti X closing paragraph.** Vivekananda. [Source](cite://vivekananda/complete-works/inspectable-sm-vivekananda-love-unity).

> Love, the Lover, and the Beloved are One.

The culmination transforms the relational starting point. Relational address does not prove independent substances, but neither does every earlier devotional stage already possess the final understanding.

**Jñāna-Yoga, The Real and the Apparent Man two wheels and mirage paragraphs.** Vivekananda. [Source](cite://vivekananda/complete-works/inspectable-sm-vivekananda-continuation).

> But upon the other wheel, that of the body and mind, will be the momentum of past acts

Vivekananda, **The Real and the Apparent Man, two wheels paragraph**. [Source](cite://vivekananda/complete-works/inspectable-sakti-context-43).

> Knowledge is the axe which will sever the bond between the two, and the wheel of the soul will stop — stop thinking that it is coming and going, living and dying, stop thinking that it is nature and has wants and desires, and will find that it is perfect, desireless.

Vivekananda, **The Real and the Apparent Man, mirage paragraph**. [Source](cite://vivekananda/complete-works/inspectable-sakti-context-44).

> There was again the lake, but with it came also the idea that it was the mirage and not a true lake.

Vivekananda, **The Real and the Apparent Man, application of the mirage**. [Source](cite://vivekananda/complete-works/inspectable-sakti-context-45).

> Under the influence of the new knowledge the strength of Karma will be broken, its poison will be lost. It becomes transformed, for along with it there comes the idea that we know it now, that the sharp distinction between the reality and the mirage has been known.

Knowledge cuts the connection of false identification; subsequent mirage appears with recognition of its status. The correction of false identification can persist while bodily and mental appearances continue.

**The Real and the Apparent Man ethical test following mirage.** Vivekananda. [Source](cite://vivekananda/complete-works/inspectable-sm-vivekananda-ethics).

> Even if he lives in the body and works incessantly, he works only to do good

The preceding challenge is to those claiming to transcend virtue/vice while doing harm. His account of fulfillment includes this ethical test.

Vivekananda, **The Real and the Apparent Man, question preceding love after realization**. [Source](cite://vivekananda/complete-works/inspectable-sakti-context-46).

> The next question is to know what comes after realisation. Suppose we have realised this oneness of the universe, that we are that one Infinite Being, and suppose we have realised that this Self is the only Existence and that it is the same Self which is manifesting in all these various phenomenal forms, what becomes of us after that?

**The Real and the Apparent Man, paragraph beginning “The next question is to know what comes after realisation”.** Vivekananda. [Source](cite://vivekananda/complete-works/inspectable-sm-vivekananda-love-after-realization).

> The wife will love the husband the more when she thinks that the husband is God Himself. The husband will love the wife the more when he knows that the wife is God Himself.

Vivekananda presents transformed love as a consequence of realized oneness. This goes beyond permission to continue a preliminary practice: his account connects recognition of the divine in another person with intensified affection.

### Aurobindo and Medhananda: intrinsic power and dependence

Aurobindo’s account includes intrinsic force, no independent outside, freedom from exhaustion by determinations, and the validity of both silent and dynamic realization. His demand for selective formative knowledge then asks more than whether all phenomena share an underlying reality. Medhananda’s interpretation likewise begins with aspects of one reality. The further question concerns his inference from inseparability and parity to mutual ontological dependence.

**The Life Divine I.X, Conscious Force printed 90.** Sri Aurobindo. [Source](cite://aurobindo/life-divine/inspectable-sm-aurobindo-force-rest).

> Force is inherent in Existence. Shiva and Kali, Brahman and Shakti are one and not two who are separable.

Sri Aurobindo, **The Life Divine I.X, printed 90**. [Source](cite://aurobindo/life-divine/inspectable-sakti-context-47).

> For it is impossible, because contradictory of reason, to suppose that Force is a thing alien to the one and inﬁnite existence and entered into it from outside or was non-existent and arose in it at some point in Time.

The following quoted sentence specifies what rest does not alter; the reason above excludes an alien outside source. Intrinsic power must be distinguished from its exercise and from changing appearances. Aurobindo’s descriptions of other schools are not independent verification of those schools’ positions.

**The Life Divine I.X printed 90.** Sri Aurobindo. [Source](cite://aurobindo/life-divine/inspectable-sm-aurobindo-power-persistence).

> when it is at rest, it exists none the less and is not abolished, diminished or in any way essentially altered.

The antecedent is Force inherent in existence; preceding sentence says Śiva/Kālī and Brahman/Śakti are not separable. Power persists while its exercise varies. Rest does not therefore amount to the destruction or essential alteration of power.

**The Life Divine II.III, Brahman, Purusha, Ishwara printed 347–348.** Sri Aurobindo. [Source](cite://aurobindo/life-divine/inspectable-sm-aurobindo-nonbinding).

> free from all restraining effect of its own creations

Sri Aurobindo, **The Life Divine II.III, printed 347**. [Source](cite://aurobindo/life-divine/inspectable-sakti-context-48).

> But the contradiction disappears when we understand that the indeterminability is not in its true sense negative, not an imposition of incapacity on the Inﬁnite, but positive, a freedom within itself from limitation by its own determinations and necessarily a freedom from all external determination by anything not itself, since there is no real possibility of such a not-self coming into existence.

Sri Aurobindo, **The Life Divine II.III, printed 348**. [Source](cite://aurobindo/life-divine/inspectable-sakti-context-49).

> In fact the Inﬁnite does not create, it manifests what is in itself, in its own essence of reality; it is itself that essence of all reality and all realities are powers of that one Reality. The Absolute neither creates nor is created, — in the current sense of making or being made; we can speak of creation only in the sense of the Being becoming in form and movement what it already is in substance and status.

Pages 347–348 exclude an external not-self and explain determination as manifestation of intrinsic reality. Nonbinding manifestation is an additional premise of Aurobindo’s account, not a conclusion supplied by self-presence alone or automatically shared by inactive-self theories.

**The Life Divine II.III printed 351; 361–362.** Sri Aurobindo. [Source](cite://aurobindo/life-divine/inspectable-sm-aurobindo-silence-dynamis).

> it is an essential realisation, but not the total realisation. For we can see that the Conscious-Power, the Shakti that acts and creates, is not other than the Maya or all-knowledge of Brahman; it is the Power of the Self;

Sri Aurobindo, **The Life Divine II.III, printed 351**. [Source](cite://aurobindo/life-divine/inspectable-sakti-context-50).

> The opposition we make is mental and conceptual; in reality, the silence of the Spirit and the dynamis of the Spirit are complementary truths and inseparable. The immutable silent Spirit may hold its inﬁnite energy silent and immobile within it, for it is not bound by its own forces, is not their subject or instrument, but it does possess them, does release them, is capable of an eternal and inﬁnite action, does not weary or need to stop, and yet all the time its silent immobility inherent in its action and movement is not for a moment shaken or disturbed or altered by its action and movement;

The passage on page 351 argues that silence and dynamis are complementary. A more comprehensive realization must preserve the achieved freedom whose validity it acknowledges. Greater experiential completeness is a further philosophical claim.

**The Life Divine II.III printed 362.** Sri Aurobindo. [Source](cite://aurobindo/life-divine/inspectable-sm-aurobindo-free-identity).

> constituting everything and identiﬁed in a free identity with all its becomings in Nature. Even so, freedom and impersonality are always the character of the Self.

 Identity with becoming is described as free, while freedom and impersonality remain. This claim of intrinsic constitution does not itself settle Śaṅkara’s distinction between ultimate and provisional status.

**The Life Divine I.XIII, The Divine Maya printed 122–123.** Sri Aurobindo. [Source](cite://aurobindo/life-divine/inspectable-sm-aurobindo-law).

> We perceive a process, we are aware of a Law.

Sri Aurobindo, **The Life Divine I.XIII, printed 122**. [Source](cite://aurobindo/life-divine/inspectable-sakti-context-51).

> Still, when we have found that all things are Sachchidananda, all has not yet been explained. We know the Reality of the universe, we do not yet know the process by which that Reality has turned itself into this phenomenon.

Sri Aurobindo, **The Life Divine I.XIII, printed 123**. [Source](cite://aurobindo/life-divine/inspectable-sakti-context-52).

> And since consciousness is the nature of the original Existence and the essence of its Force, this truth must be a self-perception in Conscious-Being and this determination of the line taken by Force must result from a power of selfdirective knowledge inherent in Consciousness which enables it to guide its own Force inevitably along the logical line of the original self-perception. It is then a self-determining power in universal consciousness, a capacity in self-awareness of inﬁnite existence to perceive a certain Truth in itself and direct its force of creation along the line of that Truth, which has presided over the cosmic manifestation.

Page 122 distinguishes reality from process; 123 argues for selective self-directive knowledge shaping finite forms. A common underlying reality does not yet explain the determinate process. Aurobindo demands an account of selection and order, and proposes knowledge-power to supply it.

**The Life Divine I.XIII printed 123.** Sri Aurobindo. [Source](cite://aurobindo/life-divine/inspectable-sm-aurobindo-law-selection).

> a selective faculty of knowledge commissioned to shape ﬁnite appearance out of the inﬁnite Reality.

Sri Aurobindo, **The Life Divine I.XIII, printed 123**. [Source](cite://aurobindo/life-divine/inspectable-sakti-context-53).

> But when we say, “God said, Let there be Light”, we assume the act of a power of consciousness which determines light out of everything else that is not light; and when we say “and there was Light” we presume a directing faculty, an active power corresponding to the original perceptive power, which brings out the phenomenon and, working out Light according to the line of the original perception, prevents it from being overpowered by all the inﬁnite possibilities that are other than itself.

The paragraph argues that choosing light rather than other possibilities already presupposes determination and directing power. Infinite power does not here mean unrestricted arbitrary change. The argument asks how particular possibilities are selected and shaped.

### Aurobindo: the aim of integration and the individual who realizes it

Aurobindo also distinguishes a practice’s own end from the further task of integration. In *The Synthesis of Yoga*, he says that if the aim is escape from the world to God, “synthesis is unnecessary and a waste of time.” A suitable effective path can suffice. Synthesis becomes necessary for his further aim of transforming the whole being. His proposed practice brings the entire conscious being into relation with the Divine and progressively surrenders the ego’s instruments; its movement responds to individual temperament, capacities and obstacles. This is a positive practical proposal, not merely the verdict that narrower practices fail at their own ends. ([The Synthesis of Yoga, Introduction V, pp. 44–46](cite://aurobindo/synthesis-of-yoga/reader-synthesis-aim)).

The same work distinguishes persistent spiritual individuality from changing mental, vital and bodily personality. Aurobindo identifies a positive truth here in Sāṅkhya’s many subjects and qualified monism, and also defends the devotee’s continuing relation to the Divine. Yet essential identity does not confer unrestricted cosmic government: “no one can become individually the Lord of all the universes.” The individual participates through its own centre; dependence upon its highest Self can coexist with freedom. ([The Synthesis of Yoga, II.11, pp. 376–381](cite://aurobindo/synthesis-of-yoga/reader-individuality)).

In *Essays on the Gita* he takes seriously the associated plurality problem: why do subjects have different experiential outlooks, and why does one person’s liberation not end everyone’s experience? His interpretation of the Gītā introduces further principles to answer the problem rather than treating it as already solved by one-Self language. ([Essays on the Gita, First Series VIII, pp. 74–80](cite://aurobindo/essays-on-the-gita/reader-plurality)). These are Aurobindo’s constructive comparisons. They do not independently establish what every earlier school intended, but they give an integrative theory concrete explanatory work to do.

### Medhananda: inseparability and dependence

Medhananda’s modern interpretation makes a related but distinguishable claim. The book was published under the name Ayon Maharaj; its formulations should be compared with Ramakrishna’s recorded wording rather than substituted for it.

**Infinite Paths to Infinite Reality 2018, printed 37–38.** Maharaj’s modern scholarly interpretation. [Source](cite://medhananda/infinite-paths-to-infinite-reality/inspectable-sm-medhananda-actual-thesis).

> equally real aspects of one and the same Divine Reality

Ayon Maharaj / Swami Medhananda, scholarly interpretation, **Infinite Paths to Infinite Reality, printed 37**. [Source](cite://medhananda/infinite-paths-to-infinite-reality/inspectable-sakti-context-54).

> Tellingly, all of the analogies Sri Ramakrishna employs to illustrate the inseparability of Brahman and Śakti also indicate their ontological parity.

Page 38 then calls their relation mutual ontological dependence; conceptual inseparability is part of his support. Medhananda’s position must therefore be compared with accounts of intrinsic, coessential power, not refuted as an assertion of two independent substances. His wording is modern interpretation rather than Ramakrishna’s Bengali speech.

**Infinite Paths to Infinite Reality 2018, printed 38.** Maharaj’s modern scholarly interpretation. [Source](cite://medhananda/infinite-paths-to-infinite-reality/inspectable-sm-medhananda-dependence).

> mutual ontological dependence of Brahman and Śakti

Ayon Maharaj / Swami Medhananda, scholarly interpretation, **Infinite Paths to Infinite Reality, printed 38**. [Source](cite://medhananda/infinite-paths-to-infinite-reality/inspectable-sakti-context-55).

> Like fire and its power to burn, Brahman and Śakti mutually entail each other. The bidirectionality of all these analogies clearly rules out an Advaitic interpretation of Sri Ramakrishna’s doctrine of the inseparability of Brahman and Śakti. According to Advaita, Śakti is ontologically dependent on nirguṇa Brahman, but nirguṇa Brahman is not ontologically dependent on Śakti, since Śakti is ultimately unreal. Sri Ramakrishna, by contrast, teaches the mutual ontological dependence of Brahman and Śakti.

The excerpt stops before the ensuing translated Ramakrishna quotation; the superscript footnote 54 is a source marker, not part of the argument.

The surrounding paragraph infers parity/dependence from bidirectional analogies; it also characterizes Advaita, which must be checked separately.
